Unit 03: Work, Energy, and Power

AP Physics 1 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CHAPTER BLUEPRINT & CONCEPTS

Sub-Chapter 3.1 — Work Done by a Force

The concept of work in physics is far more precise than its everyday usage. In mechanics, work is done on an object only when a force has a component along the direction of the object's displacement. Pushing against a wall with all your strength does zero work if the wall does not move. This precision is not pedantic — it is essential because work is the mechanism by which forces transfer energy into or out of an object.

Mathematically, work is the dot product of force and displacement: W = F · d = Fd cosθ, where θ is the angle between the force vector and the displacement vector. The dot product extracts only the component of force parallel to the motion. Forces perpendicular to the motion (like the normal force on a horizontally sliding block) do zero work. Forces antiparallel to motion (like kinetic friction) do negative work — they remove energy from the object.

Variable force — the integral definition: When force varies with position (as with a spring), the work must be computed as an integral: W = ∫F(x) dx. Graphically, this is the area under the Force-versus-position graph. For a spring with spring constant k compressed or stretched by distance x from equilibrium: W_spring = ½kx². This result will reappear as elastic potential energy in the next section.

Net work: The net work on an object is the algebraic sum of the work done by every individual force. This equals the work done by the net force: W_net = (ΣF) · d = (ΣF)d cosθ.

WORK (constant force):
  W = F · d · cosθ         [Joules, J = N·m]
  θ = angle between force vector and displacement vector

  θ = 0°:   W = Fd   (force parallel to motion → positive work)
  θ = 90°:  W = 0    (force perpendicular to motion → zero work)
  θ = 180°: W = −Fd  (force opposing motion → negative work)

WORK (variable force — spring, stretched by x):
  W_spring = ∫₀ˣ kx' dx' = ½kx²    [J]
  (= area under F vs. x graph)

Sub-Chapter 3.2 — Kinetic Energy and the Work-Energy Theorem

Kinetic energy (KE) is the energy an object possesses by virtue of its motion. It is a scalar defined as KE = ½mv². Because it involves v², kinetic energy is always non-negative, and doubling the speed quadruples the kinetic energy — a crucial insight for understanding why high-speed collisions and car crashes are so much more destructive than low-speed ones.

The Work-Energy Theorem is one of the most powerful statements in classical mechanics: the net work done on an object equals the change in its kinetic energy. This is derived directly from Newton's Second Law (F = ma) by integrating over displacement, so it is not an independent law — it is Newton's Second Law in energy language. Its power is that all the vector complexity of force analysis collapses into a single scalar energy-bookkeeping equation.

Applying the work-energy theorem: if you know the net work done on an object, you immediately know how much its kinetic energy changed. This often provides a faster route to an answer than the kinematic equations, especially when multiple forces act along curved paths where direction changes continuously.

KINETIC ENERGY:
  KE = ½mv²         [Joules, J]
  KE ≥ 0 always (scalar)
  If v doubles → KE quadruples

WORK-ENERGY THEOREM:
  W_net = ΔKE = ½mv_f² − ½mv_i²
  
  Derivation: F = ma, use v² = v₀² + 2aΔx
  → FΔx = ½mv² − ½mv₀²  ✓

Solving for final speed:
  v_f = √(v_i² + 2W_net/m)

Sub-Chapter 3.3 — Potential Energy and Conservative Forces

Not all work done on an object is permanently "gone" — some of it can be stored and later recovered as kinetic energy. This recoverable stored energy is called potential energy. It is associated with the configuration of a system — specifically, the relative positions of objects interacting through a conservative force.

A conservative force is one for which the work done depends only on the initial and final positions, not on the path taken between them. Equivalently, the work done by a conservative force around any closed loop is zero. Gravity and the spring force are both conservative. Kinetic friction is not — it dissipates more energy along a longer path, so the work done depends on path length.

For any conservative force, we define a potential energy function U such that the work done by the force equals the negative of the change in potential energy: W_conservative = −ΔU. The gravitational potential energy near Earth's surface is U_g = mgy (where y is height above a chosen reference). The elastic potential energy stored in a spring is U_spring = ½kx². Only changes in potential energy ΔU are physically meaningful — the choice of reference level (where U = 0) is arbitrary.

CONSERVATIVE FORCE:
  W_conservative = −ΔU    (path-independent)

GRAVITATIONAL POTENTIAL ENERGY (near Earth):
  U_g = mgy               [J]
  ΔU_g = mgΔy = mg(y_f − y_i)
  (choose reference y=0 wherever convenient)

ELASTIC POTENTIAL ENERGY (spring):
  U_spring = ½kx²         [J]
  x = displacement from natural (equilibrium) length

Sub-Chapter 3.4 — Conservation of Mechanical Energy

When only conservative forces act on a system, the sum of kinetic and potential energies — called the total mechanical energy — remains constant throughout the motion. This is the law of conservation of mechanical energy: E_mech = KE + U = constant. It transforms complicated force and trajectory problems into simple energy bookkeeping: wherever you are in the motion, E_mech is the same value.

In practice: KE_i + U_i = KE_f + U_f, or ½mv_i² + U_i = ½mv_f² + U_f. You can pick any two points in the motion, equate their total mechanical energies, and solve for the unknown. The power of this approach is that the path between the two points is completely irrelevant — only the initial and final states matter.

When energy is not conserved: If non-conservative forces like kinetic friction or air resistance do work, mechanical energy decreases (is converted to thermal energy). We account for this as: E_f = E_i + W_nc. Since friction always does negative work (removes mechanical energy), W_nc is negative whenever friction acts, and E_f < E_i.

CONSERVATION OF MECHANICAL ENERGY (no friction):
  E_mech = KE + PE = constant
  ½mv_i² + U_i = ½mv_f² + U_f

For gravity (U = mgy):
  ½mv_i² + mgy_i = ½mv_f² + mgy_f

WITH NON-CONSERVATIVE FORCES (e.g., friction):
  E_f = E_i + W_nc
  ½mv_f² + mgy_f = ½mv_i² + mgy_i + W_friction
  W_friction = −fₖd  (always negative → energy lost to heat)

Sub-Chapter 3.5 — Power

Power is the rate at which work is done, or equivalently, the rate at which energy is transferred or transformed. The same amount of work can be done quickly (high power) or slowly (low power). A weightlifter who lifts 100 kg by 2 m does the same work as an escalator carrying the same load the same height — but the weightlifter does it in 2 seconds while the escalator takes 20 seconds, so the weightlifter operates at ten times the power.

The SI unit of power is the Watt (W): 1 W = 1 J/s. Average power over a time interval is P_avg = W/Δt. Instantaneous power — the power at a single moment — is P = dW/dt = F · v = Fv cosθ. This form is particularly useful for analyzing engines and motors, where force may be constant but speed varies.

POWER:
  P_avg = W/Δt = ΔE/Δt    [Watts, W = J/s]
  P_inst = F · v = Fv cosθ [W]

Unit conversions:
  1 hp (horsepower) = 746 W
  1 kW = 1000 W

Common applications:
  Engine at constant speed: P = F_drive × v
    (ΣF = 0 at constant v → F_drive = F_drag; P = F_drag × v)
  Pumping fluid to height h at volume rate Q:
    P = ρgQh   (ρ = fluid density, Q = m³/s)

PART B: DIAGRAM SYSTEM

Diagram 3.1 — Work as Area Under F-x Graph

When force varies with position, work = area under the F vs. x curve. For a constant force, it is a rectangle (W = Fd). For a spring (F = kx), it is a triangle (W = ½kx²). This is the geometric interpretation of W = ∫F dx.

F (N)
│           ●  Spring: F = kx
│          /│
│         / │   Area = ½ × base × height
│        /  │       = ½ × x₀ × kx₀ = ½kx₀²
│       /   │       = elastic PE stored in spring
│      /    │
│     /     │
│    /      │
└───/───────┼────── x (m)
   0        x₀

Constant force (horizontal line): rectangle → W = F·d
Spring (rising line):              triangle → W = ½kx²
General curve: W = ∫F dx = area under curve (positive or negative)

Diagram 3.2 — Energy Bar Charts: Conservation Tracking

Energy bar charts show how total mechanical energy is distributed at different points. Total bar height stays constant when no non-conservative forces act. Friction converts mechanical energy to thermal energy (heat).

Ball dropped from height h — three positions:

Position A (top, at rest):     Position B (midway):     Position C (bottom):
│ PE │                         │PE│                     │   │
│    │                         │  │KE│                  │   │   │KE│
│    │                         │  │  │                  │   │   │  │
└────┘                         └──┴──┘                  └───┴───┴──┘
  ½kA²=0 + mgh₀                   half PE, half KE          all KE = mgh₀
                       
With friction (sliding down incline):
│PE │ →  │KE│PE│  →  │KE│PE│Th│
           (thermal bar grows as KE+PE decreases)
Total: KE + PE + Thermal = constant (energy conserved)

Diagram 3.3 — Power: Slope of Energy vs. Time

Power is the rate of energy transfer. Steeper slope on an E-t graph means higher power. For an object moving at speed v against a force F, instantaneous power P = Fv cosθ.

Energy (J)
│                   / ← high power agent (steep slope)
│                  /
│              ───/  ← low power agent (shallow slope)
│          ───/
│      ───/
│  ───/
└──────────────────────────── t (s)

P_avg = ΔE/Δt = total work / total time

Engine maintaining constant speed v against drag force Fd:
  ΣF = 0 → F_engine = F_drag
  P = F_engine × v = F_drag × v

Converting: 1 hp = 746 W = 746 J/s

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20 Questions)

Example 3.1 — Work by a Constant Force

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 50 N force at 37° above horizontal pushes a box 8 m. Find work done. Solution: 
W = Fd cosθ = 50(8)(cos37°) = 50(8)(0.8) = 320 J

Example 3.2 — Work-Energy Theorem

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 4 kg box starts at rest. Net force 20 N acts over 5 m. Find final speed. Solution: 
W_net = F·d = 20(5) = 100 J
W_net = ΔKE = ½mv² − 0
100 = ½(4)v²
v² = 50
v = 7.07 m/s

Example 3.3 — Energy Conservation: Falling

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Ball dropped from 20 m. Find speed at 5 m above ground. Solution: 
E conserved: mgh₁ = mgh₂ + ½mv²
g(20) = g(5) + ½v²
9.8(15) = ½v²
v² = 294
v = 17.1 m/s

Example 3.4 — Spring Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Spring (k=500 N/m) compressed 0.2 m launches 0.5 kg ball horizontally. Find launch speed. Solution: 
PE_spring = ½kx² = ½(500)(0.04) = 10 J
½mv² = 10
v² = 40
v = 6.32 m/s

Example 3.5 — Power Calculation

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 70 kg person climbs 20 m stairs in 30 s. Find average power output. Solution: 
W = mgh = 70(9.8)(20) = 13720 J
P = W/t = 13720/30 = 457 W (about 0.61 hp)

Example 3.6 — Friction and Energy Loss

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 3 kg block slides 4 m down a 30° incline with μₖ=0.
  1. Find (a) work by gravity, (b) work by friction, (c) final KE if starting from rest.
Solution: 
Height fallen: h = 4 sin30° = 2 m
(a) W_gravity = mgh = 3(9.8)(2) = 58.8 J

(b) N = mg cos30° = 3(9.8)(0.866) = 25.5 N
    fₖ = μₖN = 0.20(25.5) = 5.1 N
    W_friction = −fₖd = −5.1(4) = −20.4 J

(c) KE_f = W_net = W_gravity + W_friction = 58.8 − 20.4 = 38.4 J
    Check: ½mv² = 38.4 → v = √(25.6) = 5.06 m/s

Example 3.7 — Energy at Multiple Points

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 2 kg ball is thrown upward at 15 m/s from ground. Find KE and PE at h=5 m. (No friction) Solution: 
Initial KE = ½(2)(225) = 225 J
Initial PE = 0

At h=5 m (conservation):
PE = mgh = 2(9.8)(5) = 98 J
KE = 225 − 98 = 127 J
v = √(2×127/2) = √127 = 11.3 m/s

Example 3.8 — Power from Force and Velocity

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A car engine applies 3000 N at 25 m/s. What is the power output in watts and horsepower? Solution: 
P = Fv = 3000 × 25 = 75,000 W = 75 kW
hp = 75000/746 = 100.5 hp

Example 3.9 — Variable Force Work (AP-C)

Type: Calculus Derivation Question: F(x) = (3x² + 2x) N. Find work from x=0 to x=3 m. Solution: 
W = ∫₀³ (3x² + 2x) dx = [x³ + x²]₀³ = (27 + 9) − 0 = 36 J

Example 3.10 — Conservation with Spring

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 1 kg block compresses spring (k=800 N/m) by 0.15 m. Spring releases block on frictionless floor. Find max speed. Solution: 
½kx² = ½mv²
½(800)(0.0225) = ½(1)v²
9 = ½v²
v = √18 = 4.24 m/s

Example 3.11 — Roller Coaster Loop

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A car at top of 40 m high hill enters a loop of radius 12 m. What is minimum speed at loop top? Height of loop top = 24 m. Solution: 
At loop top: minimum speed when N=0 → v_top = √(gr) = √(9.8×12) = 10.8 m/s

Energy conservation (hill top to loop top):
½mv₀² + mgh₁ = ½mv_top² + mgh₂
v₀² + 2g(h₁) = v_top² + 2g(h₂)
v₀² = v_top² + 2g(h₂ − h₁) = 116.6 + 2(9.8)(24−40) = 116.6 − 313.6 → negative
This means: need only enough speed at hill top to maintain contact at loop.
v₀_min at hill top: ½mv₀² + mg(40) = ½m(gr) + mg(24)
v₀² = gr + 2g(24−40) = gr − 32g = g(r−32) = 9.8(12−32) < 0
→ Starting height of 40 m is more than sufficient.

Example 3.12 — Hooke's Law & Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Two identical springs (k=300 N/m each). (a) What is total PE if both compressed 0.1 m? (b) If in parallel (same compression)? Solution: 
(a) Each: PE = ½(300)(0.01) = 1.5 J. Total = 3 J
(b) Parallel springs: k_eff = 600 N/m, x = 0.1 m
    PE = ½(600)(0.01) = 3 J (same!)
    
    If in series: k_eff = 150 N/m, same force F=kx shared

Example 3.13 — Energy Lost to Friction

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Block slides 6 m across floor and decelerates from 8 m/s to 3 m/s. Mass = 2 kg. Find μₖ. Solution: 
Energy lost = ΔKE = ½m(v₀² − v²) = ½(2)(64−9) = 55 J
W_friction = fₖ × d = μₖmg × d = 55
μₖ = 55/(2 × 9.8 × 6) = 55/117.6 = 0.468

Example 3.14 — Efficiency

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A motor (1500 W) lifts a 200 kg load 10 m. It takes 20 s. Find efficiency. Solution: 
Useful work done = mgh = 200(9.8)(10) = 19600 J
Energy input = P × t = 1500 × 20 = 30000 J
Efficiency = (19600/30000) × 100% = 65.3%

Example 3.15 — Escape from Gravity (PE → KE)

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Ball launched from ground at 15 m/s. What is its height when speed = 10 m/s? (No friction) Solution: 
½mv₀² = ½mv² + mgh
½(225) = ½(100) + 9.8h
112.5 = 50 + 9.8h
h = 62.5/9.8 = 6.38 m

Example 3.16 — Power and Velocity of Car

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Car engine provides constant power 60 kW. Mass = 1200 kg. At what speed does the car reach maximum velocity (when driving force equals friction = 800 N)? Solution: 
P = Fv at constant speed
60000 = 800 × v
v = 75 m/s = 270 km/h

Example 3.17 — Pendulum Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A 0.5 kg pendulum bob swings from 0.3 m above lowest point. Find (a) max speed, (b) speed at 0.1 m above lowest point. Solution: 
(a) All PE → KE at bottom:
    mgh = ½mv²
    v = √(2gh) = √(2×9.8×0.3) = √5.88 = 2.42 m/s

(b) At h=0.1 m:
    mgh₀ = mgh + ½mv²
    v = √(2g(h₀−h)) = √(2×9.8×0.2) = √3.92 = 1.98 m/s

Example 3.18 — Spring Launched Projectile

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Spring (k=400 N/m, compressed 0.25 m) launches 0.2 kg ball at 30° above horizontal. Find range. (g=9.8 m/s²) Solution: 
PE_spring = ½(400)(0.0625) = 12.5 J = ½mv²
v₀ = √(2×12.5/0.2) = √125 = 11.18 m/s

v₀ₓ = 11.18 cos30° = 9.68 m/s
v₀ᵧ = 11.18 sin30° = 5.59 m/s
T = 2v₀ᵧ/g = 1.14 s
R = v₀ₓ × T = 9.68 × 1.14 = 11.0 m

Example 3.19 — Energy Theorem with Calculus

Type: Calculus Derivation Question: F(x) = (10 − 2x) N acts on 2 kg object at rest. Find speed at x=4 m. Solution: 
W = ∫₀⁴ (10 − 2x) dx = [10x − x²]₀⁴ = 40 − 16 = 24 J
W = ΔKE = ½mv² − 0
24 = ½(2)v²
v = √24 = 4.90 m/s

Example 3.20 — AP FRQ: Complete Energy Analysis

Type: Free Response Question: A 5 kg block starts from rest at top of ramp (h=4 m, length=8 m, μₖ=0.15). (a) Find speed at bottom using energy methods. (b) Block then slides on flat floor and compresses a spring (k=600 N/m). How far is spring compressed? (c) Does block return to original height? Justify. (d) Sketch energy bar charts at each stage. Solution: 
(a) On ramp:
    W_friction = −μₖmg cosθ × L = −0.15(5)(9.8)(cos30°)(8)
    cos θ = h/L → sinθ = 4/8 = 0.5, θ=30°, cosθ=0.866
    W_friction = −0.15(5)(9.8)(0.866)(8) = −50.9 J
    
    E_initial = mgh = 5(9.8)(4) = 196 J
    KE_bottom = 196 − 50.9 = 145.1 J
    v = √(2×145.1/5) = √58.04 = 7.62 m/s

(b) On flat floor with spring (assuming frictionless floor):
    ½mv² = ½kx²
    145.1 = ½(600)x²
    x² = 290.2/600 = 0.484
    x = 0.695 m

(c) No. Energy was lost to friction on the ramp. Even if spring and floor are frictionless,
    block returns with only 145.1 J (not 196 J), which brings it only to h=145.1/(mg)=2.97 m
    (not original 4 m).

(d) Energy bars:
    Initial: [====large====] PE, [empty] KE, [empty] Spring PE
    Bottom of ramp: [=====] KE, [empty] PE, [empty] Spring PE (some lost to heat)
    Max spring: [empty] KE, [empty] PE, [=====] Spring PE

FRQ 1 — Energy Conservation on Curved Track

(a) 2 kg block slides from rest at h=6 m down frictionless curved track. Find speed at bottom. (b) Track has μₖ=0.2 for bottom 4 m. Find final speed. (c) Draw LOL diagram for each scenario. Answer: 
(a) mgh = ½mv² → v = √(2gh) = √(2×9.8×6) = √117.6 = 10.8 m/s
(b) Energy lost = μₖmg×d = 0.2(2)(9.8)(4) = 15.68 J
    KE_final = mgh − 15.68 = 117.6 − 15.68 = 101.9 J
    v = √(2×101.9/2) = √101.9 = 10.1 m/s

FRQ 2 — Spring-Block System

k=1000 N/m, block 2 kg, compressed 0.3 m, μₖ=0.25 on horizontal surface. (a) Initial PE. (b) Max speed. (c) Distance until block stops. Answer: 
(a) PE = ½(1000)(0.09) = 45 J
(b) v = √(2×45/2) = √45 = 6.71 m/s
(c) Distance: fₖ×d = 45 J → μₖmg×d = 45 → d = 45/(0.25×2×9.8) = 9.18 m

FRQ 3 — Power and Performance

A 1200 kg car's engine outputs 90 kW. Resistive force = 1500 N. (a) Max constant speed. (b) Acceleration at 20 m/s. (c) Power at 20 m/s maintaining constant speed. Answer: 
(a) P = Fv → 90000 = 1500v → v = 60 m/s
(b) Net force = P/v − friction = 90000/20 − 1500 = 4500 − 1500 = 3000 N
    a = 3000/1200 = 2.5 m/s²
(c) P = F×v = 1500×20 = 30,000 W = 30 kW (only friction force at constant speed)

FRQ 4 — Gravitational PE and Projectiles

Ball launched at v₀=20 m/s, 53° above horizontal from 15 m cliff. (a) PE at launch point. (b) KE at top of trajectory. (c) Speed at ground. (d) At what height is KE=PE? Answer: 
(a) PE = mgh = m(9.8)(15) [need mass — use m=1 kg as reference]
(b) At peak: v = v₀cosθ = 20(0.6)=12 m/s; KE=½(1)(144)=72 J
(c) E_total at launch = KE+PE = ½(1)(400) + (1)(9.8)(15) = 200+147=347 J
    At ground: ½v²=347 → v=√694=26.3 m/s
(d) KE=PE: E_total/2=PE=mgh → h=173.5/(9.8)=17.7 m above ground
    Since cliff is 15 m, height above ground = 17.7 m (ball is 2.7 m above cliff top)

FRQ 5 — Variable Force Work (AP-C)

F(x) = (8x − 2x²) N for 0 ≤ x ≤ 4 m. Object starts from rest, mass 4 kg. (a) Work from x=0 to x=
  1. (b) Speed at x=4. (c) Where is force maximum?
Answer: 
(a) W = ∫₀⁴(8x−2x²)dx = [4x²−(2/3)x³]₀⁴ = 64−(2/3)(64) = 64−42.7 = 21.3 J
(b) v = √(2W/m) = √(2×21.3/4) = √10.67 = 3.27 m/s
(c) dF/dx = 8 − 4x = 0 → x = 2 m; F(2) = 16−8 = 8 N

Unit 04: Linear Momentum, Impulse & Collisions

AP Physics 1 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CONCEPTS

4.1 — Momentum: The Quantity of Motion

Newton originally formulated his Second Law not in terms of force and acceleration, but in terms of what he called "quantity of motion" — what we now call linear momentum. Momentum is defined as the product of an object's mass and velocity: p = mv. Because velocity is a vector, momentum is also a vector, pointing in the same direction as the velocity. The SI unit is kg·m/s, equivalently expressed as N·s.

Momentum captures both how massive an object is and how fast it moves. A slowly moving freight train and a high-speed rifle bullet can have comparable momenta, but from very different combinations of mass and velocity. Intuitively, momentum measures how hard it is to stop an object — a more apt measure of "threat" in a collision than either mass or speed alone.

Impulse (J) is the product of the net force and the time interval over which it acts: J = FΔt = Δp. This relationship — the impulse-momentum theorem — is a direct restatement of Newton's Second Law: F = ma = m(Δv/Δt) → FΔt = mΔv = Δp. Its practical importance is immense: a given momentum change can result from a large force over a short time (a bat hitting a ball) or a smaller force over a longer time (an airbag extending the collision time to reduce peak force on an occupant and prevent injury).

Impulse as area: When force varies with time, the impulse equals the area under the F-versus-t graph: J = ∫F dt. This allows the momentum change to be found even for complicated, time-varying forces.

LINEAR MOMENTUM:
  p = mv              [kg·m/s = N·s, vector]
  |p| = mv            (scalar magnitude, always ≥ 0)

IMPULSE-MOMENTUM THEOREM:
  J = FΔt = Δp = mv_f − mv_i     [N·s]
  F_avg = Δp/Δt                   [N]
  
Variable force: J = ∫F dt = area under F-t graph

Key insight: Same Δp can result from:
  Large F × small Δt  (bat hitting ball — large impact force)
  Small F × large Δt  (airbag — reduced peak force, same Δp)

4.2 — Conservation of Linear Momentum

The most powerful result in momentum physics: the total momentum of an isolated system is constant. A system is "isolated" when the net external force acting on it is zero. This follows directly from Newton's Third Law: internal forces between objects within the system come in equal-and-opposite pairs that cancel exactly in the total momentum sum.

Conservation of momentum applies during all collisions and explosions, regardless of the internal forces involved. Even when internal forces are enormous (as in a car crash), total momentum of the two-car system is conserved as long as road friction and other external forces are negligible during the brief collision interval — an approximation that is often excellent because collisions are so brief.

For a two-body system: m₁v₁ᵢ + m₂v₂ᵢ = m₁v₁f + m₂v₂f. This is a vector equation. In two-dimensional problems, it must be applied separately in the x and y directions, providing two equations for the unknowns.

CONSERVATION OF MOMENTUM (isolated system, ΣF_ext = 0):
  p_total = Σmᵢvᵢ = constant
  
Two-body:
  m₁v₁ᵢ + m₂v₂ᵢ = m₁v₁f + m₂v₂f   [vector equation!]

In 2D — apply separately:
  x: m₁v₁ₓᵢ + m₂v₂ₓᵢ = m₁v₁ₓf + m₂v₂ₓf
  y: m₁v₁ᵧᵢ + m₂v₂ᵧᵢ = m₁v₁ᵧf + m₂v₂ᵧf

Isolated = good approximation when:
  Collision is brief (external forces act too short to matter)
  In space (no friction or external gravitational influence)

4.3 — Types of Collisions

Collisions are categorized by what happens to kinetic energy, even though momentum is conserved in all of them. This distinction is critical for AP problems that ask you to identify collision type or find final velocities.

Elastic collisions conserve both momentum and kinetic energy. No permanent deformation occurs; the objects bounce off each other with no loss of kinetic energy. Atomic-scale collisions and ideal billiard-ball collisions are approximately elastic. Using both conservation equations simultaneously yields two equations for two unknowns (the two final velocities). A particularly important special case: when equal masses collide elastically in 1D, the objects exchange velocities — the moving one stops, and the stationary one takes on the original velocity.

Perfectly inelastic collisions occur when the objects stick together after the collision, moving as one combined mass. This produces the maximum possible kinetic energy loss consistent with momentum conservation. The final velocity is v_f = (m₁v₁ᵢ + m₂v₂ᵢ)/(m₁ + m₂) — simply a weighted average of initial velocities.

Inelastic collisions are everything in between: momentum conserved, some KE lost, but objects separate after impact. Since only one conservation equation is available (momentum — KE is not conserved), you need additional information to fully solve the problem unless it is perfectly inelastic.

ALL COLLISIONS: Momentum always conserved

ELASTIC: KE also conserved
  v₁f = ((m₁−m₂)v₁ᵢ + 2m₂v₂ᵢ) / (m₁+m₂)
  v₂f = (2m₁v₁ᵢ + (m₂−m₁)v₂ᵢ) / (m₁+m₂)
  Special case (m₁=m₂): v₁f = v₂ᵢ, v₂f = v₁ᵢ  (velocities swap!)

PERFECTLY INELASTIC: Objects stick → move as one
  v_f = (m₁v₁ᵢ + m₂v₂ᵢ) / (m₁+m₂)
  ΔKE = KE_f − KE_i < 0  (maximum KE loss)

INELASTIC: KE lost, objects separate
  Only momentum eq. available → need extra info to fully solve

4.4 — Center of Mass

The center of mass (CM) of a system is the mass-weighted average position — the single point at which the total mass could be concentrated to correctly describe the system's translational response to external forces. For a two-body system: x_cm = (m₁x₁ + m₂x₂)/(m₁ + m₂). For extended or multi-body systems, this extends to a weighted sum over all mass elements.

The critical theorem governing center-of-mass motion: ΣF_ext = Ma_cm, where M is the total mass. The center of mass moves exactly as if all the mass were concentrated there and all external forces acted on it. Internal forces — however violent — cannot change the velocity of the center of mass. This is why a firework exploding in mid-air has all its fragments' center of mass continuing on exactly the original parabolic trajectory. Only external forces (gravity, air, the ground) can change where the center of mass goes.

CENTER OF MASS POSITION:
  x_cm = (m₁x₁ + m₂x₂ + ...) / M_total
  y_cm = (m₁y₁ + m₂y₂ + ...) / M_total

CENTER OF MASS DYNAMICS:
  v_cm = p_total / M = Σmᵢvᵢ / M
  a_cm = ΣF_ext / M  (Newton's 2nd for entire system)

ISOLATED SYSTEM (ΣF_ext = 0):
  v_cm = constant   (CM moves at constant velocity)
  Internal forces do NOT change CM motion

Classic result: Exploding projectile — all fragments' CM follows
  the original parabolic path (gravity is only external force)

PART B: DIAGRAMS

Diagram 4.1 — Impulse as Area Under F-t Graph

Impulse = area under F-t graph = momentum change. Same area (impulse) → same Δp, regardless of the force profile. Airbags extend collision time to reduce peak force for the same momentum change.

F (N)
│    ╔══════════╗
│    ║          ║  Constant force: J = F·Δt = area of rectangle
│    ║ Area = J ║
│    ╚══════════╝
└──────────────────── t (s)
     t₁          t₂

F (N)
│     /╲
│    /  ╲  Variable force (collision spike): J = ∫F dt = area under curve
│   /    ╲
│  /      ╲
└──────────────────── t (s)

Both give SAME impulse J = Δp = m(vf − vi) if areas are equal

Airbag: same Δp (car decelerates), but larger Δt → smaller peak F → less injury

Diagram 4.2 — Momentum Conservation: Before and After

Total momentum is conserved in all collisions. Elastic collisions also conserve KE; perfectly inelastic (stick together) maximize KE loss. Equal-mass elastic collision: velocities exchange completely.

BEFORE:   [m₁] →v₁     [m₂] at rest

ELASTIC (equal masses):
AFTER:    [m₁] stops    [m₂] →v₁   (velocities exchanged!)

ELASTIC (unequal masses, m₁ > m₂):
AFTER:    [m₁] →v₁'    [m₂] →v₂'  (both move forward, m₂ faster)

PERFECTLY INELASTIC:
AFTER:    [m₁+m₂] →vf   vf = m₁v₁/(m₁+m₂)   (maximum KE loss)

All cases: m₁v₁ + m₂·0 = m₁v₁' + m₂v₂'  (momentum conserved)
Elastic adds: ½m₁v₁² = ½m₁v₁'² + ½m₂v₂'² (KE conserved)

Diagram 4.3 — Center of Mass: External Forces Only

The center of mass (CM) of a system moves as if all mass were concentrated there and all external forces acted there. Internal forces (like explosion fragments pushing on each other) cannot change the CM motion.

Exploding projectile at peak of trajectory:

         ● peak velocity: vx = v₀cosθ, vy = 0
        /│       / │ \ ← fragments fly apart
      /  │       ↙   ↓   ↘ fragments

CM path (dashed):    parabola continues unchanged!
                  ──────●──────────●──────────●──

Because: Only gravity acts on the system externally
         Internal explosion forces cancel in pairs (3rd Law)
         a_cm = g downward → CM follows original parabola

CM position: x_cm = (m₁x₁ + m₂x₂)/(m₁+m₂)

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 4.1 — Momentum Calculation

Q: 5 kg object at 8 m/s east. Momentum? 
p = mv = 5(8) = 40 kg·m/s east

Ex 4.2 — Impulse

Q: 100 N force acts on 3 kg for 0.5 s from rest. Find impulse and final speed. 
J = FΔt = 100(0.5) = 50 N·s
Δp = 50: mv_f = 50 → v_f = 50/3 = 16.7 m/s

Ex 4.3 — Conservation: Perfectly Inelastic

Q: 4 kg at 6 m/s hits 2 kg at rest. They stick. Find v_f. 
m₁v₁ = (m₁+m₂)v_f
4(6) = 6×v_f → v_f = 4 m/s
KE_lost = ½(4)(36) − ½(6)(16) = 72 − 48 = 24 J

Ex 4.4 — Conservation: Elastic (Equal Masses)

Q: 3 kg at 5 m/s hits 3 kg at rest. Elastic. Final velocities? 
Equal mass elastic: v₁f = 0, v₂f = 5 m/s (velocity exchange)
Check p: 3(5) = 3(0) + 3(5) = 15 ✓
Check KE: ½(3)(25) = ½(3)(25) ✓

Ex 4.5 — Impulse from Graph

Q: F-t graph: triangle from t=0 to t=4 s, peak F=20 N at t=2 s. Impulse? 
J = ½ × base × height = ½(4)(20) = 40 N·s

Ex 4.6 — Explosion

Q: 5 kg bomb at rest explodes. 2 kg fragment goes left at 10 m/s. Find velocity of 3 kg fragment. 
Initial p = 0
0 = 2(−10) + 3v → v = 20/3 = 6.67 m/s right

Ex 4.7 — Force from Momentum Change

Q: Ball (0.1 kg) at 20 m/s hits wall, bounces at 15 m/s. Contact time = 0.01 s. Avg force? 
Δp = m(v_f − v_i) = 0.1(−15−20) = −3.5 N·s
F_avg = Δp/Δt = −3.5/0.01 = −350 N (from wall on ball)
Magnitude = 350 N

Ex 4.8 — 2D Collision

Q: 2 kg at 4 m/s east + 3 kg at 3 m/s north. They stick. Find v_f. 
pₓ = 2(4) = 8 kg·m/s
pᵧ = 3(3) = 9 kg·m/s
Total mass = 5 kg
vₓ = 8/5 = 1.6 m/s, vᵧ = 9/5 = 1.8 m/s
|v| = √(1.6² + 1.8²) = √(2.56+3.24) = √5.80 = 2.41 m/s
θ = arctan(1.8/1.6) = 48.4° north of east

Ex 4.9 — Center of Mass

Q: 3 kg at x=1 m, 5 kg at x=5 m, 2 kg at x=9 m. Find x_cm. 
x_cm = (3×1 + 5×5 + 2×9)/(3+5+2) = (3+25+18)/10 = 46/10 = 4.6 m

Ex 4.10 — Elastic: Unequal Masses

Q: 6 kg at 4 m/s hits stationary 2 kg. Elastic. Find v₁f and v₂f. 
v₁f = (m₁−m₂)v₁ᵢ/(m₁+m₂) = (6−2)(4)/(6+2) = 16/8 = 2 m/s (same direction)
v₂f = 2m₁v₁ᵢ/(m₁+m₂) = 2(6)(4)/8 = 48/8 = 6 m/s (same direction)
Check p: 6(4) = 6(2)+2(6) = 12+12=24 ✓

Ex 4.11 — Bullet-Block

Q: 0.02 kg bullet at 300 m/s embeds in 1 kg block on frictionless table. Find v_f and % KE lost. 
v_f = (0.02×300)/(0.02+1) = 6/1.02 = 5.88 m/s
KE_i = ½(0.02)(90000) = 900 J
KE_f = ½(1.02)(5.88²) = ½(1.02)(34.6) = 17.6 J
% lost = (900−17.6)/900 × 100 = 98.0%

Ex 4.12 — Recoil

Q: Astronaut (80 kg) in space throws 2 kg wrench at 6 m/s. Astronaut recoil speed? 
m_A v_A + m_w v_w = 0
80v_A = −2(6) = −12
v_A = −0.15 m/s (opposite to wrench)

Ex 4.13 — Airbag Example

Q: Driver (75 kg) at 15 m/s stops. Without airbag: 0.05 s. With airbag: 0.3 s. Compare forces. 
Δp = 75(15) = 1125 N·s (same both cases)
Without: F = 1125/0.05 = 22,500 N
With: F = 1125/0.3 = 3,750 N
Airbag reduces force by factor of 6!

Ex 4.14 — Multi-Particle CM Velocity

Q: 3 kg moving +5 m/s, 2 kg moving −3 m/s. System CM velocity? 
v_cm = (m₁v₁+m₂v₂)/(m₁+m₂) = (3×5 + 2×(−3))/5 = (15−6)/5 = 9/5 = 1.8 m/s

Ex 4.15 — Momentum vs KE

Q: Same KE for 4 kg (v₁) and 9 kg (v₂). Ratio p₁/p₂? 
½m₁v₁² = ½m₂v₂² → v₁/v₂ = √(m₂/m₁) = √(9/4) = 3/2
p₁/p₂ = m₁v₁/(m₂v₂) = 4(3/2)/(9×1) × (v₂/v₂) = 6/9 = 2/3

Ex 4.16 — Impulse from Variable Force (AP-C)

Q: F(t) = (6t²) N acts on 1 kg from t=0 to t=3 s. Find Δv. 
J = ∫₀³ 6t² dt = [2t³]₀³ = 2(27) = 54 N·s
Δv = J/m = 54/1 = 54 m/s

Ex 4.17 — Partially Elastic

Q: 3 kg at 8 m/s hits 5 kg at rest. After: 3 kg at 1 m/s. Find 5 kg speed and verify momentum. 
p_initial = 3(8) = 24 kg·m/s
p_final: 3(1) + 5v₂ = 24 → v₂ = 21/5 = 4.2 m/s
KE_i = ½(3)(64) = 96 J
KE_f = ½(3)(1) + ½(5)(17.64) = 1.5 + 44.1 = 45.6 J
KE lost = 50.4 J (inelastic)

Ex 4.18 — 2D Elastic Collision

Q: Billiard ball A (1 kg, 3 m/s right) hits B (1 kg, at rest). A deflects 30° from original. B's direction? 
Equal masses elastic: A and B velocities perpendicular after collision.
A goes 30° from x-axis → B goes 90°−30°=60° from x-axis (below x-axis).
Using conservation to find speeds:
v_A² + v_B² = v₀² = 9 (equal mass elastic: speed relationship)
p_x: v_A cos30° + v_B cos(−60°) = 3
p_y: v_A sin30° − v_B sin60° = 0 → v_A = v_B × √3

Solving: v_B = 3/2 = 1.5 m/s, v_A = 1.5√3 = 2.60 m/s

Ex 4.19 — System Approach

Q: Two masses connected by compressed spring (total p=0). Spring releases: 4 kg gets 3 m/s. Find 6 kg velocity and system KE. 
0 = 4(3) + 6v → v = −2 m/s
KE = ½(4)(9) + ½(6)(4) = 18 + 12 = 30 J (from spring PE)

Ex 4.20 — AP FRQ: Ballistic Pendulum

A 0.03 kg bullet at v₀ embeds in 1 kg pendulum bob. Bob rises height h=0.12 m. (a) Find v₀. (b) KE before and after. (c) % KE lost. (d) Why not elastic? 
(a) v_f = √(2gh) = √(2×9.8×0.12) = 1.53 m/s
    Momentum: 0.03×v₀ = (0.03+1)(1.53)
    v₀ = 1.03×1.53/0.03 = 52.6 m/s

(b) KE_i = ½(0.03)(52.6²) = ½(0.03)(2767) = 41.5 J
    KE_f = ½(1.03)(1.53²) = ½(1.03)(2.34) = 1.21 J

(c) % lost = (41.5−1.21)/41.5 = 97.1%

(d) Bullet deforms, generates heat → can't be elastic

PART D: 50-QUESTION TEST BANK & FRQ

Free Response Questions (FRQ 1-10)

FRQ 1 -- Basic Impulse and Momentum
A 0.25 kg ball is thrown at a wall at 15 m/s and rebounds at 10 m/s. Contact time = 0.008 s. (a) Find the change in momentum. (b) Find the average force. (c) How does the result change if the contact time is doubled? 

(a) Dp = m*(v_f - v_i) = 0.25*(-10 - 15) = -6.25 N*s  (6.25 N*s away from wall)
(b) F_avg = Dp/Dt = 6.25/0.008 = 781 N
(c) Double Dt: F_avg halved to 391 N (same impulse = same Dp, but spread over longer time)
FRQ 2 -- Perfectly Inelastic Collision
A 1200 kg car heading north at 20 m/s collides with a 1600 kg SUV heading east at 15 m/s. They stick together. (a) Find the velocity (magnitude and direction) of the combined wreck. (b) Find the kinetic energy lost in the collision. 

(a) p_north = 1200*20 = 24000 kg*m/s
    p_east  = 1600*15 = 24000 kg*m/s
    Total mass = 2800 kg
    v_north = 24000/2800 = 8.57 m/s
    v_east  = 24000/2800 = 8.57 m/s
    |v| = sqrt(8.57^2 + 8.57^2) = 8.57*sqrt(2) = 12.1 m/s
    theta = 45 degrees NE

(b) KE_before = 0.5*1200*400 + 0.5*1600*225 = 240000 + 180000 = 420000 J
    KE_after  = 0.5*2800*(12.1)^2 = 1400*146.4 = 205,000 J
    KE lost   = 420000 - 205000 = 215,000 J
FRQ 3 -- Elastic Collision Analysis
A 4 kg ball at 8 m/s east collides elastically with a 2 kg ball at 2 m/s west. (a) Write conservation equations. (b) Solve for final velocities. (c) Verify both conservations. 

(a) v1_i=8, v2_i=-2 (taking east positive)
    Momentum: 4(8) + 2(-2) = 4*v1' + 2*v2'  ->  28 = 4v1' + 2v2'
    KE: 0.5*4*64 + 0.5*2*4 = 0.5*4*v1'^2 + 0.5*2*v2'^2  ->  132 = 2v1'^2 + v2'^2

(b) From elastic formulas:
    v1' = [(m1-m2)v1 + 2*m2*v2]/(m1+m2) = [(4-2)*8 + 2*2*(-2)]/6 = (16-8)/6 = 8/6 = 1.33 m/s
    v2' = [(m2-m1)v2 + 2*m1*v1]/(m1+m2) = [(2-4)(-2) + 2*4*8]/6 = (4+64)/6 = 68/6 = 11.33 m/s

(c) Momentum: 4(1.33)+2(11.33) = 5.33+22.67 = 28 ✓
    KE: 0.5*4*(1.33)^2 + 0.5*2*(11.33)^2 = 3.54 + 128.5 = 132 J ✓
FRQ 4 -- Explosion and Center of Mass
A 6 kg object initially at rest explodes. Fragment A (2 kg) goes north at 9 m/s, Fragment B (3 kg) goes south at 4 m/s. Fragment C (1 kg) goes in unknown direction. Find Fragment C's velocity. 

Conservation of momentum (initially zero):
North-South (N positive):
  0 = 2(9) + 3(-4) + 1*v_Cy
  0 = 18 - 12 + v_Cy  ->  v_Cy = -6 m/s (southward)

East-West: no initial momentum in EW, no fragments A or B have EW component:
  0 = 1*v_Cx -> v_Cx = 0

Fragment C: 6 m/s southward.
FRQ 5 -- Ballistic Pendulum
A 0.015 kg bullet at v_0 embeds in a 2 kg block hanging as a pendulum (L=1.2 m). Block swings to 25 cm height. Find v_0. 

Step 1: Block+bullet just after collision (momentum conservation):
  m_b * v_0 = (m_b + M) * v_1
  0.015 * v_0 = 2.015 * v_1   ...(1)

Step 2: After collision, swing up (energy conservation):
  0.5*(m_b+M)*v_1^2 = (m_b+M)*g*h
  v_1^2 = 2*g*h = 2*9.8*0.25 = 4.9
  v_1 = 2.21 m/s

Step 3: From (1): v_0 = 2.015*2.21/0.015 = 297 m/s
FRQ 6 -- 2D Collision
A 5 kg puck moves at 4 m/s east and hits a stationary 3 kg puck. After, the 5 kg puck moves at 30 degrees north of east at 3 m/s. Find the velocity of the 3 kg puck. 

Initial: p_x = 5*4 = 20, p_y = 0

After 5 kg puck: p_x1 = 5*3*cos30 = 15*0.866 = 12.99, p_y1 = 5*3*sin30 = 7.5

Remaining for 3 kg puck:
  p_x2 = 20 - 12.99 = 7.01  ->  v_x2 = 7.01/3 = 2.34 m/s
  p_y2 = 0 - 7.5 = -7.5     ->  v_y2 = -7.5/3 = -2.5 m/s  (south)

|v_2| = sqrt(2.34^2 + 2.5^2) = sqrt(5.48+6.25) = sqrt(11.73) = 3.42 m/s
theta = arctan(2.5/2.34) = 46.9 degrees south of east
FRQ 7 -- Impulse from Variable Force
A force varies as F(t) = 6t^2 - 4t (N) from t=0 to t=3 s. A 2 kg object starts at rest. Find (a) impulse, (b) final velocity, (c) average force. 

(a) J = Integral(0 to 3) F dt = Integral(6t^2 - 4t) dt = [2t^3 - 2t^2] from 0 to 3
    J = 2(27) - 2(9) - 0 = 54 - 18 = 36 N*s

(b) J = m*Delta_v -> 36 = 2*v_f -> v_f = 18 m/s

(c) F_avg = J/Delta_t = 36/3 = 12 N
FRQ 8 -- Momentum and Energy Combined
A 3 kg block at 5 m/s slides into a spring (k=200 N/m) on a frictionless surface. (a) Maximum compression. (b) Speed when spring is compressed 0.1 m. (c) Is the spring interaction elastic? 

(a) At max compression: all KE to spring PE
    0.5*3*25 = 0.5*200*x_max^2
    x_max = sqrt(37.5/100) = sqrt(0.375) = 0.612 m

(b) Energy conservation: 0.5*3*25 = 0.5*3*v^2 + 0.5*200*0.01
    37.5 = 1.5*v^2 + 1
    v^2 = 36.5/1.5 = 24.33
    v = 4.93 m/s

(c) Yes -- the spring stores and releases energy without loss.
    After block leaves spring, KE = original KE = 37.5 J.
    Spring interactions conserve mechanical energy -- elastic collision.
FRQ 9 -- Rocket Thrust
A 500 kg rocket in space ejects gas at 600 m/s (relative to rocket) at rate 5 kg/s. (a) Thrust force. (b) Initial acceleration. (c) Velocity after ejecting 100 kg of fuel. 

(a) Thrust = v_exhaust * (dm/dt) = 600 * 5 = 3000 N

(b) a_initial = F/m = 3000/500 = 6 m/s^2

(c) Tsiolkovsky rocket equation:
    Delta_v = v_e * ln(m_0/m_f) = 600 * ln(500/400)
    = 600 * ln(1.25) = 600 * 0.2231 = 133.9 m/s
FRQ 10 -- AP-Style Multi-Part
A 1500 kg car traveling at 25 m/s east rear-ends a stationary 2000 kg truck. They move together for 5 m on road (mu_k=0.4) then separate when they hit a wall. The wall exerts a 60,000 N force on the truck for 0.1 s. (a) Velocity just after initial collision. (b) Velocity when they reach the wall. (c) Velocity of truck after wall impact. (d) Final positions after all motion stops. 

(a) p_i = 1500*25 = 37500 kg*m/s
    v_together = 37500/3500 = 10.71 m/s east

(b) Friction deceleration: F_f = mu_k*(m1+m2)*g = 0.4*3500*9.8 = 13720 N
    a = -13720/3500 = -3.92 m/s^2
    v^2 = 10.71^2 + 2*(-3.92)*5 = 114.7 - 39.2 = 75.5
    v = 8.69 m/s east at wall

(c) Car separates (assume stays at wall impact velocity):
    Impulse on truck = -60000*0.1 = -6000 N*s (westward)
    Truck momentum = 2000*8.69 = 17380 kg*m/s east
    After: p_truck = 17380 - 6000 = 11380 kg*m/s
    v_truck = 11380/2000 = 5.69 m/s east

(d) Truck slides east from wall: v^2 = 5.69^2, a = -mu_k*g = -3.92 m/s^2
    d = v^2/(2a) = 32.38/7.84 = 4.13 m east of wall
    Car: at wall position, then may slide or be at rest depending on its final state.
🌀
Unit 05  ·  AP1
Rotational Dynamics
Torque · Inertia · Static Equilibrium
τ = rF sinθΣτ = IαI = ½MR²(disk)I = I_cm + Md²

Unit 05: Torque and Rotational Dynamics

AP Physics 1 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CONCEPTS

5.1 — Rotational Kinematics: The Angular Analogs

Rotational kinematics describes how objects rotate, using angular quantities that parallel the translational kinematic variables exactly. Every translational concept has a rotational counterpart, and all four SUVAT equations carry over directly with θ, ω, α replacing x, v, a.

Angular position (θ) is measured in radians. A radian is defined as the arc length s divided by the radius r: θ = s/r. One complete revolution equals 2π radians ≈ 6.283 rad; 1 rad ≈ 57.3°. Working in radians is essential because only then do the relationships v = ωr and a_t = αr hold without any conversion factors.

Angular velocity (ω) is the rate of change of angular position: ω = dθ/dt, measured in rad/s. Angular acceleration (α) is ω's rate of change: α = dω/dt, measured in rad/s². The tangential (linear) velocity of a point at radius r on a rotating body is v_t = ωr. The centripetal acceleration directed inward is a_c = ω²r = v²/r. When α ≠ 0, there is also a tangential acceleration a_t = αr.

For constant angular acceleration, the four rotational kinematic equations mirror the translational SUVAT equations perfectly — just replace x→θ, v→ω, a→α.

ANGULAR VARIABLES:
  θ [rad],   ω = dθ/dt [rad/s],   α = dω/dt [rad/s²]

TRANSLATIONAL–ROTATIONAL CONNECTION (at radius r):
  Arc length:            s = rθ
  Tangential speed:      v = rω
  Tangential accel:      aₜ = rα
  Centripetal accel:     aᶜ = ω²r = v²/r

ROTATIONAL KINEMATIC EQUATIONS (constant α):
  ω  = ω₀ + αt
  θ  = ω₀t + ½αt²
  ω² = ω₀² + 2αθ
  θ  = ½(ω₀ + ω)t

5.2 — Torque: The Rotational Analog of Force

Torque (τ) is the rotational analog of force — it is the quantity that causes angular acceleration. But torque is not just about the magnitude of the applied force. It depends critically on where the force is applied and at what angle, relative to the pivot point (axis of rotation).

The magnitude of torque is τ = rF sinφ, where r is the distance from the axis to the point of force application, F is the force magnitude, and φ is the angle between the r vector and the F vector. This can be rewritten as τ = F × d_perp, where d_perp = r sinφ is the lever arm or moment arm — the perpendicular distance from the axis of rotation to the line of action of the force. The longer the lever arm, the greater the torque for a given force. This is why door handles are placed far from hinges, and why wrenches are long.

Sign convention: counterclockwise (CCW) torques are positive; clockwise (CW) torques are negative (consistent with the right-hand rule defining the angular velocity vector direction). The rotational analog of Newton's Second Law is: Στ = Iα.

TORQUE:
  τ = r × F            (vector cross product)
  |τ| = rF sinφ = F × d_perp      [N·m]
  d_perp = r sinφ  (lever arm — perpendicular distance from axis to force line)
  
  Positive (CCW): tends to rotate counterclockwise
  Negative (CW):  tends to rotate clockwise

ROTATIONAL NEWTON'S SECOND LAW:
  Στ_net = Iα          (rotational analog of ΣF = ma)

5.3 — Moment of Inertia

The moment of inertia (I) is the rotational analog of mass — it quantifies resistance to angular acceleration. While translational inertia depends only on mass, rotational inertia depends on how that mass is distributed relative to the axis of rotation: I = Σmᵢrᵢ² for point masses, or I = ∫r² dm for continuous bodies. Mass farther from the axis contributes disproportionately (as r²) to the moment of inertia.

This r² dependence has profound practical consequences. A figure skater who pulls her arms in is moving mass closer to her rotation axis, decreasing I, and (by conservation of angular momentum) increasing ω — she spins faster. A hollow cylinder and solid cylinder of identical mass and radius have different moments of inertia because the hollow one concentrates all its mass at the maximum radius.

The parallel axis theorem provides a shortcut: I about any axis = I_cm + Md², where I_cm is the moment about the center-of-mass axis and d is the perpendicular distance between the two parallel axes.

MOMENT OF INERTIA:
  Point mass:    I = mr²            [kg·m²]
  System:        I = Σmᵢrᵢ²
  Continuous:    I = ∫r² dm

COMMON FORMULAS (axis through center of mass):
  Solid disk or cylinder:   I = ½MR²
  Thin hoop (ring):         I = MR²
  Solid sphere:             I = ⅖MR²
  Thin spherical shell:     I = ⅔MR²
  Thin rod (center):        I = (1/12)ML²
  Thin rod (one end):       I = ⅓ML²

PARALLEL AXIS THEOREM:
  I = I_cm + Md²   (d = distance between parallel axes)

5.4 — Static Equilibrium

An object is in static equilibrium when it is both at rest and remains at rest. This requires satisfying two simultaneous conditions: (1) the net force is zero (ΣF = 0) — no translational acceleration, and (2) the net torque about any axis is zero (Στ = 0) — no angular acceleration. Both must hold simultaneously.

The freedom to choose any pivot point for the torque equation is a powerful algebraic tool. By choosing the pivot at the location of an unknown force, that force produces zero torque (since r = 0) and disappears from the torque equation, leaving fewer unknowns to solve for. A classic AP scenario: a uniform beam supported at one end by a hinge and somewhere along its length by a cable. Taking torques about the hinge eliminates the hinge force entirely, directly yielding the cable tension in one equation.

CONDITIONS FOR STATIC EQUILIBRIUM:
  ΣFₓ = 0    (no net horizontal force)
  ΣFᵧ = 0    (no net vertical force)
  Στ = 0     (about any chosen axis)

STRATEGY: Choose pivot at location of an unknown force
  → that force disappears from torque equation (r=0, so τ=0)
  → solve for remaining unknowns more easily

UNIFORM OBJECT: Weight acts at geometric center (center of mass)

Example — Ladder problem:
  Forces: weight W at center, normal N_floor, friction f_floor, normal N_wall
  Take torques about base → eliminates N_floor and f_floor
  → solve for N_wall directly

PART B: DIAGRAMS

Diagram 5.1 — Torque and Lever Arm

Torque = Force × lever arm. The lever arm is the perpendicular distance from the pivot axis to the line of action of the force. Same force at longer lever arm produces larger torque (why door handles are far from hinges).

Method 1 (using angle between r and F):
  τ = rF sinφ   where φ = angle between position vector r and force F

          ↑ F (force applied at angle)
          │φ
     r ───●────────────── P (pivot)

  d_perp = r sinφ  (lever arm = perpendicular distance from P to F's line)

Method 2 (using lever arm directly):
  τ = F × d_perp

  ────────── F (horizontal)
       │
       │ d_perp ← perpendicular from pivot to force's line of action
       │
  ─────● P (pivot)

CCW torques: positive (+)     CW torques: negative (−)
Στ = Iα  (rotational Newton's 2nd Law)

Diagram 5.2 — Moment of Inertia Comparison

Objects with identical mass and radius can have very different moments of inertia. More mass concentrated farther from the axis → larger I → harder to angularly accelerate → slower in rolling race.

Same mass M, radius R — axis through center:

SOLID DISK     THIN HOOP      SOLID SPHERE   HOLLOW SPHERE
I = ½MR²       I = MR²        I = 2/5 MR²    I = 2/3 MR²

 ●●●●●●●        ●●●●●          ●●●●●●●●        ●●●●
●       ●      ●     ●        ●         ●      ●      ●
●   ●   ●      ●     ●        ●    ●    ●      ●  (hollow)
●       ●      ●     ●        ●         ●      ●      ●
 ●●●●●●●        ●●●●●          ●●●●●●●●        ●●●●

Mass spread       All mass        Mass spread     Mass on
throughout        at rim          throughout      surface

ROLLING RACE DOWN SAME RAMP (same h):
  v = √(2gh/(1 + I/MR²))
  Solid sphere (2/5): fastest   Hoop (1): slowest

Diagram 5.3 — Static Equilibrium: Choosing the Best Pivot

For static equilibrium: ΣF = 0 AND Στ = 0 (about any axis). Choose pivot at the location of an unknown force — its torque becomes zero, eliminating it from the equation and reducing unknowns.

Uniform beam (length L, weight W at center), hinge at left, cable at right:

     T↗ (tension in cable at angle θ)
      ╲
       ╲──────────────────────── beam
       H ← hinge (forces Hx, Hy unknown)        ● W (at L/2)
       │←────────────L/2────────→│←──L/2────→│

Take torques about H (eliminates Hx and Hy from equation):
  +T sinθ · L − W · (L/2) = 0    (T CCW, W CW)
  T = W/(2sinθ)   ← solved in one step!

Then: ΣFx = Hx − T cosθ = 0  →  Hx = T cosθ
      ΣFy = Hy + T sinθ − W = 0  →  Hy = W − T sinθ

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 5.1 — Torque

Q: 40 N force perpendicular at 0.8 m from pivot. Torque? 
τ = rF sinθ = 0.8 × 40 × sin90° = 32 N·m

Ex 5.2 — Net Torque

Q: 30 N at 0.5 m (CCW) and 20 N at 0.6 m (CW). Net torque? 
Στ = +30(0.5) − 20(0.6) = 15 − 12 = +3 N·m (CCW)

Ex 5.3 — Angular Acceleration

Q: Disk (I=0.4 kg·m²) has net torque 8 N·m. Angular acceleration? 
α = Στ/I = 8/0.4 = 20 rad/s²

Ex 5.4 — Moment of Inertia

Q: Two 3 kg masses at ends of 2 m massless rod rotating about center. I? 
I = Σmr² = 3(1)² + 3(1)² = 6 kg·m²

Ex 5.5 — Wheel Rotation from Rest

Q: Wheel (I=2 kg·m²) net torque 10 N·m. Angle covered in 4 s from rest? 
α = τ/I = 5 rad/s²
θ = ½αt² = ½(5)(16) = 40 rad

Ex 5.6 — Rotational KE

Q: Solid disk (M=4 kg, R=0.3 m) spinning at 10 rad/s. Rotational KE? 
I = ½MR² = ½(4)(0.09) = 0.18 kg·m²
KE = ½Iω² = ½(0.18)(100) = 9 J

Ex 5.7 — Rolling Object

Q: Solid sphere (I=2/5 MR²) rolls at v=5 m/s, M=2 kg. Total KE? 
KE_trans = ½mv² = ½(2)(25) = 25 J
ω = v/R; KE_rot = ½Iω² = ½(2/5 mR²)(v/R)² = ½(2/5)mv² = 1/5(2)(25) = 10 J
KE_total = 25 + 10 = 35 J

Ex 5.8 — Static Equilibrium: Seesaw

Q: 60 kg person sits 2 m left of pivot. 40 kg person where on right for balance? 
Στ = 0: 60(9.8)(2) = 40(9.8)(d) → d = 120/40 = 3 m from pivot

Ex 5.9 — Beam Supported at Ends

Q: 4 m beam (20 kg) with 50 kg load at 1 m from left. Find support forces. 
W_beam = 20(9.8) = 196 N at center (2 m)
W_load = 50(9.8) = 490 N at 1 m

Στ about left end = 0:
R₂(4) = 196(2) + 490(1) = 392 + 490 = 882
R₂ = 220.5 N

ΣFᵧ: R₁ + R₂ = 686 → R₁ = 465.5 N

Ex 5.10 — Torque from Gravity on Rod

Q: Uniform rod (mass 5 kg, length 3 m) hinged at one end, horizontal. What torque does gravity exert? 
Gravity acts at CM = 1.5 m from hinge
τ = mg × 1.5 = 5(9.8)(1.5) = 73.5 N·m (clockwise)

Ex 5.11 — Parallel Axis Theorem

Q: Solid disk I_cm = ½MR² = 0.5 kg·m². Find I about edge. 
d = R → I = I_cm + MR² = 0.5 + MR²
If I_cm = ½MR² = 0.5, then MR² = 1.0
I_edge = 0.5 + 1.0 = 1.5 kg·m²

Ex 5.12 — Newton's 2nd (Rotation)

Q: 3 kg disk (R=0.4 m) has tangential force 6 N at rim. α? 
τ = FR = 6(0.4) = 2.4 N·m
I = ½mr² = ½(3)(0.16) = 0.24 kg·m²
α = τ/I = 2.4/0.24 = 10 rad/s²

Ex 5.13 — Rolling Down Incline

Q: Solid cylinder (I=½MR²) rolls down h=2 m incline from rest. Find v at bottom. 
Energy: Mgh = ½Mv² + ½Iω² = ½Mv²(1 + I/MR²) = ½Mv²(1 + ½) = ¾Mv²
v² = 4gh/3 = 4(9.8)(2)/3 = 26.1
v = 5.11 m/s
(Compare: frictionless slide v=√(2gh) = 6.26 m/s — rolling is slower!)

Ex 5.14 — Torque at Angle

Q: 50 N force at 60° to 0.6 m lever arm. Torque? 
τ = rF sinθ = 0.6(50)(sin60°) = 0.6(50)(0.866) = 25.98 N·m

Ex 5.15 — Angular Velocity from KE

Q: I=3 kg·m², KE_rot=24 J. Angular velocity? 
½Iω² = 24 → ω² = 48/3 = 16 → ω = 4 rad/s

Ex 5.16 — Ladder Equilibrium

Q: 5 m uniform ladder (30 kg) leans at 60° against frictionless wall. Person (70 kg) at 4 m up. Find floor reactions. 
Nᵥᵥₐₗₗ acts horizontally at top.
ΣFₓ: N_wall − f_floor = 0 → f_floor = N_wall
ΣFᵧ: N_floor − 30g − 70g = 0 → N_floor = 980 N

Στ (about base):
N_wall × 5sin60° = 30g × 2.5cos60° + 70g × 4cos60°
N_wall × 4.33 = 30(9.8)(1.25) + 70(9.8)(2.0)
N_wall × 4.33 = 367.5 + 1372 = 1739.5
N_wall = 401.7 N = f_floor

Ex 5.17 — Calculus: Angular Acceleration (AP-C)

Q: τ(t) = 6t² N·m on I=3 kg·m², ω₀=2 rad/s. Find ω(t). 
α(t) = τ/I = 2t²
ω(t) = ω₀ + ∫₀ᵗ 2t² dt = 2 + 2t³/3
ω(3) = 2 + 2(27)/3 = 2 + 18 = 20 rad/s

Ex 5.18 — Wrench Problem

Q: Two wrenches: 0.3 m vs 0.5 m applying same torque. Force ratio F₁/F₂? 
τ = r₁F₁ = r₂F₂
F₁/F₂ = r₂/r₁ = 0.5/0.3 = 5/3
Longer wrench needs less force!

Ex 5.19 — Rotational Kinematic

Q: Disk starts at rest, α=8 rad/s². How many revolutions in 5 s? 
θ = ½αt² = ½(8)(25) = 100 rad
Revolutions = 100/(2π) = 15.9 rev

Ex 5.20 — AP FRQ: Rotating Rod

Uniform rod (M=2 kg, L=1 m) pivoted at one end, released from horizontal. (a) Initial angular acceleration. (b) Speed of free end when vertical. (c) Torque when 60° from vertical. 
(a) τ = Mg(L/2) = 2(9.8)(0.5) = 9.8 N·m
    I = ML²/3 = 2(1)/3 = 0.667 kg·m²
    α = τ/I = 9.8/0.667 = 14.7 rad/s²

(b) Energy: Mg(L/2) = ½Iω² [CM falls L/2]
    9.8 = ½(0.667)ω² → ω = 5.42 rad/s
    v_end = ωL = 5.42 m/s

(c) τ = Mg(L/2)cos60° = 2(9.8)(0.5)(0.5) = 4.9 N·m

PART D: TEST BANK (50 MCQ + 10 FRQ)

MCQ Key: 1-C, 2-B, 3-A, 4-D, 5-B, 6-C, 7-A, 8-D, 9-B, 10-C, 11-A, 12-D, 13-C, 14-B, 15-A, 16-D, 17-C, 18-B, 19-A, 20-C, 21-D, 22-A, 23-B, 24-C, 25-D, 26-A, 27-B, 28-C, 29-D, 30-A, 31-B, 32-C, 33-A, 34-D, 35-B, 36-C, 37-A, 38-D, 39-B, 40-C, 41-A, 42-D, 43-C, 44-B, 45-A, 46-D, 47-C, 48-B, 49-A, 50-C

Key FRQ Formulas:

Torque: τ = rF sinθ
Rotational Newton's 2nd: Στ = Iα
Parallel axis: I = I_cm + Md²
Rolling: v_cm = Rω
KE_roll = ½mv²(1 + I/mR²)
Static equilibrium: ΣF=0, Στ=0

A disk starts at 30 rad/s and decelerates uniformly to rest in 10 s. Number of revolutions: A) 24 rev B) 150 rad/2π=23.9 rev C) 30 rev D) 15 rev → B [θ=½(ω₀+ω)t=½(30)(10)=150 rad; n=150/2π=23.9 rev] 41. Torque = r × F in cross product. Its direction is determined by: A) Law of cosines B) Right-hand rule C) Dot product D) Left-hand rule → B 42. Equal torques act on two objects with I₁=2I₂. Ratio α₁/α₂: A) 2 B) 4 C) 0.5 D) 1 → C [α=τ/I; α₁/α₂=I₂/I₁=1/2] 43. An object is in static equilibrium. The torque about point A is 5 N·m CW. About point B: A) Also 5 N·m CW B) 0 N·m (since ΣF=0 and Στ=0 about ANY point) C) 5 N·m CCW D) Depends on B location → B 44. A solid sphere rolls without slipping down a 30° incline.   1. Find θ at t=3 s:

FULL FRQ SET (Units 05 — 10 Questions)

FRQ 05-1 — Beam Balance Design

A 6 m uniform beam (mass=20 kg) is supported by cables at 1 m from each end. A 50 kg object hangs at 1.5 m from the left cable. (a) Draw a free body diagram of the beam. (b) Find the tension in each cable using torque equilibrium. (c) Verify your answer using force equilibrium. (d) If the right cable is cut, find initial angular acceleration using the left cable as pivot. Answer: 
Setup: Left cable at x=1m; right cable at x=5m; beam CM at x=3m; 50kg load at x=2.5m.

(a) FBD: T_L up at x=1m, T_R up at x=5m, beam weight (196N) down at x=3m, load (490N) down at x=2.5m.

(b) Στ about left cable (x=1m):
T_R×(5−1) = 196×(3−1) + 490×(2.5−1)
4T_R = 196×2 + 490×1.5 = 392 + 735 = 1127
T_R = 281.75 N

(c) ΣFᵧ=0: T_L + T_R = 196 + 490 = 686 N
T_L = 686 − 281.75 = 404.25 N

(d) Right cable cut → only T_L acts at x=1m.
Torques about left end of beam (x=0):
Gravity torques:
τ_gravity = 196×3 + 490×2.5 = 588+1225=1813 N·m (CW, downward)
τ_T_L = 404.25×1 = 404.25 N·m (CCW) [if T_L still holds]
But T_L is now the only support. The beam now rotates.

Net torque about x=0 (end of beam) = T_L(1) − 196(3) − 490(2.5)
= 404.25 − 1813 = −1408.75 N·m (clockwise)

I about end = I_cm + Md² = (1/12)(20)(36) + 20(3²) = 60+180=240 kg·m² (beam)
+ load: 50(2.5²)=312.5 kg·m²
I_total = 552.5 kg·m²
α = τ/I = 1408.75/552.5 = 2.55 rad/s² (clockwise)

FRQ 05-2 — Rotating Disk System

A motor applies a constant torque of 24 N·m to a solid disk (M=4 kg, R=0.5 m) that starts from rest. (a) Calculate the moment of inertia of the disk. (b) Calculate angular acceleration. (c) Find angular velocity after 6 s. (d) Find the rotational KE at t=6 s. (e) Find total angle rotated and number of revolutions. Answer: 
(a) I = ½MR² = ½(4)(0.25) = 0.5 kg·m²

(b) α = τ/I = 24/0.5 = 48 rad/s²

(c) ω = ω₀ + αt = 0 + 48(6) = 288 rad/s

(d) KE = ½Iω² = ½(0.5)(288²) = 0.25(82944) = 20,736 J
    Check with work: W = τ×θ

(e) θ = ½αt² = ½(48)(36) = 864 rad
    Revolutions = 864/(2π) = 137.5 rev
    Check: W = τθ = 24×864 = 20,736 J ✓

FRQ 05-3 — Ladder Problem (Classic Statics)

A 4 m uniform ladder (mass=15 kg) leans against a frictionless wall at angle 60° from floor. A 70 kg person stands 3 m up the ladder. The floor has friction. (a) Draw complete FBD for the ladder. (b) Find normal force from wall. (c) Find normal force from floor. (d) Find minimum static friction coefficient to prevent slipping. Answer: 
(a) FBD: N_wall horizontal at top; N_floor vertical at base; f_floor horizontal at base; W_ladder at center; W_person at 3m up.

(b) Στ about base (x=0):
N_wall × 4sin60° = W_ladder × 2cos60° + W_person × 3cos60°
N_wall × 3.464 = 15(9.8)(1.0) + 70(9.8)(1.5)
N_wall × 3.464 = 147 + 1029 = 1176
N_wall = 339.6 N

(c) N_floor = W_ladder + W_person = 15(9.8)+70(9.8) = 147+686 = 833 N

(d) ΣFₓ = 0: f_floor = N_wall = 339.6 N
μ_min = f/N_floor = 339.6/833 = 0.408

FRQs 05-4 through 05-10 Key Answers:

FRQ 05-4: Rolling sphere down ramp — use energy conservation with rotational KE
  v = √(10gh/7); solid sphere reaches bottom faster than hollow cylinder

FRQ 05-5: Atwood machine with pulley (I≠0) — tension different on each side
  a = (m₁-m₂)g/(m₁+m₂+I/R²); T₁≠T₂ now

FRQ 05-6: Torque from multiple forces on irregular shape — vector approach
  Sum all τ_i = r_i × F_i; use CCW positive convention

FRQ 05-7: Finding center of mass experimentally — hanging method
  CM is always below pivot when hanging; two pivot positions give intersection

FRQ 05-8: Angular kinematics — car wheel acceleration
  ω=v/R; α=a/R; all rotational kinematics equations apply

FRQ 05-9: Stability analysis — tipping condition
  Object tips when CM moves beyond support point; τ_gravity CW > 0

FRQ 05-10: Synthesis — rotating system with multiple objects
  I_total = Σm_iR_i²; apply Στ=Iα; include all contributing masses

Unit 06  ·  AP1/C
Rotating Systems
Angular Momentum · Rolling Motion
L = IωI₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂KE_roll = ½mv²(1+I/mR²)v_cm = Rω

Unit 06: Energy and Momentum of Rotating Systems

AP Physics 1 & C | Georgia Standards

PART A: CONCEPTS

6.1 — Angular Momentum

Angular momentum (L) is to rotation what linear momentum is to translation. For a point mass moving at speed v at perpendicular distance r from an axis: L = mvr = Iω (for circular motion). For an extended rigid body rotating about a fixed axis: L = Iω. The SI unit is kg·m²/s.

The rotational analog of Newton's Second Law connects net external torque to the rate of change of angular momentum: Στ_ext = dL/dt. When the net external torque on a system is zero, L is constant. This is the law of conservation of angular momentum — one of the fundamental conservation laws of nature, on equal footing with conservation of energy and linear momentum.

When the moment of inertia of a system changes (e.g., a skater pulling in her arms), angular velocity must change to keep L constant: I₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂. This explains a spinning figure skater speeding up when pulling in her arms, the dramatic spin-up of a collapsing star forming a neutron star or pulsar, and the gyroscopic stability of spinning tops and bicycle wheels.

ANGULAR MOMENTUM:
  Point mass:   L = mvr sinφ = Iω    [kg·m²/s]
  Rigid body:   L = Iω

ROTATIONAL NEWTON'S 2ND LAW:
  Στ_ext = dL/dt = Iα   (when I is constant)

CONSERVATION (Στ_ext = 0):
  L_i = L_f
  I₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂

Example — skater pulls arms in:
  I decreases → ω increases proportionally
  KE_rot increases (energy from muscular work)

6.2 — Rotational Kinetic Energy and Rolling Motion

A rotating object stores rotational kinetic energy: KE_rot = ½Iω². For an object that simultaneously translates and rotates — like a rolling wheel — the total kinetic energy is the sum of translational KE of the center of mass and rotational KE about the center of mass: KE_total = ½Mv_cm² + ½Iω².

Rolling without slipping is the constraint that links translational and rotational motion: the contact point has zero velocity relative to the surface, giving v_cm = Rω and a_cm = Rα. Using this, the total KE of a rolling object is KE = ½Mv_cm²(1 + I/(MR²)).

When objects of different shapes roll down the same incline from the same height, they do not arrive at the bottom simultaneously. The object with the smallest I/(MR²) ratio concentrates mass nearest to its center, stores less energy in rotation, and thus converts more gravitational PE into translational KE — arriving fastest. Order from fastest to slowest: solid sphere (I/MR² = 2/5) > solid disk (1/2) > hollow sphere (2/3) > thin hoop (1). Notably, this ranking is independent of mass, radius, and the incline angle.

ROTATIONAL KINETIC ENERGY:
  KE_rot = ½Iω²          [J]
  
TOTAL KE (rolling + translating):
  KE = ½Mv_cm² + ½Iω²

ROLLING WITHOUT SLIPPING:
  v_cm = Rω    a_cm = Rα

ROLLING DOWN INCLINE (height h, using energy conservation):
  Mgh = ½Mv_cm²(1 + I/MR²)
  v_cm = √(2gh / (1 + I/MR²))

  Solid sphere (2/5): v_cm = √(10gh/7)  ← fastest
  Solid disk  (1/2):  v_cm = √(4gh/3)
  Hoop        (1):    v_cm = √(gh)       ← slowest

6.3 — Angular Impulse and Rotational Collisions

The rotational analog of the impulse-momentum theorem: angular impulse = τΔt = ΔL = Δ(Iω). When a torque acts briefly (as in a rotational collision), the angular momentum of the system changes by the angular impulse, but if external torques are negligible, angular momentum is conserved.

In rotational collision problems, a moving object strikes a rotating (or initially stationary) system and sticks to it or rebounds. The angular momentum of the moving object about the pivot axis is L = mvd, where d is the perpendicular distance from the pivot to the velocity vector (the impact parameter). After the collision, total angular momentum is conserved: L_before = I_system × ω_after.

ANGULAR IMPULSE:
  τ·Δt = ΔL = Δ(Iω)     [N·m·s = kg·m²/s]

ANGULAR MOMENTUM OF MOVING PARTICLE (about pivot O):
  L = mvd   (d = perpendicular distance from O to line of velocity)

ROTATIONAL COLLISION EXAMPLE (bullet m, speed v, hits rod at d from pivot):
  Before: L = mvd (bullet) + 0 (rod at rest)
  After:  L = (I_rod + md²) × ω_f
  Conserve: mvd = (I_rod + md²) ω_f
  Solve: ω_f = mvd / (I_rod + md²)

PART B: DIAGRAMS

Diagram 6.1 — Angular Momentum Conservation: Skater and Neutron Star

L = Iω = constant when Στ_ext = 0. Decreasing I forces ω to increase proportionally. This explains figure skaters, collapsing stars forming pulsars, and gyroscopes.

FIGURE SKATER:
  Arms OUT:             Arms IN:
  I₁ = large            I₂ = small
  ω₁ = slow             ω₂ = fast
  L₁ = I₁ω₁            L₂ = I₂ω₂ = L₁ (same!)

   ─●─                   ●
  / │ \    L = Iω        │    ω₂ = ω₁(I₁/I₂) >> ω₁
 ●  │  ●               ●
    │                   │
    ●                   ●

COLLAPSING STAR → PULSAR:
  Sun-sized star (R ~ 7×10⁸ m, T ~ 25 days)
  Collapses to neutron star (R ~ 10⁴ m)
  
  I₁/I₂ = (R₁/R₂)² ≈ (7×10⁴)² ≈ 5×10⁹
  ω₂ = ω₁ × 5×10⁹ → T₂ ~ milliseconds! (millisecond pulsar)

Diagram 6.2 — Rolling Without Slipping: Two Types of KE

A rolling object simultaneously translates (KE = ½Mv²_cm) and rotates (KE = ½Iω²). The no-slip condition v_cm = Rω links them. Total KE depends on the object's shape through I.

Rolling disk — no slipping:

     v_cm →      ω (spinning CCW when rolling right)
    ────────●────────  center moves at v_cm
   /        │          │         ↓         │
   \                  /
    ──────────────────── ground
    contact pt: v = 0  ← instantaneous rest (no slip condition)

Top of disk: v = 2v_cm (fastest point)
Center:      v = v_cm
Contact:     v = 0

Total KE = ½Mv²_cm + ½Iω²
         = ½Mv²_cm(1 + I/MR²)   [using ω = v_cm/R]

Solid sphere (2/5):  KE = ½Mv²(7/5)  → v_cm = √(10gh/7)  fastest
Solid disk  (1/2):   KE = ½Mv²(3/2)  → v_cm = √(4gh/3)
Thin hoop   (1):     KE = ½Mv²(2)    → v_cm = √(gh)     slowest

Diagram 6.3 — Rotational Collision: Bullet Strikes Rod

When a moving particle strikes a rotating system, angular momentum about the pivot is conserved (if external torques are negligible during the collision). The particle's angular momentum about the pivot = mvd, where d is the perpendicular distance.

Bullet (mass m, velocity v) strikes rod (mass M, length L) at end, pivoted at top:

BEFORE:                  AFTER:
  ● pivot                  ● pivot
  │                        │╲
  │                        │  ╲ω
  │ rod (at rest)          │    ╲
  ─── ← bullet →v         ─────── ● (rotating together)
  (end of rod, d = L)

Angular momentum about pivot:
  BEFORE: L = mvL  (only bullet contributes)
  AFTER:  L = (I_rod + mL²) ω  = (ML²/3 + mL²) ω

Conservation: mvL = (ML²/3 + mL²) ω
Solve: ω = mvL / (ML²/3 + mL²) = 3mv / (M + 3m)L

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 6.1 — L = Iω

Q: I=2 kg·m², ω=5 rad/s. L? 
L = Iω = 10 kg·m²/s

Ex 6.2 — Conservation of L

Q: Skater: I₁=3 kg·m², ω₁=2 rev/s. Pulls to I₂=1.5 kg·m². ω₂? 
L₁=L₂: 3(2) = 1.5ω₂ → ω₂ = 4 rev/s
KE₁=½(3)(4π²)×4=236 J; KE₂=½(1.5)(16π²)=118... 
Actually: KE₂/KE₁ = I₁/I₂ × (ω₂/ω₁)² = 2 (KE increases)

Ex 6.3 — Rolling KE

Q: 2 kg solid disk (R=0.2 m) rolls at 4 m/s. Total KE? 
I=½mr²=0.04; ω=v/R=20 rad/s
KE = ½(2)(16) + ½(0.04)(400) = 16 + 8 = 24 J
Or: KE = ½mv²(1 + ½) = ¾mv² = ¾(2)(16) = 24 J ✓

Ex 6.4 — Rolling Down Incline

Q: Solid sphere rolls from rest, h=3.5 m. Final v? 
v = √(10gh/7) = √(10×9.8×3.5/7) = √49 = 7 m/s

Ex 6.5 — Rotational Work

Q: Torque 15 N·m rotates object through 8 rad. Work done? 
W = τθ = 15(8) = 120 J

Ex 6.6 — L of Planet

Q: Earth (m=6×10²⁴ kg) orbits at r=1.5×10¹¹ m, v=3×10⁴ m/s. L? 
L = mvr = 6×10²⁴ × 3×10⁴ × 1.5×10¹¹ = 2.7×10⁴⁰ kg·m²/s

Ex 6.7 — Conservation: Star Collapse

Q: Star (I₁=5×10⁴⁰ kg·m²) at T=30 days collapses to I₂=2×10³⁰ kg·m². New period? 
L conserved: I₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂
ω₁ = 2π/T₁ ; ω₂ = I₁ω₁/I₂
T₂ = T₁ × I₂/I₁ = 30 × 2×10³⁰/(5×10⁴⁰) = 1.2×10⁻⁸ days

Ex 6.8 — Which Rolls Fastest?

Q: Sphere, disk, and ring of same mass/radius released on same incline. Rank by speed. 
Speed ∝ √(2gh/(1 + I/mR²))
Sphere: 1/(1+2/5) = 5/7 → fastest
Disk: 1/(1+1/2) = 2/3
Ring: 1/(1+1) = 1/2 → slowest

Ex 6.9 — Power at Angular Speed

Q: Engine torque 200 N·m at 3000 rpm. Power? 
ω = 3000×2π/60 = 314 rad/s
P = τω = 200(314) = 62,800 W = 62.8 kW

Ex 6.10 — Point Mass L

Q: 0.5 kg ball on 1.2 m string swings at 3 m/s. L? 
L = mvr = 0.5(3)(1.2) = 1.8 kg·m²/s

Ex 6.11 — Angular Impulse

Q: Torque 6 N·m acts for 3 s on disk at rest with I=0.9 kg·m². Final ω? 
τΔt = IΔω → 6(3) = 0.9ω → ω = 20 rad/s

Ex 6.12 — Merry-Go-Round + Person

Q: I_mgr=200 kg·m², ω=2 rad/s. Person (60 kg) at r=2 m jumps on. New ω? 
L_i = 200(2) = 400 kg·m²/s
I_person = mr² = 60(4) = 240 kg·m²
L_f = (200+240)ω_f = 440ω_f = 400
ω_f = 0.91 rad/s

Ex 6.13 — KE Change in Conservation

Q: Above problem. Initial KE? Final KE? 
KE_i = ½(200)(4) = 400 J
KE_f = ½(440)(0.91²) = ½(440)(0.828) = 182 J
KE lost = 218 J (to friction/heat as person lands)

Ex 6.14 — Rolling Without Slipping Condition

Q: Cylinder (R=0.1 m) rolls without slipping. v_cm=2 m/s. ω? 
ω = v_cm/R = 2/0.1 = 20 rad/s
v_top = 2v_cm = 4 m/s
v_bottom = 0

Ex 6.15 — Torque Produces L Change

Q: Object (I=0.5 kg·m²) has 10 N·m torque applied 3 s. ΔL? 
ΔL = τΔt = 10(3) = 30 kg·m²/s

Ex 6.16 — Hollow vs Solid Cylinder Race

Q: Hollow (I=mr²) and solid (I=½mr²) cylinders race down 1 m incline. Speed difference? 
v_solid = √(4gh/3) = √(4×9.8/3) = √13.1 = 3.61 m/s
v_hollow = √(gh) = √9.8 = 3.13 m/s
Δv = 0.48 m/s (solid wins)

Ex 6.17 — Rotation with Friction

Q: Disk (I=0.3 kg·m²) at 20 rad/s. Friction torque 1.5 N·m. Time to stop? 
α = −τ/I = −1.5/0.3 = −5 rad/s²
t = −ω₀/α = −20/(−5) = 4 s

Ex 6.18 — Flywheel Energy Storage

Q: Flywheel (I=50 kg·m², ω=300 rad/s). Stored energy? 
KE = ½Iω² = ½(50)(90000) = 2,250,000 J = 2.25 MJ

Ex 6.19 — Precession Concept (AP-C)

Q: Gyroscope (L=10 kg·m²/s) with gravity torque 5 N·m. Precession rate? 
Precession: Ω = τ/L = 5/10 = 0.5 rad/s

Ex 6.20 — AP FRQ: Rotating Rod System

Rod (M=1 kg, L=2 m) horizontal, pivoted at end. Ball (m=0.5 kg) dropped on far end. (a) Initial L of ball just before impact: v=3 m/s downward. (b) After ball sticks, find ω_f. (c) Find KE before and after. 
(a) L_ball = m×v×r = 0.5×3×2 = 3 kg·m²/s (about pivot)

(b) I_rod = ML²/3 = 1(4)/3 = 1.33 kg·m²
    I_ball = mr² = 0.5(4) = 2 kg·m²
    I_total = 3.33 kg·m²
    
    L conserved: 3 = 3.33ω_f → ω_f = 0.90 rad/s

(c) KE_ball_i = ½(0.5)(9) = 2.25 J
    KE_f = ½(3.33)(0.81) = 1.35 J
    KE_lost = 0.90 J

For an ice skater to slow rotation: she should: A) Extend arms (increases I, decreases ω) B) Pull arms in C) Jump up D) Tilt forward → A 19. Rotational KE = ½Iω². If ω doubles: A) KE doubles B) KE quadruples C) KE halves D) KE unchanged → B 20. A hoop (I=MR²) vs. disk (I=½MR²), same M and R, roll from same height. Hoop's speed at bottom: A) Greater than disk B) Less than disk (more I, more KE goes to rotation) C) Same D) Zero → B 21. Flywheel (I=50 kg·m²) at ω=100 rad/s. Stored energy: A) 250,000 J B) 5000 J C) 125,000 J D) 500,000 J → A [½(50)(10000)=250,000] 22. Conservation of angular momentum is analogous to: A) Conservation of work B) Conservation of linear momentum C) Newton's 1st law D) Conservation of energy → B 23. A star collapses; I decreases by factor
  1. Rotation period:
A) Increases 1000× B) Decreases 1000× C) Unchanged D) Increases √1000 → B [I₁ω₁=I₂ω₂; ω increases 1000×; T=2π/ω decreases 1000×] 24. Point on rolling object at top: speed = A) v_cm B) ½v_cm C) 2v_cm D) 0 → C 25. Point on rolling object at bottom: speed = A) v_cm B) 2v_cm C) 0 D) ½v_cm → C 26–50. (Answer key already provided in earlier MCQ Key section) 26-A, 27-B, 28-C, 29-D, 30-A, 31-B, 32-C, 33-D, 34-A, 35-C, 36-B, 37-D, 38-A, 39-C, 40-B, 41-D, 42-A, 43-C, 44-B, 45-D, 46-A, 47-C, 48-B, 49-D, 50-A

FULL FRQ SET (Unit 06 — 10 Questions)

FRQ 06-1 — Figure Skater Angular Momentum

A figure skater (I_extended = 4.8 kg·m², ω_i = 1.5 rev/s) pulls arms to I_tucked = 1.2 kg·m². (a) Find final angular velocity. (b) Find initial and final rotational KE. (c) Where does the extra KE come from? (d) Repeat if she extends arms back — does she return to exactly 1.5 rev/s? Why? Answer: 
(a) L conserved: I₁ω₁ = I₂ω₂
    4.8(1.5) = 1.2ω₂ → ω₂ = 6.0 rev/s

(b) KE₁ = ½I₁ω₁² = ½(4.8)(9π²) = 212.1 J  [using ω=1.5×2π=9.42 rad/s]
    More precisely: ω₁=1.5×2π=9.42 rad/s; KE₁=½(4.8)(88.8)=213 J
    ω₂=6×2π=37.7 rad/s; KE₂=½(1.2)(1421)=852 J

(c) The skater's muscles do work in pulling arms inward against centrifugal effect.
    Extra KE = 852−213 = 639 J (comes from internal muscular energy)

(d) Yes — if muscles extend arms back to exact same I, ω returns to 1.5 rev/s.
    But in practice: friction at ice, air resistance → slight decrease.

FRQ 06-2 — Rolling Object Energy

A solid sphere (M=2 kg, R=0.1 m) rolls from rest down a ramp (height h=0.7 m) onto flat floor. (a) Find speed at bottom of ramp. (b) Find translational and rotational KE at bottom. (c) On flat floor (μ_k=0.05), how far does it roll before stopping? Answer: 
(a) v = √(10gh/7) = √(10×9.8×0.7/7) = √9.8 = 3.13 m/s

(b) KE_trans = ½mv² = ½(2)(9.8) = 9.8 J
    KE_rot = 2/7 × mgh = 2/7 × 2×9.8×0.7 = 3.92 J
    Total = 9.8+3.92 = 13.72 J = mgh = 2×9.8×0.7 ✓

(c) For rolling deceleration: friction decelerates both translation and rotation.
    Net friction torque: τ=fR=μmgR
    Deceleration: a = μg(1+I/mR²)⁻¹ → for sphere: a=5μg/7=5(0.05)(9.8)/7=0.35 m/s²
    Distance: v²=2ad → d=v²/(2a)=9.8/(2×0.35)=14 m

FRQ 06-3 through 06-10 Key Results:

06-3: Merry-go-round + person — L conserved; calculate new ω and KE change
06-4: Atwood with massive pulley — use energy including ½Iω² for pulley
06-5: Gyroscope precession — Ω=τ/L; slower spin → faster precession
06-6: Angular impulse-momentum — τΔt=ΔL; find final ω
06-7: Comparison of rolling shapes — rank by speed using v=√(2gh/(1+k))
06-8: Planet Kepler's 2nd law — equal areas equal times from angular momentum conservation
06-9: Rotating rod catches ball — use conservation of L; rod+ball system
06-10: Nuclear fission angular momentum — initial L=0; fragments must have opposite L
