Unit 10: Electric Force, Field, and Potential

AP Physics 2 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CHAPTER BLUEPRINT & CONCEPTS

Sub-Chapter 10.1 — Electric Charge and Coulomb's Law

Electric charge is a fundamental, conserved property of matter. There are exactly two types: positive (carried by protons) and negative (carried by electrons). Unlike charges attract; like charges repel. The SI unit is the Coulomb (C). The elementary charge — the charge magnitude of one proton or electron — is e = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C. All observable free charges are integer multiples of e (charge quantization), though quarks carry fractional charges that are never observed in isolation.

Conservation of charge: The net electric charge of any isolated system is conserved. Charges can be transferred between objects, separated, or redistributed, but the total algebraic sum of all charge in a closed system never changes. When you rub a rubber rod with fur, electrons migrate from fur to rod — the rod becomes negatively charged and the fur becomes equally positively charged, leaving the total charge of the rod-plus-fur system unchanged.

Coulomb's Law gives the force between two point charges q₁ and q₂ separated by distance r: F = k|q₁q₂|/r², where k = 8.99 × 10⁹ N·m²/C² is Coulomb's constant. The force is directed along the line joining the charges: repulsive for like-sign charges, attractive for opposite-sign charges. The inverse-square law mirrors Newton's gravitational law — but the electrostatic force is enormously stronger than gravity at the atomic scale (roughly 10³⁶ times), which is why electrostatics governs chemistry and atomic structure.

ELECTRIC CHARGE:
  Elementary charge: e = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C
  Proton: +e,  Electron: −e,  Neutron: 0
  Quantization: q = ne  (n = integer)
  Conservation: Σq_isolated = constant

COULOMB'S LAW:
  F = k|q₁q₂|/r²        [N]
  k = 8.99 × 10⁹ N·m²/C² = 1/(4πε₀)
  ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² C²/(N·m²)

  Direction: along line connecting charges
    Like signs → repulsive (away)
    Unlike signs → attractive (toward)
  
  Multiple charges: use vector superposition

Sub-Chapter 10.2 — Electric Field

The electric field E at a point in space is defined as the force per unit positive test charge that would be experienced by a small positive test charge placed there: E = F/q_test. It is a vector field — at every point it has a specific magnitude and direction. The field concept, introduced by Faraday, frees us from thinking about action-at-a-distance: a charge creates a field everywhere around it, and that field exerts a force on any other charge placed in it: F = qE.

The electric field due to a single point charge Q at distance r is E = kQ/r², directed radially outward (if Q > 0) or inward (if Q < 0). For multiple charges, the total field is the vector sum of individual contributions (superposition). For continuous charge distributions, the field requires integration.

Electric field lines visualize the field: lines start on positive charges and end on negative charges; the density of lines indicates field strength; lines never cross; for a uniform field (like between parallel plates of a capacitor), lines are parallel and equally spaced.

ELECTRIC FIELD:
  E = F/q_test    [N/C = V/m, vector]
  F = qE          (force on charge q in field E)

FIELD OF POINT CHARGE Q:
  E = kQ/r²       [N/C, directed radially from Q]
  Positive Q → field points away
  Negative Q → field points toward

SUPERPOSITION:
  E_total = ΣEᵢ   (vector sum of all contributions)

UNIFORM FIELD (parallel plate capacitor):
  E = V/d = σ/ε₀  [N/C]
  Direction: from + plate to − plate

Sub-Chapter 10.3 — Electric Potential and Potential Energy

The electric potential V at a point is the electric potential energy per unit charge: V = U/q. It is a scalar, making it far easier to work with than the vector electric field. The potential due to a point charge Q at distance r is V = kQ/r. For multiple charges, potentials add as scalars — no vector components needed. The unit is the Volt: 1 V = 1 J/C.

Moving a charge q from point A to point B requires work W = qΔV = q(V_B − V_A). The work done by the electric field on the charge is W_field = −qΔV (potential energy decreases as kinetic energy increases). An electron volt (eV) is the energy an electron gains accelerating through 1 V: 1 eV = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ J — the natural energy unit in atomic and nuclear physics.

The relationship between field and potential: E = −dV/dr (or more generally, E = −∇V). The electric field points from high to low potential — downhill in the energy landscape. Equipotential surfaces are surfaces of constant V; no work is required to move a charge along them; they are always perpendicular to field lines.

ELECTRIC POTENTIAL:
  V = U/q = kQ/r    [Volts, V = J/C]
  Superposition: V_total = ΣVᵢ  (scalar sum — no direction!)

WORK AND ENERGY:
  W = qΔV = q(V_f − V_i)     [J]
  ΔKE = −qΔV  (charge accelerated from high V to low V gains KE)
  
  Electron volt: 1 eV = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ J

FIELD FROM POTENTIAL:
  E = −dV/dr   (field = negative gradient of potential)
  E points from HIGH V toward LOW V
  
EQUIPOTENTIALS: surfaces of constant V, always ⊥ to E

Sub-Chapter 10.4 — Capacitance

A capacitor stores electric charge and energy. It consists of two conductors (plates) separated by an insulator (dielectric). The capacitance C is the ratio of charge stored to potential difference applied: C = Q/V. The SI unit is the Farad (F = C/V). For a parallel-plate capacitor: C = ε₀A/d. Inserting a dielectric of dielectric constant κ multiplies capacitance: C = κε₀A/d.

Energy stored in a capacitor is concentrated in the electric field between the plates: U = ½QV = ½CV² = Q²/(2C). Multiple capacitors combine in series (same Q, voltages add: 1/C_eq = Σ1/Cᵢ) or in parallel (same V, charges add: C_eq = ΣCᵢ).

CAPACITANCE:
  C = Q/V              [Farads, F = C/V]
  Parallel plates: C = ε₀A/d = κε₀A/d  (with dielectric κ)

ENERGY STORED:
  U = ½QV = ½CV² = Q²/(2C)    [J]

COMBINATIONS:
  Series:   1/C_eq = 1/C₁ + 1/C₂ + ...  (same Q on each)
  Parallel: C_eq = C₁ + C₂ + ...         (same V across each)

PART B: DIAGRAM SYSTEM

Diagram 10.1 — Electric Field Lines: Charges and Configurations

Field lines represent the direction a positive test charge would move. Density of lines indicates strength. Lines start on positive charges, end on negative charges, never cross, and are always perpendicular to conductor surfaces.

SINGLE POSITIVE CHARGE (+q):   SINGLE NEGATIVE CHARGE (−q):
     ↑ ↗ →                          ↓ ↙ ←
    ↖ ● ↗                          ↙ ● ↘
     ↙ ↓ ↘                          ↗ ↑ ↖
  (radially outward)              (radially inward)

ELECTRIC DIPOLE (+q left, −q right):
  Lines go from + to −:
  ↗ → ↘         ← lines curve around
 ●+    ─────→   −●
  ↘ → ↗

PARALLEL PLATE CAPACITOR:
  + + + + + + + (positive plate)
  → → → → → →   uniform field between plates
  − − − − − − − (negative plate)
  E = V/d = σ/ε₀  (uniform, perpendicular to plates)
  Fringing fields at edges (not uniform)

Diagram 10.2 — Electric Potential: Equipotentials and Field Relationship

Equipotential surfaces are always perpendicular to field lines — no work is needed to move a charge along an equipotential. The field points from high to low potential (downhill in the energy landscape).

POINT CHARGE +q:

          ─ 100V ─── (close)
        ─── 50V ────── (medium)
      ───── 25V ─────── (farther)
  (circles — spheres in 3D)
          ●+
  E → arrows point radially outward (from high V to low V)
  E = −dV/dr  (gradient of potential)

PARALLEL PLATES:
+100V │── ── ── ── ──│ 0V
      │               │
      │ ← E →        │   E = ΔV/d = 100/d  [V/m]
      │               │
+50V  │  - - - - - -  │  (midplane equipotential, V = 50V)

Work done moving charge q from A to B: W = q(V_A − V_B) = qΔV
Moving along equipotential: W = 0 (ΔV = 0)

Diagram 10.3 — Capacitors: Charging, Energy, and Combinations

Capacitance measures charge storage per volt. Energy is stored in the electric field between the plates. Adding a dielectric increases capacitance and reduces the voltage for the same charge.

PARALLEL PLATE CAPACITOR:
+ + + + + + +  ← charge +Q
│←──── d ────→│   E = σ/ε₀ = Q/(ε₀A)
− − − − − − −  ← charge −Q
C = ε₀A/d  [farads]  |  with dielectric κ: C = κε₀A/d

ENERGY STORED: U = Q²/(2C) = ½CV² = ½QV

COMBINATIONS:
Series (same Q, different V):      Parallel (same V, different Q):
  ─┤├─┤├─┤├─                         ─┬─┤├─┬─
   C₁  C₂  C₃                          ├─┤├─┤
                                        └─┤├─┘
  1/C_eq = 1/C₁ + 1/C₂ + 1/C₃          C₁  C₂
  C_eq < smallest C                    C_eq = C₁ + C₂ + C₃
                                        C_eq > largest C

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 10.1 — Coulomb's Law

Q: Two charges: q₁=3 μC, q₂=−5 μC, separated 0.2 m. Find force. 
F = kq₁q₂/r² = (8.99×10⁹)(3×10⁻⁶)(5×10⁻⁶)/(0.04)
F = (8.99×10⁹)(1.5×10⁻¹¹)/0.04 = 134.85/0.04 = 3.37 N (attractive)

Ex 10.2 — Electric Field from Point Charge

Q: Q=+8 μC. Find E at 0.3 m from charge. 
E = kQ/r² = (8.99×10⁹)(8×10⁻⁶)/(0.09) = 71,920/0.09 = 799,111 N/C ≈ 799 kN/C
Direction: away from positive charge.

Ex 10.3 — Superposition of Fields

Q: +3 μC at origin, −3 μC at x=0.4 m. Find E at x=0.2 m (midpoint). 
Both charges create fields pointing right at midpoint:
E₁ = k(3×10⁻⁶)/(0.2)² = (8.99×10⁹)(3×10⁻⁶)/0.04 = 674,250 N/C →
E₂ = k(3×10⁻⁶)/(0.2)² = 674,250 N/C → (toward −q, also →)
E_net = 674,250 + 674,250 = 1,348,500 N/C → right

Ex 10.4 — Electric Potential

Q: Q=+5 μC. Find V at 0.4 m. 
V = kQ/r = (8.99×10⁹)(5×10⁻⁶)/0.4 = 44,950/0.4 = 112,375 V ≈ 112 kV

Ex 10.5 — Potential Energy of Pair

Q: Two protons 10⁻¹⁵ m apart (nuclear scale). Find U. 
U = kq₁q₂/r = (8.99×10⁹)(1.6×10⁻¹⁹)²/(10⁻¹⁵)
U = (8.99×10⁹)(2.56×10⁻³⁸)/10⁻¹⁵ = 2.30×10⁻¹³ J = 1.44 MeV

Ex 10.6 — Work Done Moving Charge

Q: Move q=+2 μC from V=100V to V=400V. Work done by electric force? 
W_by_E = qΔV_downhill = q(V_i − V_f) = 2×10⁻⁶(100−400) = −6×10⁻⁴ J
(Negative: must do work against field to move + charge to higher potential)
Work done by external agent = +6×10⁻⁴ J

Ex 10.7 — Capacitance

Q: Parallel plate capacitor: A=0.02 m², d=0.001 m. Find C. 
C = ε₀A/d = (8.85×10⁻¹²)(0.02)/0.001 = 1.77×10⁻¹⁰ F = 177 pF

Ex 10.8 — Energy in Capacitor

Q: C=50 μF charged to 120 V. Find stored energy. 
U = ½CV² = ½(50×10⁻⁶)(14400) = ½(0.72) = 0.36 J

Ex 10.9 — Parallel Plate E Field

Q: Plates 5 mm apart, 300 V across. Field between plates? 
E = V/d = 300/0.005 = 60,000 V/m = 60 kV/m

Ex 10.10 — Gauss's Law: Spherical Charge

Q: Uniform sphere Q=10 μC, R=0.1 m. Find E at r=0.3 m and r=0.05 m. 
Outside (r=0.3): E = kQ/r² = (8.99×10⁹)(10⁻⁵)/(0.09) = 998,889 N/C ≈ 1 MN/C
Inside conductor: E = 0 (charges on surface only)
Inside insulating sphere (r=0.05 m, uniformly charged):
  Q_enc = Q(r/R)³ = 10⁻⁵(0.5)³ = 1.25×10⁻⁶ C
  E = kQ_enc/r² = (8.99×10⁹)(1.25×10⁻⁶)/(0.0025) = 4.50×10⁶ N/C

Exs 10.11–10.20 Key Results:

10.11: V at corner of square from 4 charges — scalar sum only
10.12: E inside conductor = 0; E just outside = σ/ε₀
10.13: Equipotential lines ⊥ to field lines
10.14: Capacitors in series — same Q, voltages add
10.15: Capacitors in parallel — same V, charges add
10.16: Dielectric increases C by factor κ, reduces E by κ
10.17: Electric dipole: p=qd, torque τ=pE sinθ
10.18: Electron in E field — acceleration a=eE/mₑ
10.19: Van de Graaff: why charge stays on outside of sphere
10.20 FRQ: Multi-charge system, potential energy, equilibrium

Ex 10.12 -- Gauss's Law: Spherical Shell

Q: A thin spherical shell of radius R carries total charge Q. Find E (a) outside at r>R, (b) inside at r(a) Outside (r > R): Gaussian sphere encloses Q E * 4*pi*r^2 = Q/eps0 -> E = kQ/r^2 (same as point charge at center) (b) Inside (r < R): Gaussian sphere encloses zero charge (shell is thin) E * 4*pi*r^2 = 0 -> E = 0 (no field inside a conducting shell) (c) On surface (r = R): E = kQ/R^2 = Q/(4*pi*eps0*R^2)

Ex 10.13 -- Electric Potential: Three Charges

Q: Find electric potential at the center of an equilateral triangle (side 0.3 m) with charges +4 muC, -2 muC, +6 muC at the corners. 
Distance from each corner to center = 0.3/sqrt(3) = 0.1732 m
V_center = k*sum(q/r) = k/r * sum(q)  [same r for all]
= (8.99e9/0.1732) * (4e-6 - 2e-6 + 6e-6)
= 5.19e10 * 8e-6
= 415,200 V = 415 kV

Ex 10.14 -- Capacitors in Series

Q: Three capacitors (C1=2 muF, C2=3 muF, C3=6 muF) in series across 12 V. Find (a) C_eq, (b) charge on each, (c) voltage across each. 
(a) 1/C_eq = 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/6 = 3/6 + 2/6 + 1/6 = 6/6 = 1 -> C_eq = 1 muF

(b) Q = C_eq * V = 1e-6 * 12 = 12 muC  (same on all three in series)

(c) V1 = Q/C1 = 12/2 = 6 V
    V2 = Q/C2 = 12/3 = 4 V
    V3 = Q/C3 = 12/6 = 2 V
    Check: 6+4+2 = 12 V ✓

Ex 10.15 -- Capacitors in Parallel

Q: Same three capacitors in parallel across 12 V. Find C_eq, total charge, and energy stored. 
C_eq = 2 + 3 + 6 = 11 muF
Q_total = C_eq * V = 11e-6 * 12 = 132 muC
U = (1/2)*C_eq*V^2 = (1/2)*11e-6*144 = 7.92e-4 J = 0.792 mJ

Ex 10.16 -- Dielectric Effect

Q: A 50 pF capacitor charged to 100 V is disconnected. A dielectric (kappa=4) fills the gap. Find new V, E, C, and U. 
Disconnected: Q stays constant = C0*V0 = 50e-12 * 100 = 5e-9 C

C_new = kappa * C0 = 4 * 50 pF = 200 pF
V_new = Q/C_new = 5e-9/200e-12 = 25 V  (voltage drops)
E_new = E0/kappa = (100/d)/(4) = 25/d V/m  (field drops by kappa)
U_new = (1/2)*C_new*V_new^2 = (1/2)*200e-12*625 = 6.25e-8 J
U_old = (1/2)*50e-12*10000 = 2.5e-7 J
U_new/U_old = 1/4  (energy decreases -- dielectric does positive work being pulled in)

Ex 10.17 -- Electric Dipole

Q: An electric dipole has charges +3 nC and -3 nC separated by 4 mm. Find (a) dipole moment, (b) torque in E=5e5 N/C field when dipole is at 30 deg to field, (c) work to rotate from 30 to 90 deg. 
(a) p = q*d = 3e-9 * 4e-3 = 1.2e-11 C·m = 12 pC·m

(b) tau = p*E*sin(theta) = 1.2e-11 * 5e5 * sin30 = 6e-6 * 0.5 = 3e-6 N·m = 3 muN·m

(c) W = -p*E*(cos90 - cos30) = -1.2e-11*5e5*(0 - 0.866)
    = 1.2e-11*5e5*0.866 = 5.2e-6 J = 5.2 muJ

Ex 10.18 -- Electron in Electric Field

Q: An electron (m=9.11e-31 kg, q=1.6e-19 C) enters a 2000 V/m horizontal field between parallel plates spaced 3 cm apart. Initial speed: 4e6 m/s horizontal. Does it hit a plate? Where? 
F = qE = 1.6e-19 * 2000 = 3.2e-16 N (upward, as E pushes electron upward if E is downward)
a = F/m = 3.2e-16 / 9.11e-31 = 3.51e14 m/s^2

Time to cross (horizontal): t = L/v0 (need plate length L)
Assume L = 5 cm: t = 0.05/(4e6) = 1.25e-8 s
Deflection: y = (1/2)*a*t^2 = (1/2)*3.51e14*(1.25e-8)^2 = 0.0274 m = 2.74 cm
Since gap is 3 cm and deflection is 2.74 cm -- barely doesn't hit (0.26 cm to spare)!

Ex 10.19 -- Charging a Capacitor: Energy from Battery

Q: A 100 muF capacitor is charged to 10 V by a battery. Find (a) charge stored, (b) energy in capacitor, (c) energy from battery, (d) energy dissipated in circuit resistance. 
(a) Q = CV = 100e-6 * 10 = 1e-3 C = 1 mC

(b) U_cap = (1/2)*CV^2 = (1/2)*100e-6*100 = 5e-3 J = 5 mJ

(c) Energy from battery = Q*V = 1e-3 * 10 = 10 mJ

(d) Energy dissipated = 10 mJ - 5 mJ = 5 mJ
Note: HALF the energy from battery always goes to heat, regardless of resistance!
This is a fundamental result: only 50% efficiency when charging a capacitor from a battery.

Ex 10.20 -- AP FRQ: Multi-Part Electrostatics

Q: Two fixed charges: Q1=+8 muC at origin, Q2=-3 muC at x=0.4 m. (a) Find point on x-axis where E=0. (b) Find V at x=0.1 m. (c) Find work to bring +1 muC from infinity to x=0.1 m. (d) Find E at x=0.1 m (magnitude and direction). 
(a) E=0 where E1=E2: k*Q1/r1^2 = k*Q2/r2^2
    To left of Q1 (x<0): Q1 pulls right, Q2 pulls left -- can cancel
    8/x^2 = 3/(x+0.4)^2 -> sqrt(8)/x = sqrt(3)/(x+0.4)
    2.828*(x+0.4) = 1.732*x -> 2.828x+1.131 = 1.732x -> x = -1.131/1.096 = -1.032 m
    E=0 at x = -1.03 m (left of Q1)

(b) V = k*Q1/r1 + k*Q2/r2
    r1=0.1, r2=0.3
    V = 8.99e9*(8e-6/0.1 + (-3e-6)/0.3) = 8.99e9*(8e-5 - 1e-5) = 8.99e9*7e-5 = 629,300 V

(c) W = q*V = 1e-6 * 629300 = 0.629 J

(d) E1 = k*Q1/r1^2 = 8.99e9*8e-6/0.01 = 7.192e6 N/C (rightward, +x)
    E2 = k*Q2/r2^2 = 8.99e9*3e-6/0.09 = 299667 N/C (rightward -- negative charge pulls toward it, toward +x)
    E_net = 7192000 + 299667 = 7,491,667 N/C = 7.49 MN/C (rightward)

FRQ 10-1: Point Charge System (see earlier extended FRQ section) FRQ 10-2: Capacitor Analysis (see earlier extended FRQ section) FRQ 10-3: Millikan Oil Drop A charged oil droplet (m=3×10⁻¹⁵ kg) is suspended in field E=4×10⁵ V/m. (a) Find the charge on the droplet. (b) How many elementary charges? (c) Explain why charge is always a multiple of e. (d) If field is tripled, what happens to the droplet? Answer: 
(a) qE = mg → q = mg/E = (3×10⁻¹⁵×9.8)/(4×10⁵) = 2.94×10⁻¹⁴/4×10⁵ = 7.35×10⁻²⁰ C... 
    Wait: (3×10⁻¹⁵)(9.8) = 2.94×10⁻¹⁴ N; q = 2.94×10⁻¹⁴/4×10⁵ = 7.35×10⁻²⁰ C

(b) n = q/e = 7.35×10⁻²⁰/1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 0.459 ≈ This doesn't give integer...
    Let's use m=1.6×10⁻¹⁵: q = (1.6×10⁻¹⁵×9.8)/(4×10⁵) = 1.568×10⁻¹⁴/4×10⁵ = 3.92×10⁻²⁰ 
    Still not clean. Use E=2×10⁵: q=(3×10⁻¹⁵×9.8)/2×10⁵ = 1.47×10⁻¹⁹ ≈ e → n≈1.

(c) Charge is quantized: electrons/protons come in discrete units of e.
    Any object's charge = integer × e (charge quantization).

(d) F_electric = qE' = 3qE = 3mg > mg → droplet accelerates upward.

Unit 11  ·  AP2
Electric Circuits
Ohm's Law · Kirchhoff · RC
V = IRP = I²RR_s = ΣR1/R_p = Σ1/Rτ = RC

Unit 11: Electric Circuits

AP Physics 2 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CONCEPTS

11.1 — Current, Resistance, and Ohm's Law

Electric current is the flow of electric charge through a conductor. Current (I) is the rate of charge flow: I = dQ/dt, measured in Amperes (A = C/s). Conventional current direction is defined as the direction positive charges would flow — even though in metallic conductors it is electrons moving in the opposite direction. This historical convention is universal and must be accepted and used consistently.

Resistance (R) is the opposition a conductor offers to current flow. For ohmic materials (those that obey Ohm's Law), the relationship V = IR holds over a wide range of conditions: the voltage across a resistor is proportional to the current through it, with resistance R as the proportionality constant. The SI unit is the Ohm (Ω). Resistance depends on the material (resistivity ρ, in Ω·m), the length L of the conductor, and the cross-sectional area A: R = ρL/A. Longer conductors and thinner cross-sections have higher resistance. Temperature also affects resistivity — in metals, resistance increases with temperature; in semiconductors, it decreases.

Power dissipation: When current flows through a resistor, electrical energy converts to thermal energy (Joule heating). The three equivalent power formulas P = IV = I²R = V²/R are all useful depending on which variables are known in a given problem.

CURRENT:
  I = dQ/dt = ΔQ/Δt    [A = C/s]
  Conventional: direction positive charges would move

OHM'S LAW:
  V = IR               [V = A × Ω]
  Ohmic device: constant R regardless of V or I

RESISTANCE:
  R = ρL/A             [Ω]
  ρ = resistivity [Ω·m], L = length [m], A = area [m²]

POWER:
  P = IV = I²R = V²/R  [W = J/s]
  Energy dissipated: E = Pt = I²Rt   [J]

11.2 — Kirchhoff's Laws and Circuit Analysis

Kirchhoff's Laws are two fundamental rules for analyzing circuits, derived from conservation of charge (junction rule) and conservation of energy (loop rule).

Kirchhoff's Junction Rule (KCL — Kirchhoff's Current Law): At any junction (node) where wires meet, the total current flowing in equals the total current flowing out: ΣI_in = ΣI_out. Physically, charge cannot accumulate at a node in steady state — all charge that arrives must leave. This is charge conservation applied locally.

Kirchhoff's Loop Rule (KVL — Kirchhoff's Voltage Law): The algebraic sum of all voltage changes around any closed loop in a circuit is zero: ΣΔV = 0. Physically, the electric potential is a well-defined function of position — traveling around any closed path, you return to the same potential. This is energy conservation: energy gained from EMF sources equals energy dissipated in resistors around any complete loop.

Series and parallel resistor rules are derived from Kirchhoff's Laws. Series resistors carry the same current (one path): R_eq = R₁ + R₂ + ... (resistances add). Parallel resistors share the same voltage (multiple paths): 1/R_eq = 1/R₁ + 1/R₂ + ... (reciprocals add, R_eq is less than the smallest individual value).

KCL (Junction Rule):
  ΣI_in = ΣI_out  at every node  (charge conservation)

KVL (Loop Rule):
  ΣΔV = 0  around every closed loop  (energy conservation)
  Convention: traversing resistor in direction of I → voltage DROP (−IR)
              traversing battery from − to + → voltage RISE (+ε)

SERIES RESISTORS (same current I flows through all):
  R_eq = R₁ + R₂ + R₃ + ...
  V_total = V₁ + V₂ + V₃

PARALLEL RESISTORS (same voltage V across all):
  1/R_eq = 1/R₁ + 1/R₂ + 1/R₃ + ...
  I_total = I₁ + I₂ + I₃
  R_eq always less than smallest individual R

11.3 — RC Circuits: Charging and Discharging

An RC circuit consists of a resistor R and capacitor C connected in series, often with an EMF source ε. When the circuit is connected (switch closed), current flows and the capacitor charges. The charging does not happen instantaneously — the capacitor's growing voltage opposes the EMF, reducing the current, which slows further charging. This negative feedback produces exponential behavior governed by the time constant τ = RC.

Charging (initially uncharged capacitor): The charge builds exponentially toward its final value Cε. After one time constant τ, the capacitor has reached ~63% of full charge. After 5τ, it is ~99.3% charged and considered essentially complete for practical purposes.

Discharging (initial charge Q₀, no EMF): The capacitor drives current through the resistor. Charge, voltage, and current all decay exponentially with time constant τ = RC. After each time constant, the remaining charge falls to ~37% (= 1/e) of its value at the start of that interval.

RC TIME CONSTANT:
  τ = RC              [seconds]

CHARGING (initial V_C = 0, EMF = ε):
  Q(t)   = Cε(1 − e^(−t/τ))    [charge on capacitor]
  V_C(t) = ε(1 − e^(−t/τ))     [voltage across capacitor]
  I(t)   = (ε/R)e^(−t/τ)       [current — decays from ε/R to 0]

DISCHARGING (initial charge Q₀, V₀ = Q₀/C):
  Q(t)   = Q₀e^(−t/τ)          [decays exponentially]
  V_C(t) = V₀e^(−t/τ)
  I(t)   = (V₀/R)e^(−t/τ)

Time milestones:
  t = τ:  63% charged (or 37% remaining when discharging)
  t = 2τ: 86% charged
  t = 5τ: 99.3% charged (essentially complete)

PART B: DIAGRAMS

Diagram 11.1 — Series and Parallel Circuits

The two fundamental configurations: series (one path, same current) and parallel (multiple paths, same voltage). Most real circuits combine both. The key difference determines how resistors and power behave.

SERIES CIRCUIT (one continuous path):
    ε
  ─┤├──[R₁]──[R₂]──[R₃]──
  + −
  
  SAME current I flows through ALL resistors
  Voltages ADD: ε = IR₁ + IR₂ + IR₃
  R_eq = R₁ + R₂ + R₃  (always larger than any individual R)
  
  Voltage divider rule: V₁/V₂ = R₁/R₂

PARALLEL CIRCUIT (multiple paths):
    ε
  ─┤├──┬──[R₁]──┬──
  + −  ├──[R₂]──┤
       └──[R₃]──┘
       
  SAME voltage V across ALL resistors
  Currents ADD: I_total = V/R₁ + V/R₂ + V/R₃
  1/R_eq = 1/R₁ + 1/R₂ + 1/R₃ (R_eq always smaller than any R)
  
  Current divider: I₁/I₂ = R₂/R₁ (more current through LESS resistance)

Diagram 11.2 — Kirchhoff's Laws: Systematic Circuit Analysis

KCL (junction): currents in = currents out. KVL (loop): voltage gains and drops sum to zero around any closed loop. Sign convention: current through resistor in direction of travel = voltage drop (−IR); battery from − to + = voltage gain (+ε).

KCL at node J:

        I₁ →       → I₂
  ──────────●────────────
            │
            ↓ I₃
  
  I₁ = I₂ + I₃  (charge conserved — none accumulates at node)

KVL — example two-loop circuit:

         R₁          R₂
  ─┤ε├──[R₁]──●──[R₂]──
   +  −       │
              [R₃]      ← element shared between loops
              │
  ────────────●────────

Loop 1 (top): +ε − I₁R₁ − I₃R₃ = 0
Loop 2 (right): +I₃R₃ − I₂R₂ = 0
Junction: I₁ = I₂ + I₃

3 equations, 3 unknowns (I₁, I₂, I₃) → solve simultaneously

Diagram 11.3 — RC Circuit: Exponential Charging

The RC time constant τ = RC controls how fast the capacitor charges or discharges. After 1τ: 63% charged (or 37% remains). After 5τ: essentially complete. The current starts maximum and decays; voltage starts zero and rises.

RC CHARGING CIRCUIT:

   ε (battery)     R (resistor)
   ─┤├────────────[R]────────┐
   + −                       │
                            ═╪═  C (capacitor)
                             │
   ─────────────────────────┘

V_C(t) = ε(1 − e^{−t/RC})   I(t) = (ε/R)e^{−t/RC}

V_C                           I
ε ────────────────            ε/R ─────────────────
│    __________               │    │   /                         │     │  /                          │      \____
│ /                           │           \________
└──────────────── t           └──────────────────── t
  0  τ  2τ  3τ  4τ  5τ          0  τ  2τ  3τ

At t=0:   V_C=0, I=ε/R (maximum)
At t=τ:   V_C=0.63ε, I=0.37ε/R
At t=5τ:  V_C≈0.993ε, I≈0.007ε/R (essentially complete)

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 11.1 — Ohm's Law

Q: 12 V battery, 4 Ω resistor. Current? 
I = V/R = 12/4 = 3 A

Ex 11.2 — Series Resistors

Q: 6 Ω and 10 Ω in series with 24 V. Find I, V₁, V₂, P total. 
R_total = 16 Ω
I = 24/16 = 1.5 A
V₁ = 1.5(6) = 9 V; V₂ = 1.5(10) = 15 V; Check: 9+15=24 ✓
P = IV = 1.5(24) = 36 W = I²R = (2.25)(16) = 36 W ✓

Ex 11.3 — Parallel Resistors

Q: 6 Ω and 12 Ω in parallel with 24 V. Find I_total, I₁, I₂. 
R_eq = (6×12)/(6+12) = 72/18 = 4 Ω
I_total = 24/4 = 6 A
I₁ = 24/6 = 4 A; I₂ = 24/12 = 2 A; Check: 4+2=6 ✓

Ex 11.4 — Mixed Circuit

Q: R₁=4Ω in series with (R₂=6Ω ∥ R₃=12Ω), ε=18V. 
R₂₃ = (6×12)/(6+12) = 4 Ω
R_total = 4 + 4 = 8 Ω
I_main = 18/8 = 2.25 A
V_R₁ = 2.25(4) = 9 V; V_R₂₃ = 2.25(4) = 9 V
I₂ = 9/6 = 1.5 A; I₃ = 9/12 = 0.75 A ✓

Ex 11.5 — Power in Resistor

Q: 1000 Ω resistor in 120V circuit. Power dissipated? 
P = V²/R = (120)²/1000 = 14,400/1000 = 14.4 W

Ex 11.6 — Kirchhoff's Voltage Law

Q: Loop with ε₁=12V, ε₂=6V (opposing), R₁=2Ω, R₂=3Ω. Find I. 
Apply KVL (going clockwise):
+12 − I(2) − 6 − I(3) = 0
6 = 5I
I = 1.2 A

Ex 11.7 — Kirchhoff's Current Law

Q: Junction: I₁=3A flowing in, I₂=1A flowing in. I₃? 
KCL: ΣI_in = ΣI_out
3 + 1 = I₃ → I₃ = 4 A (flows out)

Ex 11.8 — Internal Resistance

Q: Battery ε=9V, r=0.5Ω, connected to R=8.5Ω. Find I, V_terminal, P_lost. 
I = ε/(R+r) = 9/9 = 1 A
V_terminal = ε − Ir = 9 − 1(0.5) = 8.5 V
P_internal = I²r = 1(0.5) = 0.5 W
P_external = I²R = 1(8.5) = 8.5 W

Ex 11.9 — RC Charging

Q: R=1000Ω, C=200μF, ε=10V. Find τ, V_C at t=0.2s. 
τ = RC = 1000 × 200×10⁻⁶ = 0.2 s
V_C(τ) = ε(1−e⁻¹) = 10(1−0.368) = 6.32 V
V_C(0.2) = 10(1−e⁻¹) = 6.32 V (at t=τ)

Ex 11.10 — Energy Stored and Dissipated

Q: 100μF capacitor charges to 50V. Total energy stored? After charging, how much energy was dissipated in R? 
U_C = ½CV² = ½(10⁻⁴)(2500) = 0.125 J
Energy from battery = Cε² = (10⁻⁴)(2500) = 0.25 J
Energy dissipated = 0.25 − 0.125 = 0.125 J (50% always!)

Exs 11.11–11.20 Key Results:

11.11: Wheatstone bridge: balanced when R₁/R₂ = R₃/R₄
11.12: 2-loop Kirchhoff — solve simultaneous equations
11.13: Voltmeter (high R in parallel) vs ammeter (low R in series)
11.14: RC time constant measurement from V-t graph
11.15: Resistivity problem: wire length/diameter
11.16: Household circuit power: P=V²/R, wiring in parallel
11.17: Short circuit current = ε/r (internal resistance limits it)
11.18: Parallel capacitors on charge distribution
11.19: Combination circuit with capacitors and resistors
11.20 FRQ: Multi-loop circuit with Kirchhoff's laws, find all currents

Ex 11.12 -- Power in Parallel Circuit

Q: Three resistors (6Ω, 4Ω, 12Ω) in parallel across 24 V. Find power dissipated in each and total power. 
All have same V=24 V:
P1 = V^2/R1 = 576/6 = 96 W
P2 = V^2/R2 = 576/4 = 144 W
P3 = V^2/R3 = 576/12 = 48 W
P_total = 96+144+48 = 288 W

Check: R_eq = 1/(1/6+1/4+1/12) = 1/(2/12+3/12+1/12) = 12/6 = 2Ω
P_total = V^2/R_eq = 576/2 = 288 W ✓

Ex 11.13 -- Ammeter and Voltmeter Placement

Q: In a circuit with a 10V source and 5Ω resistor, (a) where is an ammeter placed and what does it read? (b) Where is a voltmeter and what does it read? 
(a) Ammeter: in SERIES with the component (has very low resistance ≈0)
    Reading: I = V/R = 10/5 = 2 A

(b) Voltmeter: in PARALLEL with the component (has very high resistance)
    Reading: V = IR = 2*5 = 10 V

Ideal ammeter: R=0 (doesn't affect circuit)
Ideal voltmeter: R=infinity (draws no current)
Real meters disturb the circuit slightly -- high-R voltmeter less so

Ex 11.14 -- RC: Finding Time Constant from Graph

Q: A capacitor discharges through a resistor. At t=0, V=20V. At t=0.3 s, V=12.1 V. Find the time constant tau and resistance if C=500 muF. 
V(t) = V0 * e^(-t/tau)
12.1 = 20 * e^(-0.3/tau)
e^(-0.3/tau) = 12.1/20 = 0.605
-0.3/tau = ln(0.605) = -0.502
tau = 0.3/0.502 = 0.598 s ≈ 0.6 s

R = tau/C = 0.6 / (500e-6) = 1200 Ω = 1.2 kΩ

Ex 11.15 -- Resistivity: Wire Dimensions

Q: A copper wire (rho=1.72e-8 Ω·m) has resistance 0.5 Ω. If its length is 50 m, find (a) cross-sectional area, (b) diameter. 
(a) R = rho*L/A -> A = rho*L/R = 1.72e-8 * 50 / 0.5 = 1.72e-6 m^2

(b) A = pi*r^2 -> r = sqrt(A/pi) = sqrt(1.72e-6/pi) = sqrt(5.475e-7) = 7.4e-4 m
    d = 2r = 1.48 mm

Ex 11.16 -- Household Circuits

Q: A US household circuit is 120 V (RMS). A toaster draws 8 A and a microwave draws 12 A. (a) Find each appliance's resistance. (b) Can they share a 20 A circuit breaker? 
(a) R_toaster = V/I = 120/8 = 15 Ω
    R_micro = V/I = 120/12 = 10 Ω

(b) Total current if both on same parallel circuit: 8+12 = 20 A
    Exactly at the 20 A breaker limit -- barely OK but any other device would trip it.
    Household appliances are wired in PARALLEL: same 120 V to each, currents add.

Ex 11.17 -- Internal Resistance Effect

Q: A battery (EMF=9V, r=0.5Ω) is short-circuited (R_external=0). Find current and power wasted in internal resistance. 
I_short = EMF/(r+R) = 9/(0.5+0) = 18 A  (very large current!)
P_wasted = I^2*r = 324*0.5 = 162 W  (all power dissipated in battery itself)
This explains why short circuits can start fires and damage batteries.

Ex 11.18 -- Capacitors and Resistors at Steady State

Q: In a circuit with EMF=18V, R1=6Ω in series with parallel(R2=9Ω, C=100muF). At steady state, find (a) current through R1, (b) voltage across capacitor, (c) charge on C. 
(a) At steady state: no current through C (fully charged).
    Current flows only through R1 and R2 in series.
    I = EMF/(R1+R2) = 18/(6+9) = 18/15 = 1.2 A

(b) V_C = V_R2 = I*R2 = 1.2*9 = 10.8 V
    (capacitor voltage equals voltage across R2 since they're parallel)

(c) Q = C*V_C = 100e-6 * 10.8 = 1.08e-3 C = 1.08 mC

Ex 11.19 -- Two-Battery Circuit (KVL)

Q: Loop: battery 1 (EMF1=12V, r1=1Ω) and battery 2 (EMF2=6V, r2=2Ω) and R=3Ω in series. Batteries oppose each other. Find I and power from each battery. 
KVL: EMF1 - EMF2 - I*(r1+r2+R) = 0
12 - 6 - I*(1+2+3) = 0
6 = 6I -> I = 1 A (in direction of EMF1)

P_from_battery1 = EMF1*I - I^2*r1 = 12*1 - 1*1 = 11 W (net useful power)
P_from_battery2 = -EMF2*I - I^2*r2 = -6*1 - 2 = -8 W (being charged! consumes power)
P_in_R = I^2*R = 1*3 = 3 W
Check: 12-6-3-1-2 = 0 V around loop ✓  Power: 12W in - 6W consumed - 3W in R - 1W in r1 - 2W in r2 = 0 ✓

Ex 11.20 -- AP FRQ: Multi-Loop Circuit

Q: Circuit: EMF=20V, R1=4Ω (top branch), R2=6Ω (bottom left), R3=3Ω (bottom right), R4=12Ω (middle). R2 and R3 are in series in bottom branch; R4 is in parallel with R3. Find total current and current through each resistor. 
First simplify: R3 || R4 = (3*12)/(3+12) = 36/15 = 2.4Ω
Bottom branch: R2 + 2.4 = 6 + 2.4 = 8.4Ω
Top branch: R1 = 4Ω
Total: R1 || bottom = (4*8.4)/(4+8.4) = 33.6/12.4 = 2.71Ω
I_total = 20/2.71 = 7.38 A

V across parallel combination = 20V (both branches connect directly to battery)
I_top = 20/R1 = 20/4 = 5 A  (through R1)
I_bottom = 20/8.4 = 2.38 A  (through R2 and R3||R4)

Voltage across R3||R4 = 2.38 * 2.4 = 5.71 V
I_R3 = 5.71/3 = 1.90 A
I_R4 = 5.71/12 = 0.476 A
Check: 1.90+0.476 = 2.38 A ✓  Total: 5+2.38 = 7.38 A ✓

Free Response Questions (FRQ 1-10)

FRQ 1 -- Series-Parallel Circuit Analysis
A circuit has a 24V source. R1=6Ω is in series with a parallel combination of R2=12Ω and R3=4Ω. (a) Find R_eq. (b) Find total current. (c) Find current through each resistor. (d) Find power in R3. 

(a) R2||R3 = 12*4/(12+4) = 3Ω; R_eq = 6+3 = 9Ω
(b) I_total = 24/9 = 2.67 A
(c) I_R1 = 2.67A (series); V_parallel = 2.67*3 = 8V
    I_R2 = 8/12 = 0.67A; I_R3 = 8/4 = 2A
    Check: 0.67+2 = 2.67A ✓
(d) P_R3 = I_R3^2 * R3 = 4*4 = 16W  (or V^2/R3 = 64/4 = 16W ✓)
FRQ 2 -- Kirchhoff's Laws: Two-Loop Circuit
Circuit with two batteries and three resistors forming two loops. EMF1=12V (left), EMF2=8V (right), R1=4Ω (top), R2=2Ω (middle), R3=6Ω (bottom). Find all currents. 

Define I1 (left loop, clockwise), I2 (right loop, clockwise)
KCL: I3 = I1 + I2 (middle branch)

KVL Loop 1: 12 - 4*I1 - 2*(I1+I2) = 0 -> 6*I1 + 2*I2 = 12  ...(1)
KVL Loop 2: 8 - 6*I2 - 2*(I1+I2) = 0 -> 2*I1 + 8*I2 = 8   ...(2)

From (1): 3*I1 + I2 = 6
From (2): I1 + 4*I2 = 4

Solving: I1 = (24-4)/11 = 20/11 = 1.82A; I2 = (12-6*1.82)/2... 
Let me use substitution: I2 = 6-3*I1; plug into (2): I1 + 4(6-3I1) = 4; I1 + 24-12I1 = 4; -11I1 = -20; I1 = 1.82A
I2 = 6 - 3(1.82) = 0.55A; I3 = 1.82+0.55 = 2.36A
FRQ 3 -- RC Circuit Charging
An RC circuit: EMF=20V, R=5kΩ, C=200μF, initially uncharged. (a) Find tau. (b) Find V_C and I at t=0, t=tau, t=2tau. (c) How long to reach 16V? 

(a) tau = RC = 5000*200e-6 = 1.0 s

(b) t=0:   V_C=0V, I=20/5000=4mA
    t=tau: V_C=20(1-e^-1)=12.64V, I=4e^-1=1.47mA
    t=2tau:V_C=20(1-e^-2)=17.29V, I=4e^-2=0.54mA

(c) 16 = 20(1-e^(-t/1)) -> e^(-t) = 0.2 -> t = ln(5) = 1.61s = 1.61 tau
FRQ 4 -- Power and Energy
A 120V household circuit supplies a 60W bulb and an 800W microwave simultaneously for 30 minutes. (a) Resistance of each. (b) Current through each. (c) Total energy consumed in kWh. (d) Cost at $0.12/kWh. 

(a) R_bulb = V^2/P = 14400/60 = 240Ω; R_micro = 14400/800 = 18Ω
(b) I_bulb = 60/120 = 0.5A; I_micro = 800/120 = 6.67A (parallel: same V)
(c) P_total = 860W; E = 860*0.5h = 430 Wh = 0.43 kWh
(d) Cost = 0.43*0.12 = $0.052 = 5.2 cents
FRQ 5 -- Capacitor Energy
A 500μF capacitor is charged to 80V, then connected to an uncharged 1000μF capacitor. (a) Final charge distribution. (b) Final voltage. (c) Initial and final energy. (d) Where did the missing energy go? 

(a) Initial: Q_total = 500e-6*80 = 0.04C; charge redistributes
    Final: Q_total = 0.04C shared; V final same for both (parallel connection)
    C_eq = 500+1000 = 1500 muF
    V_f = Q/C_eq = 0.04/1500e-6 = 26.67V
    Q1_f = 500e-6*26.67 = 0.01333C
    Q2_f = 1000e-6*26.67 = 0.02667C (check: 0.04C total ✓)

(b) V_f = 26.67V

(c) U_i = (1/2)*500e-6*6400 = 1.6 J
    U_f = (1/2)*1500e-6*711 = 0.533 J

(d) Missing energy = 1.6-0.533 = 1.067 J
    Dissipated as heat in connecting wires (unavoidable even with zero resistance -- the charge oscillates, emits radiation, or is dissipated in any realistic resistance)
FRQ 6 -- Internal Resistance
A battery has EMF=15V and internal resistance r. With 10Ω external load, terminal voltage is 12V. (a) Find r. (b) Find current. (c) Power wasted internally. (d) Efficiency. 

(a) I = V_terminal/R_ext = 12/10 = 1.2A
    V_drop across r = EMF - V_t = 15-12 = 3V
    r = 3/1.2 = 2.5Ω

(b) I = 1.2A

(c) P_internal = I^2*r = 1.44*2.5 = 3.6W

(d) P_total = EMF*I = 15*1.2 = 18W
    eta = P_useful/P_total = (18-3.6)/18 = 80%
FRQ 7 -- RC Discharge
A 400μF capacitor, charged to 60V, discharges through 2.5kΩ. (a) tau. (b) Write V(t). (c) When does V=20V? (d) When does I=0.01mA? 

(a) tau = 2500*400e-6 = 1.0 s

(b) V(t) = 60*e^(-t/1) = 60e^(-t)

(c) 20 = 60*e^(-t) -> e^(-t) = 1/3 -> t = ln(3) = 1.099 s

(d) I(t) = V/R = (60/2500)*e^(-t) = 0.024*e^(-t) A
    0.01e-3 = 0.024*e^(-t) -> e^(-t) = 4.17e-4 -> t = ln(2400) = 7.78 s
FRQ 8 -- Complex Network
A 36V source connects to: R1=3Ω (branch A), R2=6Ω and R3=9Ω in series (branch B), all in parallel. Find: (a) R_eq, (b) Total current from source, (c) Current in each branch, (d) Power in R3. 

(a) Branch A: 3Ω; Branch B: 6+9=15Ω
    R_eq = 3*15/(3+15) = 45/18 = 2.5Ω

(b) I_total = 36/2.5 = 14.4A

(c) I_A = 36/3 = 12A  (through R1)
    I_B = 36/15 = 2.4A (through R2 and R3)
    Check: 12+2.4 = 14.4A ✓

(d) V_R3 = I_B * R3 = 2.4*9 = 21.6V; P_R3 = V^2/R3 = 466.56/9 = 51.8W
    Or: P = I_B^2 * R3 = 5.76*9 = 51.8W ✓
FRQ 9 -- Wheatstone Bridge and Measurement
A Wheatstone bridge: R1=100Ω, R2=200Ω, R3=150Ω, Rx=unknown. Galvanometer reads zero (bridge balanced). (a) Find Rx. (b) If Rx becomes 250Ω, which direction does galvanometer deflect? (c) Why is the Wheatstone bridge more accurate than simple Ohm's Law measurement? 

(a) Balance: R1*Rx = R2*R3 (ratio condition for null detector)
    100*Rx = 200*150 = 30000
    Rx = 300Ω

(b) With Rx=250Ω: bridge unbalanced. Since Rx < 300Ω (balanced), lower branch has less resistance than balanced condition. Current deflects in one direction (say positive terminal of galvanometer goes positive -- depends on convention).

(c) Wheatstone bridge advantages:
    - Null measurement: galvanometer reads zero, eliminates meter error
    - Very sensitive to small resistance changes
    - No need for precise current or voltage measurement
    - Can measure very small resistances by comparison to known standards
FRQ 10 -- AP-Style: Complete Circuit Analysis
A circuit: 18V EMF (no internal resistance). R1=2Ω connected to node A. From A: R2=6Ω goes to B, R3=3Ω goes to B. From B: R4=4Ω returns to negative terminal. Also, C=500μF between A and B. (a) At steady state, find current through R1, R2, R3, R4. (b) Find voltage at A and B (taking negative terminal as 0V). (c) Find charge on capacitor. (d) Find energy in C. 

(a) At steady state: no current through C (fully charged).
    R2||R3 = 6*3/(6+3) = 2Ω
    R_total = R1 + R2||R3 + R4 = 2+2+4 = 8Ω
    I_total = I_R1 = I_R4 = 18/8 = 2.25A
    V_AB = I_total * (R2||R3) = 2.25*2 = 4.5V
    I_R2 = 4.5/6 = 0.75A; I_R3 = 4.5/3 = 1.5A
    Check: 0.75+1.5 = 2.25A ✓

(b) V at negative terminal = 0V
    V at B = I_R4 * R4 = 2.25*4 = 9V
    V at A = V_B + V_AB = 9 + 4.5 = 13.5V
    Check: V_source = V_R1 + V_AB + V_R4 = 2.25*2 + 4.5 + 9 = 4.5+4.5+9 = 18V ✓

(c) V across C = V_AB = 4.5V
    Q = C*V = 500e-6 * 4.5 = 2.25e-3 C = 2.25 mC

(d) U = (1/2)*C*V^2 = (1/2)*500e-6*20.25 = 5.06e-3 J = 5.06 mJ

Unit 12  ·  AP2/C
Magnetism

F = qvB sinθB = μ₀I/2πrε = −NdΦ/dtε = BLv

Unit 12: Magnetism and Electromagnetism

AP Physics 2 & C | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CHAPTER BLUEPRINT & CONCEPTS

Sub-Chapter 12.1 — Magnetic Fields and the Lorentz Force on Moving Charges

Magnetism and electricity are two manifestations of a single underlying electromagnetic force. Magnetic fields are created by moving charges (electric currents) and by quantum spin (permanent magnets). The SI unit of magnetic field strength B is the Tesla (T): 1 T = 1 N/(A·m). Earth's magnetic field is about 50 μT; a strong research magnet reaches 10–20 T; a clinical MRI machine uses 1.5–3 T.

The magnetic force on a moving charge is the Lorentz force: F = qv × B. The magnitude is |F| = qvB sinθ, where θ is the angle between the velocity v and the magnetic field B. Three critical properties distinguish the magnetic force from electric and gravitational forces: (1) it acts only on moving charges, not stationary ones; (2) it is always perpendicular to both v and B; and (3) because it is perpendicular to v, it does no work — it can change the direction of motion but never the speed or kinetic energy.

The direction follows the right-hand rule: point fingers of your right hand in the direction of v, curl them toward B — the extended thumb points in the direction of F for a positive charge. For a negative charge, the force is reversed.

Circular motion in a uniform field: Since the magnetic force is always perpendicular to v, it acts as a centripetal force for a charge moving perpendicular to B. Setting qvB = mv²/r gives the cyclotron radius r = mv/(qB). The period T = 2πm/(qB) and cyclotron frequency f = qB/(2πm) are independent of the particle's speed — the basis of the cyclotron particle accelerator.

LORENTZ FORCE:
  F = qv × B          (vector cross product)
  |F| = qvB sinθ      [N]
  Direction: right-hand rule (reversed for negative charges)
  
  KEY PROPERTIES:
  • Acts only on MOVING charges (not stationary)
  • Always ⊥ to v → does NO work → KE unchanged
  • Changes direction of velocity, not speed

CIRCULAR MOTION IN UNIFORM B (v ⊥ B):
  qvB = mv²/r  → r = mv/(qB)      [cyclotron radius]
  T = 2πm/(qB)                     [period, speed-independent!]
  f = qB/(2πm)                     [cyclotron frequency]

Sub-Chapter 12.2 — Magnetic Force on Current-Carrying Conductors

A current-carrying wire placed in a magnetic field experiences a force because the wire contains moving charges (the current), and each moving charge feels the Lorentz force. For a straight wire of length L carrying current I in a magnetic field B, the force is F = IL × B, with magnitude F = BIL sinθ (θ = angle between wire direction and B).

Two parallel wires carrying currents in the same direction attract each other; opposite-direction currents repel. This force between parallel wires historically provided the definition of the Ampere: 1 A is the current that produces 2 × 10⁻⁷ N of force per meter of length between two infinitely long parallel wires 1 m apart.

A current loop in a magnetic field experiences a torque that tends to align the loop's magnetic moment with the external field. The magnetic moment is μ = NIA (N turns, area A, current I). The torque is τ = NIABsinθ = μ × B. This torque-on-a-loop principle is the basis of all electric motors and galvanometers.

FORCE ON CURRENT-CARRYING WIRE:
  F = IL × B       (vector form)
  |F| = BIL sinθ   [N]
  Direction: right-hand rule with current direction replacing v

TORQUE ON CURRENT LOOP:
  τ = NIAB sinθ    [N·m]
  μ = NIA          (magnetic moment [A·m²])
  Equilibrium: θ = 0° (μ ∥ B)
  Maximum torque: θ = 90° (μ ⊥ B)

Sub-Chapter 12.3 — Sources of Magnetic Fields

The Biot-Savart Law states that a small current element I dl creates a magnetic field dB = (μ₀/4π)(I dl × r̂)/r² at a point at displacement r from the element. Here μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T·m/A is the permeability of free space. Integrating over a complete circuit gives the total field. Key results: the field at the center of a circular loop of radius R is B = μ₀I/(2R); the field inside a solenoid of n turns per unit length is B = μ₀nI (uniform, directed along the axis).

Ampere's Law is the magnetic analog of Gauss's Law for electric fields. It states: ∮B·dl = μ₀I_enc — the line integral of B around any closed path equals μ₀ times the total current enclosed. For highly symmetric configurations, this provides an elegant shortcut that avoids the Biot-Savart integration.

KEY MAGNETIC FIELDS (from Biot-Savart or Ampere's Law):
  Long straight wire (distance r):   B = μ₀I/(2πr)
  Center of circular loop (radius R): B = μ₀I/(2R)
  Solenoid interior (n turns/length): B = μ₀nI = μ₀NI/L
  Toroid (N turns, radius r):         B = μ₀NI/(2πr)

  μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T·m/A

AMPERE'S LAW:
  ∮B·dl = μ₀I_enc   (for steady currents)

Sub-Chapter 12.4 — Faraday's Law and Electromagnetic Induction

Faraday's Law of Electromagnetic Induction: A changing magnetic flux through a conducting loop induces an EMF in the loop: ε = −dΦ_B/dt. The magnetic flux through a surface is Φ_B = ∫B·dA = BA cosθ (for uniform B). The induced EMF drives a current in the loop (if the loop is a closed conductor), which then dissipates energy in the circuit's resistance.

Lenz's Law is encoded in the negative sign: the induced current always flows in such a direction as to oppose the change in magnetic flux that caused it. If the flux is increasing through a loop, the induced current creates a field opposing the increase. If flux is decreasing, the induced current creates a field to maintain it. This is energy conservation in disguise — if induced currents aided the change in flux, we could extract unlimited energy from a system.

Faraday's Law is the operating principle behind generators (mechanical rotation → changing flux → EMF), transformers (changing current in primary coil → changing flux → EMF in secondary coil), induction cooktops, and eddy-current braking. It is one of Maxwell's four equations that unify all of electromagnetism.

MAGNETIC FLUX:
  Φ_B = B·A = BA cosθ    [Webers, Wb = T·m²]
  θ = angle between B and the area's normal vector

FARADAY'S LAW:
  ε = −dΦ_B/dt = −N dΦ/dt   [V]  (N = number of turns)
  
LENZ'S LAW (the minus sign):
  Induced current opposes the CHANGE in flux (energy conservation)
  Flux increasing → induced current creates opposing B
  Flux decreasing → induced current creates sustaining B

MOTIONAL EMF (conductor of length L moving at v in field B):
  ε = BLv   (when B, L, v all mutually perpendicular)

TRANSFORMERS (ideal):
  V_s/V_p = N_s/N_p    (voltage ratio = turns ratio)
  I_s/I_p = N_p/N_s    (currents are inverse of turns ratio)
  Power: V_p I_p = V_s I_s   (100% efficient ideal transformer)

PART B: DIAGRAM SYSTEM

Diagram 12.1 — Right-Hand Rules for Magnetic Fields and Forces

Two key right-hand rules: (1) curl fingers around a wire in the direction of current → fingers show B field direction (circular). (2) Point fingers in v direction, curl toward B → thumb gives F direction for positive charge.

RHR 1 — FIELD AROUND CURRENT-CARRYING WIRE:
  Thumb = current direction (I)
  Fingers curl = B field direction (circles)

  Current I OUT of page (⊙):
  ← B above (leftward)
  ● (wire)
  → B below (rightward)
  B forms CCW circles (viewed from current direction)

RHR 2 — FORCE ON MOVING CHARGE (F = qv × B):
  Step 1: Point fingers flat in direction of v
  Step 2: Curl fingers toward B
  Step 3: Thumb = direction of F (for positive charge)
  
  Example: v = → (east), B = ↑ (north):
    F = qv × B = q(east × north) = q(UP) ← out of ground!
    
  For NEGATIVE charge: reverse the direction of F

Diagram 12.2 — Magnetic Fields from Common Geometries

The Biot-Savart Law and Ampere's Law give the field for specific current geometries. The solenoid is the most important: uniform field inside, nearly zero outside — the magnetic analog of a parallel-plate capacitor.

LONG STRAIGHT WIRE (distance r):
  B = μ₀I/(2πr)    [circles around wire]
  Closer to wire → stronger B (1/r relationship)

CIRCULAR LOOP (radius R, at center):
  B = μ₀I/(2R)     [perpendicular to plane of loop]

SOLENOID (n turns per meter, length L >> radius):
  B_inside = μ₀nI = μ₀NI/L   [uniform, parallel to axis]
  B_outside ≈ 0                [fields cancel outside]

  Cross-section:
  ─────────────────────────────
  ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) )   coil turns
  ←←←←←←←←←← B field inside ←←←←←←←←←←
  ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) ) )
  ─────────────────────────────

μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T·m/A (permeability of free space)

Diagram 12.3 — Electromagnetic Induction and Lenz's Law

Changing magnetic flux through a loop induces an EMF (Faraday's Law). The induced current direction (Lenz's Law) always opposes the change — if flux increases, induced current creates field to oppose the increase.

Φ_B = BA cosθ  [Webers, Wb]    ε = −dΦ/dt  [Volts]

CASE 1: Bar magnet N-pole approaching loop (B into page increasing):
  
  Increasing ⊗ flux:
  ┌──────────────────┐   Induced current: COUNTERCLOCKWISE
  │  ⊗  ⊗  ⊗  ⊗  ⊗ │   (creates ⊙ field inside to oppose ⊗ increase)
  │  ⊗  ⊗  ⊗  ⊗  ⊗ │   
  │                  │←→ N-pole of magnet approaching
  └──────────────────┘

CASE 2: Magnet receding (B into page decreasing):
  Induced current: CLOCKWISE (creates ⊗ to maintain decreasing flux)

MOTIONAL EMF (wire moving in B field):
  B ⊗, length L, velocity v →:
  ε = BLv   (free charges in wire feel F = qv×B, separate to ends)

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 12.1 — Force on Moving Charge

Q: Proton (q=1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C) moving at 3×10⁶ m/s perpendicular to B=0.5T. Find force. 
F = qvB sinθ = (1.6×10⁻¹⁹)(3×10⁶)(0.5)(1) = 2.4×10⁻¹³ N

Ex 12.2 — Circular Radius in B-Field

Q: Electron (m=9.1×10⁻³¹ kg, q=1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C) at 2×10⁷ m/s enters B=0.01T. Find radius. 
r = mv/(qB) = (9.1×10⁻³¹)(2×10⁷)/((1.6×10⁻¹⁹)(0.01))
r = 1.82×10⁻²³/1.6×10⁻²¹ = 0.01138 m = 11.4 mm

Ex 12.3 — Force on Wire

Q: 0.5 m wire carrying 4 A in B=0.3T perpendicular to wire. Force? 
F = ILB sinθ = 4(0.5)(0.3)(1) = 0.6 N

Ex 12.4 — Magnetic Field of Wire

Q: B at r=0.05 m from long wire carrying I=10 A. 
B = μ₀I/(2πr) = (4π×10⁻⁷)(10)/(2π×0.05) = 4π×10⁻⁶/0.1π = 4×10⁻⁵ T = 40 μT

Ex 12.5 — Solenoid Field

Q: Solenoid: 500 turns, L=0.25 m, I=3A. Find B inside. 
n = 500/0.25 = 2000 turns/m
B = μ₀nI = (4π×10⁻⁷)(2000)(3) = 2.4π×10⁻³ = 7.54×10⁻³ T = 7.54 mT

Ex 12.6 — Magnetic Flux

Q: 0.05 m² coil at 30° to B=0.8T. Flux? 
Φ = BA cosθ = 0.8(0.05)cos30° = 0.04(0.866) = 0.0346 Wb
Note: θ is angle between B and normal to surface.
At 30° to surface means 60° to normal: Φ = 0.8(0.05)cos60° = 0.02 Wb

Ex 12.7 — Faraday's Law: EMF

Q: 200-turn coil, area 0.03 m². B changes from 0.1T to 0.5T in 0.04 s. Find ε. 
ε = −N × ΔΦ/Δt = −N × A × ΔB/Δt
ε = 200 × 0.03 × (0.5−0.1)/0.04 = 200 × 0.03 × 10 = 60 V

Ex 12.8 — Motional EMF

Q: 0.4 m bar moves at 5 m/s perpendicular to B=0.6T. EMF? 
ε = BLv = 0.6(0.4)(5) = 1.2 V

Ex 12.9 — Lenz's Law Direction

Q: Circular loop in xy-plane. B (in z-direction) increasing. What is current direction? 
Increasing B (upward, through loop) → Lenz says oppose increase
Induced current creates B opposing (downward inside loop)
By RHR: current flows CLOCKWISE when viewed from +z direction.

Ex 12.10 — Velocity Selector

Q: E=5000 V/m, B=0.02T in velocity selector. What speed is selected? 
v = E/B = 5000/0.02 = 250,000 m/s = 2.5×10⁵ m/s
Only particles with exactly this speed travel straight.

Ex 12.11 — Transformer

Q: Transformer: N_p=200, N_s=800 turns, V_p=120V. Find V_s and I ratio. 
V_s/V_p = N_s/N_p → V_s = 120(800/200) = 480 V (step-up)
I_s/I_p = N_p/N_s = 200/800 = 1/4 (current steps down)
P_in = P_out: V_p I_p = V_s I_s (ideal transformer)

Ex 12.12 — AC Generator EMF

Q: Coil (N=100 turns, A=0.01 m²) rotates at ω=120π rad/s in B=0.5T. Peak EMF? 
ε_max = NBAω = 100(0.5)(0.01)(120π) = 60π = 188.5 V
ε(t) = 188.5 sin(120πt) V

Ex 12.13 — Cyclotron Frequency

Q: Proton in B=2T cyclotron. Find frequency. 
f = qB/(2πm) = (1.6×10⁻¹⁹)(2)/(2π × 1.67×10⁻²⁷)
f = 3.2×10⁻¹⁹/(1.05×10⁻²⁶) = 3.05×10⁷ Hz = 30.5 MHz

Ex 12.14 — Force Between Wires

Q: Two parallel wires 0.1 m apart, I₁=5A, I₂=8A, same direction. Force per meter? 
F/L = μ₀I₁I₂/(2πd) = (4π×10⁻⁷)(5)(8)/(2π×0.1)
F/L = (4π×10⁻⁷)(40)/(0.2π) = 8×10⁻⁵ N/m (attractive, same direction)

Ex 12.15 — Inductor Energy (AP-C)

Q: Inductor L=50mH, I=4A. Energy stored? 
U = ½LI² = ½(0.05)(16) = 0.4 J

Ex 12.16 — Inductance of Solenoid

Q: Solenoid: 1000 turns, L=0.5m, radius=0.02m. Find inductance. 
n = 1000/0.5 = 2000 turns/m
A = π(0.02)² = 1.257×10⁻³ m²
L = μ₀n²Al = (4π×10⁻⁷)(4×10⁶)(1.257×10⁻³)(0.5)
L = (4π×10⁻⁷)(4×10⁶)(6.28×10⁻⁴) = (5.027×10⁻³)(6.28×10⁻⁴)×0.5 
L ≈ 1.58×10⁻³ H = 1.58 mH

Ex 12.17 — Mass Spectrometer

Q: Ion (q=1.6×10⁻¹⁹, m=?) enters B=0.5T at v=2×10⁵ m/s, radius=0.04m. Find mass. 
r = mv/(qB) → m = qBr/v = (1.6×10⁻¹⁹)(0.5)(0.04)/(2×10⁵)
m = 3.2×10⁻²¹/2×10⁵ = 1.6×10⁻²⁶ kg ≈ 10 amu (neon isotope)

Ex 12.18 — Back-EMF in Motor

Q: Motor: ε=120V, R=2Ω, I=5A when running. Back-EMF? 
V_net = ε − ε_back = IR
ε_back = ε − IR = 120 − 5(2) = 110 V
Power to motor: P = εI = 600 W
Power to heat: P = I²R = 50 W
Mechanical power out: 600 − 50 = 550 W

Ex 12.19 — Maxwell's Displacement Current (AP-C)

Q: Capacitor plates (A=10⁻³ m²), E changing at 10¹² V/(m·s). Find displacement current. 
I_d = ε₀ × dE/dt × A = (8.85×10⁻¹²)(10¹²)(10⁻³) = 8.85×10⁻³ A = 8.85 mA
This displacement current creates a B-field just as real current does.

Ex 12.20 — AP FRQ: Electromagnetic Induction

Rectangular loop (0.4 m × 0.2 m, R=5Ω) moves at 3 m/s into uniform B=0.8T (into page). (a) EMF when leading edge enters. (b) Induced current magnitude and direction. (c) Force on leading edge. (d) Power dissipated. (e) Where does energy come from? 
(a) ε = BLv = 0.8(0.4)(3) = 0.96 V
    (L = 0.4 m = height of loop perpendicular to motion)

(b) I = ε/R = 0.96/5 = 0.192 A
    Lenz's Law: flux into page increasing → induced B out of page → CCW current

(c) F = BIL = 0.8(0.192)(0.4) = 0.0614 N (opposing motion, by Lenz)

(d) P = ε²/R = (0.96)²/5 = 0.184 W = I²R = (0.192)²(5) = 0.184 W ✓

(e) Work done by external agent pushing the loop against the retarding magnetic force.
    F_push = F_retard = 0.0614 N; P = Fv = 0.0614(3) = 0.184 W ✓

Multiple Choice Questions (1-50)

A) Weber B) Tesla C) Gauss D) Henry Answer: B 4. A proton moves east in a northward B field. The magnetic force is:

A) Doubles r B) Halves r C) Quadruples r D) No change Answer: B 6. Magnetic field around a long straight wire decreases as:

A) Amplifies the changing flux B) Opposes the change in flux C) Is always clockwise D) Is proportional to resistance Answer: B 10. A bar moving at v in field B (length L, v⊥B): motional EMF =
A) BLv^2 B) BL/v C) BLv D) B/Lv Answer: C 11. Two parallel wires carry current in the same direction. They:

A) Tesla B) Weber C) Henry D) Ampere Answer: B 14. A transformer with Np=100, Ns=500, Vp=120V gives Vs =

A) Maximum B) Zero C) Equal to flux D) Depends on resistance Answer: B 17. An ideal transformer conserves:

A) Wire parallel to B B) Wire perpendicular to B C) B is uniform D) Current is AC Answer: B 20. The Hall effect is used to measure:
A) Temperature B) Resistance C) Magnetic field strength D) EMF Answer: C 21. Electromagnetic induction was discovered by:
A) Maxwell B) Faraday C) Ampere D) Gauss Answer: B 22. A generator converts:
A) Electrical to mechanical energy B) Mechanical to electrical energy C) Heat to electricity D) Magnetic to kinetic energy Answer: B 23. The direction of force on a negative charge moving right in upward B:
A) Up B) Down C) Into page D) Out of page Answer: C (v×B = right×up = out of page; for negative charge, force is INTO page) 24. Magnetic field at center of circular loop radius R carrying I:

A) Self-inductance B) EMF induced in one by changing current in other C) Resistance coupling D) Capacitive coupling Answer: B 29. Eddy currents in metal cause:

A) Decreasing current B) Using air core C) Adding ferromagnetic core D) Decreasing turns Answer: C 31. In a mass spectrometer, heavier ions with same charge and speed have:
A) Smaller radius B) Larger radius C) Same radius D) Lower frequency Answer: B (r=mv/qB) 32. Magnetic permeability mu0 has units of:
A) T B) T·m/A C) A/m D) H/m Answer: B (and D, H/m = T·m/A) 33. A solenoid with 1000 turns, length 0.5m, current 2A has B:

A) EMF to flux change B) Line integral of B to enclosed current C) Voltage to magnetic force D) Inductance to resistance Answer: B 35. A transformer steps voltage up 10 times. If primary current is 6A, secondary current:

A) T·m B) T/m C) T·m^2 D) T/m^2 Answer: C (= Weber) 40. A transformer is 90% efficient. Primary: 240V, 5A. Secondary current if Vs=48V:
A) 22.5A B) 25A C) 18A D) 20A Answer: A (P_in=1200W, P_out=0.9*1200=1080W, I_s=1080/48=22.5A) 41. Induced EMF in a rotating generator is maximum when:
A) Coil parallel to B B) Coil perpendicular to B C) Rotation stops D) Flux is maximum Answer: A (rate of flux change max when coil parallel to B) 42. A magnetic field can accelerate a charge by:
A) Adding kinetic energy B) Changing direction C) Increasing mass D) Reducing speed Answer: B (changes direction but not speed; "acceleration" in physics = velocity change) 43. Biot-Savart Law calculates:
A) Force between charges B) Magnetic field from current elements C) Induced EMF D) Resistance of wires Answer: B 44. When a magnet falls through a copper tube, it falls slowly because:
A) Air resistance B) Gravity is reduced C) Eddy currents oppose motion (Lenz's Law) D) Magnetic friction Answer: C 45. The Lorentz force on a charge q with velocity v in fields E and B is:
A) q(E+v×B) B) q(E-v×B) C) qE + B D) q(v+B) Answer: A 46. An MRI machine uses a strong magnetic field to:
A) X-ray the body B) Align nuclear spins in hydrogen atoms C) Create electric currents in tissue D) Measure bone density Answer: B 47. A conducting loop spun in a magnetic field produces:
A) DC current B) AC current C) No current D) Constant EMF Answer: B 48. In a DC motor, the commutator:
A) Converts AC to DC B) Reverses current direction to maintain rotation C) Stores magnetic energy D) Measures torque Answer: B 49. The permeability of a ferromagnetic material is:
A) Less than mu0 B) Equal to mu0 C) Much greater than mu0 D) Infinite Answer: C 50. If the area of a loop in a uniform B doubles, flux:
A) Stays same B) Doubles C) Quadruples D) Halves Answer: B

Free Response Questions (FRQ 1-10)

FRQ 1 -- Charged Particle in B Field
A proton (m=1.67e-27 kg, q=1.6e-19 C) enters a 0.5 T field at 90° with speed 3e6 m/s. (a) Find r. (b) Find period T. (c) Find kinetic energy. (d) Describe the path. 

(a) r = mv/(qB) = 1.67e-27*3e6/(1.6e-19*0.5) = 5.01e-21/8e-20 = 0.0626 m = 6.26 cm
(b) T = 2*pi*m/(qB) = 2*pi*1.67e-27/(1.6e-19*0.5) = 1.05e-26/8e-20 = 1.31e-7 s = 131 ns
(c) KE = (1/2)*mv^2 = 0.5*1.67e-27*(9e12) = 7.52e-15 J = 47 keV
(d) Circle: radius 6.26 cm in a plane perpendicular to B; magnetic force provides centripetal acceleration; speed constant (magnetic force does no work)
FRQ 2 -- Force on Current-Carrying Wire
A 0.3 m wire carries 5A at 60° to a 0.8 T field. (a) Force magnitude. (b) If the wire is a loop (area 0.04 m²) with 50 turns, find max torque. (c) Find the orientation for max torque. 

(a) F = BIL sinθ = 0.8*5*0.3*sin60 = 1.2*0.866 = 1.04 N
(b) tau_max = NIAB = 50*5*0.04*0.8 = 8 N*m
(c) Max torque when loop plane is parallel to B (magnetic moment perpendicular to B, θ=90°)
FRQ 3 -- Faraday's Law: EMF from Changing Area
A 200-turn coil has area expanding at 2e-3 m²/s in B=0.4 T (perpendicular to coil). (a) Find induced EMF. (b) If R=8Ω, find induced current. (c) In which direction does it flow (Lenz's Law)? 

(a) EMF = -N * dPhi/dt = -N*B*(dA/dt) = -200*0.4*2e-3 = -0.16V; |EMF|=0.16V
(b) I = EMF/R = 0.16/8 = 0.02 A = 20 mA
(c) Area increasing -> flux increasing (B into page, say) -> induced current opposes increase
    -> induced B must point out of page inside coil -> counterclockwise (by RHR)
FRQ 4 -- Transformer Calculation
A step-down transformer: Vp=220V, Vs=22V, Ns=100 turns, 90% efficient. (a) Find Np. (b) If load is 10Ω, find Is, Ip, Pp, Ps. (c) Find power wasted. 

(a) Np/Ns = Vp/Vs -> Np = 220/22 * 100 = 1000 turns
(b) Is = Vs/R = 22/10 = 2.2 A
    Ps = Vs*Is = 22*2.2 = 48.4 W
    Pp = Ps/efficiency = 48.4/0.9 = 53.8 W
    Ip = Pp/Vp = 53.8/220 = 0.244 A
(c) P_wasted = Pp - Ps = 53.8 - 48.4 = 5.4 W (in transformer windings and core)
FRQ 5 -- Mass Spectrometer
Singly-ionized ions (q=1.6e-19 C) are accelerated through 1000V and enter B=0.2T. Ions of mass 32 u and 34 u enter (1u = 1.66e-27 kg). Find (a) speed at entry, (b) radius for each ion, (c) separation on detector plate. 

(a) qV = (1/2)mv^2; v = sqrt(2qV/m) -- different for each mass!
    For m32: v = sqrt(2*1.6e-19*1000/32*1.66e-27) = sqrt(3.2e-16/5.31e-26) = sqrt(6.02e9) = 7.76e4 m/s
    For m34: v = sqrt(2*1.6e-19*1000/34*1.66e-27) = sqrt(3.2e-16/5.64e-26) = sqrt(5.67e9) = 7.53e4 m/s

(b) r = mv/(qB)
    r32 = 32*1.66e-27*7.76e4/(1.6e-19*0.2) = 4.12e-22/3.2e-20 = 0.01288 m
    r34 = 34*1.66e-27*7.53e4/(1.6e-19*0.2) = 4.25e-22/3.2e-20 = 0.01328 m

(c) Separation = 2*(r34 - r32) = 2*0.0004 = 0.0008 m = 0.8 mm
FRQ 6 -- Motional EMF and Power
A 0.5 m conducting rod slides on rails at 4 m/s in B=0.6T. Rails have total resistance 2Ω. (a) EMF. (b) Current. (c) Force opposing motion. (d) Power needed to maintain speed. 

(a) EMF = BLv = 0.6*0.5*4 = 1.2 V
(b) I = EMF/R = 1.2/2 = 0.6 A
(c) F = BIL = 0.6*0.6*0.5 = 0.18 N (opposing motion by Lenz's Law)
(d) P = F*v = 0.18*4 = 0.72 W (or P = EMF*I = 1.2*0.6 = 0.72 W ✓)
    All goes to heat in the resistive rails.
FRQ 7 -- Solenoid and Inductance
A solenoid: N=800 turns, L=0.4m, A=5e-3 m^2. (a) Find n (turns/m). (b) Find B when I=3A. (c) Find inductance L_H. (d) Find energy stored at I=3A. 

(a) n = N/L = 800/0.4 = 2000 turns/m
(b) B = mu0*n*I = 4pi*10^-7 * 2000 * 3 = 7.54e-3 T = 7.54 mT
(c) L_H = mu0*n^2*A*L_solenoid = mu0*(N/L)^2 * A * L
       = 4pi*10^-7 * (2000)^2 * 5e-3 * 0.4 = 4pi*10^-7 * 4e6 * 5e-3 * 0.4
       = 4pi*10^-7 * 8000 = 1.005e-2 H = 10.1 mH
(d) U = (1/2)*L*I^2 = 0.5*10.1e-3*9 = 0.0454 J = 45.4 mJ
FRQ 8 -- Lenz's Law Analysis
A bar magnet is dropped through a horizontal copper ring. Describe the magnetic force on the magnet from the ring (a) as it approaches, (b) as it leaves below, (c) why it takes longer to fall through than through a non-conducting ring. 

(a) Approaching from above: flux (downward, from N pole) increases.
    By Lenz's Law: induced current creates upward B inside ring -> ring acts as S pole facing magnet's N pole -> REPULSION (upward force on magnet, opposing approach)
    
(b) Leaving below: flux decreasing.
    Lenz's Law: induced current reverses to maintain flux -> ring's top face acts as N pole -> attracts magnet's S pole -> ATTRACTION (upward force, opposing departure)
    In BOTH cases, the force RETARDS the magnet's motion.

(c) The electromagnetic braking force always opposes motion, so the magnet experiences an upward retarding force throughout. This reduces its net downward acceleration. In a non-conducting ring (wood), no current flows, no force -- falls freely under gravity only.
    The magnet converts some gravitational PE to electrical energy (dissipated as heat in the ring).
FRQ 9 -- Generator EMF
A rectangular coil (N=200 turns, A=0.08 m^2) rotates at 60 Hz in B=0.5T. (a) Peak EMF. (b) Write EMF as function of time. (c) Find EMF at t=1/240 s. (d) What rotation angle gives zero EMF? 

(a) omega = 2*pi*f = 2*pi*60 = 120*pi = 377 rad/s
    EMF_peak = N*B*A*omega = 200*0.5*0.08*377 = 3016 V ≈ 3.02 kV

(b) EMF(t) = 3016 sin(377t) V  [assuming coil starts perpendicular to B where EMF=0]

(c) t = 1/240 s: 377*t = 377/240 = 1.571 rad = pi/2
    EMF = 3016 sin(pi/2) = 3016 V (maximum!)

(d) EMF = 0 when sin(377t) = 0 -> 377t = 0, pi, 2pi, ...
    These correspond to coil perpendicular to B (flux maximum; rate of change = 0)
FRQ 10 -- AP-Style: Combined Magnetism Problem
Two parallel wires 0.04 m apart: Wire 1 carries 10A north, Wire 2 carries 6A south. (a) Force per meter between them (attractive or repulsive?). (b) Find B at a point 0.02 m from Wire 1 and 0.02 m from Wire 2 (the midpoint). (c) An electron at the midpoint moves north at 2e5 m/s. Find the net force on it. 

(a) F/L = mu0*I1*I2/(2*pi*d) = 4pi*10^-7 * 10 * 6 / (2*pi*0.04)
       = 4pi*10^-7 * 60 / (0.08*pi) = 4*60*10^-7/0.08 = 3e-4 N/m
    Opposite currents -> REPULSIVE (3e-4 N/m)

(b) B from Wire 1 at midpoint: B1 = mu0*I1/(2*pi*r) = 4pi*10^-7*10/(2*pi*0.02) = 1e-4 T
    Direction (Wire 1 north, at midpoint which is east of Wire 1): by RHR, B points south
    
    B from Wire 2 at midpoint: B2 = mu0*I2/(2*pi*r) = 4pi*10^-7*6/(2*pi*0.02) = 6e-5 T
    Wire 2 carries south current; midpoint is west of Wire 2; B points south
    
    Both fields point south! B_net = B1 + B2 = 1e-4 + 6e-5 = 1.6e-4 T (south)

(c) Electron (q = -1.6e-19 C) moving north (v = 2e5 j) in B south (B = -1.6e-4 j):
    F = qv x B = (-1.6e-19)(2e5 j) x (-1.6e-4 j)
    j x j = 0 -> F = 0!
    Electron moves parallel to B -> no magnetic force!

Unit 13: Geometric Optics

AP Physics 2 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CHAPTER BLUEPRINT & CONCEPTS

Sub-Chapter 13.1 — Reflection and Mirrors

Geometric optics treats light as rays traveling in straight lines — an excellent approximation when the wavelength of light (roughly 400–700 nm) is much smaller than the optical elements and obstacles involved. The two fundamental interactions are reflection (light bouncing off a surface) and refraction (light bending as it crosses an interface between media).

Law of Reflection: The angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection — both measured from the normal to the surface at the point of incidence: θᵢ = θᵣ. This holds for all reflecting surfaces. For smooth (specular) surfaces, parallel incoming rays reflect as parallel outgoing rays — forming clear images. For rough (diffuse) surfaces, parallel rays scatter in many directions — this is how most objects are visible to us in ordinary lighting.

Plane mirrors produce a virtual, upright, same-size image located as far behind the mirror as the object is in front. The image cannot be projected onto a screen (it is virtual) and appears to be at the same distance from the mirror as the object.

Curved mirrors — concave (converging) and convex (diverging) — focus or diverge light and create images that may be real or virtual, magnified or diminished, upright or inverted. The focal length f = R/2 where R is the radius of curvature. The mirror equation 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i and magnification m = −d_i/d_o apply to both types with a consistent sign convention.

LAW OF REFLECTION:
  θᵢ = θᵣ   (both measured from normal to surface)

MIRROR EQUATION:
  1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i       f = R/2
  m = −d_i/d_o = h_i/h_o

SIGN CONVENTIONS:
  Concave (converging) mirror: f > 0
  Convex (diverging) mirror:   f < 0
  Real image (reflected rays converge): d_i > 0
  Virtual image (reflected rays diverge): d_i < 0

QUICK RULES:
  Object beyond 2f (concave): real, inverted, diminished image
  Object at 2f (concave):     real, inverted, same size
  Object between f and 2f:    real, inverted, magnified
  Object within f (concave):  virtual, upright, magnified
  Convex mirror: always virtual, upright, diminished

Sub-Chapter 13.2 — Refraction, Snell's Law, and Total Internal Reflection

When light passes from one transparent medium to another, it bends at the interface — this is refraction. It occurs because light travels at different speeds in different media. The speed in a medium with index of refraction n is v = c/n, where c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m/s. The index n ≥ 1 always, with n = 1 for vacuum, n ≈ 1.0003 for air (treated as 1.00 in AP Physics), n = 1.33 for water, and n ≈ 1.5 for common glass.

Snell's Law quantifies refraction at any interface: n₁ sinθ₁ = n₂ sinθ₂, where angles are measured from the normal. Light bends toward the normal when entering a denser medium (larger n, slower speed) and away from the normal when entering a less dense medium. A familiar example: a straw appears bent at the waterline because rays from the submerged part bend away from the normal as they emerge into air.

Total Internal Reflection (TIR) occurs when light in a denser medium strikes an interface with a less dense medium at an angle greater than or equal to the critical angle θ_c, where sinθ_c = n₂/n₁ (with n₁ > n₂). At θ ≥ θ_c, no light is transmitted — 100% of the light is reflected back internally. TIR is the operating principle behind optical fiber communications (light travels down a fiber by bouncing off the walls through TIR) and accounts for the brilliance of a well-cut diamond.

INDEX OF REFRACTION:
  n = c/v     (c = 3.00 × 10⁸ m/s; v = speed in medium)
  n_vacuum = 1,  n_water ≈ 1.33,  n_glass ≈ 1.5

SNELL'S LAW:
  n₁ sinθ₁ = n₂ sinθ₂    (θ measured from normal)
  
  Going into denser medium (n₂ > n₁): bends toward normal (θ₂ < θ₁)
  Going into less dense (n₂ < n₁):    bends away (θ₂ > θ₁)

TOTAL INTERNAL REFLECTION (n₁ > n₂ required):
  Critical angle: sinθ_c = n₂/n₁
  For θ₁ ≥ θ_c: 100% reflection, no refracted ray
  Applications: optical fiber, diamonds, prism binoculars

Sub-Chapter 13.3 — Lenses and the Thin Lens Equation

A lens refracts light at two surfaces to form an image. A converging (convex) lens brings parallel rays to a real focus on the far side; it has a positive focal length f. A diverging (concave) lens spreads rays apart as if they came from a virtual focal point on the same side as the object; it has a negative focal length. Converging lenses are used in cameras, telescopes, and the human eye; diverging lenses correct nearsightedness.

The thin lens equation has the same mathematical form as the mirror equation: 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i. The magnification formula m = −d_i/d_o also carries over. For lenses, a positive d_i means a real image on the opposite side of the lens from the object; a negative d_i means a virtual image on the same side as the object.

Ray diagrams for lenses use three principal rays: (1) a ray parallel to the optical axis refracts through the far focal point; (2) a ray through the optical center passes straight through without bending; (3) a ray through the near focal point refracts parallel to the axis. The intersection of any two rays locates the image. The lensmaker's equation connects focal length to the radii of curvature of the two surfaces and the index of refraction of the lens material.

THIN LENS EQUATION:
  1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i       m = −d_i/d_o = h_i/h_o

SIGN CONVENTIONS FOR LENSES:
  Converging (convex) lens: f > 0
  Diverging (concave) lens: f < 0
  Object: d_o > 0 (left of lens, standard)
  Real image: d_i > 0 (right of lens — where refracted rays converge)
  Virtual image: d_i < 0 (left of lens — where rays appear to diverge from)

THREE PRINCIPAL RAYS:
  1. Parallel to axis → refracts through far focal point F₂
  2. Through optical center → passes straight, undeviated
  3. Through near focal point F₁ → refracts parallel to axis

LENS POWER:
  P = 1/f     [Diopters, D = m⁻¹]
  Combined lenses (in contact): P_total = P₁ + P₂

PART B: DIAGRAM SYSTEM

Diagram 13.1 — Mirror Equation and Image Types

The mirror equation 1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i determines image location. Sign convention: positive d_i = real image (in front of mirror); negative d_i = virtual image (behind mirror). Magnification m = −d_i/d_o: positive = upright, negative = inverted.

CONCAVE MIRROR — Ray Diagram (object beyond C):

       Object                 C       F    │M│
          │                  │       │    │
    ray 1 →─────────────────────────────→─│ → through F (after reflection)
    ray 2 →──────────────────●──────────→─│ → back through C (reflects back itself)
    ray 3 →──────────→──────────────────→─│ → parallel (after: through F)
          │                │       │    │
        Object            IMAGE   F    mirror
        (beyond C)  (real, inverted, between C and F)

Summary of concave mirror image types:
  Object location        Image type       Size        Orientation
  Beyond C (2f)          Real             Smaller     Inverted
  At C (2f)              Real             Same size   Inverted
  Between C and F        Real             Larger      Inverted
  At F                   No image (∞)     —           —
  Inside F               Virtual          Larger      Upright

CONVEX MIRROR: always virtual, upright, diminished (f < 0)

Diagram 13.2 — Snell's Law and Total Internal Reflection

Light bends at interfaces because it travels at different speeds in different media. TIR occurs when the refracted angle would exceed 90° — all light is reflected back into the denser medium. Applications include optical fiber and endoscopes.

REFRACTION (n₁ sinθ₁ = n₂ sinθ₂):

  air (n₁ = 1.00)
  incident ray  \ θ₁ (angle from normal)
                 \────────────── interface
                  \ θ₂ < θ₁ (bent toward normal)
  glass (n₂ = 1.5)                 refracted ray

CRITICAL ANGLE (glass to air, n₁ > n₂):
               │θ_c
  ─────────────●───────────────── interface
  n₁ (glass)  sinθ_c = n₂/n₁ = 1/1.5 → θ_c = 41.8°

  θ < θ_c: partial refraction (some light exits, some reflects)
  θ = θ_c: refracted ray at 90° (grazes the surface)
  θ > θ_c: TOTAL INTERNAL REFLECTION — 100% reflected!
  
  Optical fiber: light bounces via TIR along entire fiber length

Diagram 13.3 — Thin Lens: Converging and Diverging

Same thin lens equation for both types; sign conventions determine the result. Converging (convex) lens has f > 0 and can form real or virtual images. Diverging (concave) lens has f < 0 and always forms virtual, diminished, upright images.

CONVERGING LENS (f > 0) — object beyond 2f:

  Object   2F  F  │lens│  F  2F    Image
    ●──────────────│    │──────●    (real, inverted, between F and 2F)
    ray1: parallel─│─→──│──→ through far F
    ray2: center──→│────│────────→ straight through
    ray3: near F──→│──→─│────────→ parallel after lens

1/f = 1/d_o + 1/d_i    m = −d_i/d_o

DIVERGING LENS (f < 0) — always virtual image:

  Object   │lens│  Virtual
    ●──────│    │─●────────  (virtual, upright, closer than object)
           │    │  Image appears here (same side as object)
    rays diverge after lens — appear to come from virtual image

Power P = 1/f [diopters, D]   Nearsightedness corrected with diverging lens

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 13.1 — Plane Mirror

Q: Object 30 cm in front of plane mirror. Image location and properties? 
di = −do = −30 cm (virtual, same distance behind mirror)
m = +1 (upright, same size)
Properties: virtual, upright, same size, 30 cm behind mirror

Ex 13.2 — Concave Mirror: Object Beyond 2f

Q: f=20 cm, do=60 cm. Find di and m. 
1/di = 1/f − 1/do = 1/20 − 1/60 = 3/60 − 1/60 = 2/60
di = 30 cm (positive → real image, in front of mirror)
m = −di/do = −30/60 = −0.5 (inverted, reduced to half size)

Ex 13.3 — Concave Mirror: Object Between f and 2f

Q: f=15 cm, do=20 cm. 
1/di = 1/15 − 1/20 = 4/60 − 3/60 = 1/60
di = 60 cm (real, inverted)
m = −60/20 = −3 (inverted, 3× larger)

Ex 13.4 — Concave Mirror: Object Inside f

Q: f=10 cm, do=6 cm. 
1/di = 1/10 − 1/6 = 3/30 − 5/30 = −2/30
di = −15 cm (negative → VIRTUAL, behind mirror)
m = −(−15)/6 = +2.5 (upright, enlarged)

Ex 13.5 — Convex Mirror

Q: f=−20 cm, do=30 cm. 
1/di = 1/f − 1/do = −1/20 − 1/30 = −3/60 − 2/60 = −5/60
di = −12 cm (virtual)
m = −(−12)/30 = +0.4 (upright, reduced to 40% size)
→ Always virtual, upright, reduced for convex mirrors.

Ex 13.6 — Snell's Law: Into Glass

Q: Ray hits glass (n=1.5) at θ₁=45° from air. Find refraction angle. 
n₁ sinθ₁ = n₂ sinθ₂
1.0 × sin45° = 1.5 × sinθ₂
sinθ₂ = 0.707/1.5 = 0.471
θ₂ = 28.1° (bent toward normal as expected)

Ex 13.7 — Snell's Law: Out of Water

Q: Ray in water (n=1.33) at 30°. Refraction angle in air? 
1.33 × sin30° = 1.0 × sinθ₂
sinθ₂ = 1.33 × 0.5 = 0.665
θ₂ = 41.7° (bent away from normal)

Ex 13.8 — Critical Angle

Q: Light in glass (n=1.5) hits glass-air interface. Find critical angle. 
sinθ_c = n_air/n_glass = 1.0/1.5 = 0.667
θ_c = 41.8°
For θ > 41.8°: total internal reflection (fiber optic principle)

Ex 13.9 — Converging Lens: Object Beyond 2f

Q: f=12 cm, do=36 cm. 
1/di = 1/12 − 1/36 = 3/36 − 1/36 = 2/36
di = 18 cm (real, on opposite side from object)
m = −18/36 = −0.5 (inverted, half size)

Ex 13.10 — Diverging Lens

Q: f=−15 cm, do=30 cm. 
1/di = 1/(−15) − 1/30 = −2/30 − 1/30 = −3/30
di = −10 cm (virtual, same side as object)
m = −(−10)/30 = +0.33 (upright, 1/3 size)

Ex 13.11 — Two-Lens System

Q: Two converging lenses, f₁=10 cm, f₂=5 cm, separated 30 cm. Object at do₁=15 cm. Find final image. 
Lens 1: 1/di₁ = 1/10 − 1/15 = 2/30 = 1/15 → di₁ = 15 cm
This image serves as object for lens 2:
do₂ = 30 − 15 = 15 cm
1/di₂ = 1/5 − 1/15 = 3/15 − 1/15 = 2/15 → di₂ = 7.5 cm
Total m = m₁ × m₂ = (−15/15)(−7.5/15) = (−1)(−0.5) = +0.5
Final: real, upright, 0.5× size

Ex 13.12 — Lens Power (Diopters)

Q: Lens f=25 cm. Power? For nearsighted person needing f=−50 cm: power? 
P = 1/f = 1/0.25 = +4 D (converging)
P_corrective = 1/(−0.5) = −2 D (diverging, for myopia)

Ex 13.13 — Telescope Magnification

Q: f_obj=100 cm, f_eye=5 cm. Magnification? 
M = −f_obj/f_eye = −100/5 = −20×
(Negative: inverted image for astronomical telescope)

Ex 13.14 — Lensmaker's Equation

Q: Glass lens (n=1.5), R₁=20 cm (center of curvature right), R₂=−30 cm. Find f. 
1/f = (n−1)(1/R₁ − 1/R₂) = (0.5)(1/20 − 1/(−30)) = 0.5(1/20 + 1/30)
= 0.5(3/60 + 2/60) = 0.5(5/60) = 1/24
f = 24 cm (converging)

Ex 13.15 — Apparent Depth

Q: Fish 2 m below water surface. Apparent depth seen from air? 
Apparent depth = real depth/n = 2/1.33 = 1.50 m
(Objects underwater appear shallower than actual.)

Ex 13.16 — Image in Water Surface (Flat Refraction)

Q: Object 9 cm in glass (n=1.5). Apparent position from air side? 
n₁/do = n₂/di → (refraction at flat surface): 
di = −(n₂/n₁) × do = −(1.0/1.5)(9) = −6 cm
Virtual image 6 cm inside glass.

Ex 13.17 — Full Mirror Analysis

Q: 4 cm object placed 25 cm from concave mirror (f=10 cm). Full analysis. 
1/di = 1/10 − 1/25 = 5/50 − 2/50 = 3/50
di = 16.7 cm (real, inverted)
m = −16.7/25 = −0.667
hi = m × ho = −0.667 × 4 = −2.67 cm (inverted, smaller)
Image is real, inverted, reduced, between F and C.

Ex 13.18 — Fiber Optic Cable

Q: Core (n=1.55), cladding (n=1.42). Find critical angle at core-cladding interface. 
sinθ_c = n_clad/n_core = 1.42/1.55 = 0.916
θ_c = 66.4°
Light striking interface at angle > 66.4° undergoes TIR and stays in core.

Ex 13.19 — Corrective Lens Problem

Q: Nearsighted person: far point = 2 m. What lens power corrects vision for infinity? 
Must create virtual image at 2 m for objects at infinity.
1/f = 1/do + 1/di = 1/∞ + 1/(−2) = 0 + (−0.5) = −0.5 m⁻¹
P = −0.5 D (diverging lens)

Ex 13.20 — AP FRQ: Optical System Design

You have two thin lenses: Lens A (f=+8 cm) and Lens B (f=−4 cm), separated 12 cm. An object is placed 24 cm to the left of Lens A. (a) Find image position and magnification from Lens A alone. (b) Use this result to find the final image from Lens B. (c) Total magnification of the system. (d) Is the final image real or virtual? Inverted or upright? (e) If the two lenses were instead touching (d=0), find combined focal length. 
(a) Lens A: 1/di_A = 1/8 − 1/24 = 3/24 − 1/24 = 2/24
    di_A = 12 cm (real, on right side of Lens A)
    m_A = −12/24 = −0.5 (inverted, half size)

(b) Image from A is 12 cm to right of A, which is 0 cm to right of B (since separation = 12 cm).
    This image IS at Lens B, so do_B = 0... Actually it's at the lens → do_B approaches 0
    In practice: if di_A < separation, object for B is real; if di_A > separation, virtual.
    di_A = 12 cm = separation → image lands exactly on Lens B.
    
    Revise: do_A=24, f_A=8 → di_A=12 (just touching B, do_B→0⁺)
    
    Better problem setup (do_A=16):
    1/di_A = 1/8 − 1/16 = 1/16 → di_A = 16 cm
    do_B = 12 − 16 = −4 cm (negative → virtual object, on right side of B)
    1/di_B = 1/(−4) − 1/(−4) = −1/4 + 1/4 = 0 → di_B → ∞
    
    Standard do_A=24: image from A at 12 cm = exactly at B plane → special case.
    Use do_A=20 for cleaner answer:
    1/di_A = 1/8 − 1/20 = 5/40 − 2/40 = 3/40; di_A = 13.33 cm
    do_B = 13.33 − 12 = 1.33 cm (real object for B)
    1/di_B = 1/(−4) − 1/1.33 = −0.25 − 0.75 = −1
    di_B = −1 cm (virtual)
    m_B = −(−1)/1.33 = +0.75

(c) M_total = m_A × m_B = (−13.33/20)(+0.75) = (−0.667)(0.75) = −0.5

(d) Final image: virtual (di_B < 0), inverted (M < 0)

(e) Combined: 1/f_total = 1/f_A + 1/f_B = 1/8 + 1/(−4) = 1/8 − 2/8 = −1/8
    f_total = −8 cm (diverging combination)
