Free Response Questions (FRQ 1-10)

FRQ 1 -- Mirror Image Analysis
A concave mirror has radius R=40 cm. Find image position, size, and orientation for objects at: (a) 100 cm, (b) 20 cm, (c) 10 cm. 

f = R/2 = 20 cm

(a) do=100: 1/di=1/20-1/100=4/100, di=25 cm (real, inverted)
    m = -25/100 = -0.25 (inverted, diminished to 1/4 size)

(b) do=20 (at focal point): 1/di=1/20-1/20=0, di=infinity (no image/image at infinity)

(c) do=10 (inside focal point): 1/di=1/20-1/10=-1/20, di=-20 cm (virtual, behind mirror)
    m = -(-20)/10 = +2 (upright, double size -- magnifying mirror!)
FRQ 2 -- Refraction and Total Internal Reflection
Light in glass (n=1.65) hits the glass-air interface. (a) Find critical angle. (b) A ray hits at 30°. Find refracted angle. (c) A ray hits at 45°. What happens? (d) Describe optical fiber application. 

(a) sinθc = n2/n1 = 1/1.65 = 0.606; θc = 37.3°

(b) θ1=30° < θc: refraction occurs
    n1 sinθ1 = n2 sinθ2: 1.65 sin30 = 1.0 sinθ2
    sinθ2 = 1.65*0.5 = 0.825; θ2 = 55.6° (bends away from normal)

(c) θ1=45° > θc=37.3°: TOTAL INTERNAL REFLECTION occurs
    Ray reflects at 45° on the glass side; no refracted ray exits

(d) Optical fiber: glass core (n~1.5) surrounded by cladding (n~1.45)
    Light enters at shallow angle, hits wall at angle > critical angle, totally reflects
    Continues bouncing along fiber without loss -- transmits information (internet, phone)
    Advantage: no signal loss from absorption; bandwidth much higher than copper wire
FRQ 3 -- Converging Lens System
Two converging lenses: L1 (f=10 cm) is 30 cm from L2 (f=15 cm). Object 25 cm left of L1. (a) Image from L1. (b) This image as object for L2. (c) Final image properties. (d) Total magnification. 

(a) L1: 1/di1 = 1/10 - 1/25 = 5/50 - 2/50 = 3/50; di1 = 50/3 = 16.67 cm (right of L1)
    m1 = -16.67/25 = -0.667 (inverted, diminished)

(b) L1 image is 30 - 16.67 = 13.33 cm to LEFT of L2 -> do2 = 13.33 cm
    1/di2 = 1/15 - 1/13.33 = 1/15 - 0.075 = 0.0667 - 0.075 = -0.0083
    di2 = -120 cm (virtual, 120 cm left of L2!)

(c) Virtual image, 120 cm left of L2 = 90 cm left of L1 (to left of object)
    m2 = -(-120)/13.33 = +9 (upright relative to L1's image)

(d) m_total = m1 * m2 = -0.667 * 9 = -6 (inverted, 6x larger than object)
FRQ 4 -- Vision Correction
A person can see clearly from 80 cm to infinity (both abnormal). They want to read at 25 cm. (a) Identify the vision problem. (b) Power of lens needed. (c) What is far-point correction? (d) Can one pair of glasses correct both? 

(a) Cannot see clearly closer than 80 cm -> farsighted (hyperopia)
    Near point is 80 cm (normal is 25 cm)

(b) Need to form virtual image of object at 25 cm at the person's near point 80 cm:
    do = 25 cm, di = -80 cm (virtual, same side as object)
    1/f = 1/25 + 1/(-80) = 4/100 - 1.25/100 = 2.75/100 -> f = 36.4 cm
    P = 1/f = 100/36.4 = 2.75 diopters (converging lens)

(c) Far point: person can see to infinity -- no far-point correction needed

(d) Since far vision is fine (can see infinity), only near correction is needed.
    Progressive lenses or reading glasses with +2.75 D would work.
FRQ 5 -- Telescope Analysis
An astronomical telescope: objective f_obj=120 cm, eyepiece f_eye=4 cm. (a) Angular magnification. (b) Tube length for distant object. (c) Image of object at 500 m. (d) Why are real images inverted in telescopes? 

(a) M = -f_obj/f_eye = -120/4 = -30 (inverted, 30x magnification)
    |M| = 30

(b) For distant object: objective creates image at focal point (120 cm from objective)
    Eyepiece placed so image is at its focal point: 4 cm from eyepiece
    Tube length = f_obj + f_eye = 120 + 4 = 124 cm

(c) Object at 500 m = 50000 cm:
    1/di = 1/120 - 1/50000 = 0.008333 - 0.00002 = 0.008313
    di = 120.28 cm (just beyond focal point, almost same as for infinity)

(d) The objective lens forms a real, inverted image in the telescope's tube.
    The eyepiece then magnifies this image (acts as magnifier).
    Since the intermediate image is inverted, the final view is inverted.
    For astronomy, inversion doesn't matter; terrestrial scopes add erecting prism.
FRQ 6 -- Thin Film and Wavelength
A soap bubble of n=1.35 appears green (lambda=530 nm in air) from constructive interference. (a) Minimum thickness for constructive reflection. (b) Next thickness for constructive reflection. (c) What color for t=300 nm? 

Both surfaces: top (air to film, n increases) causes phase flip; bottom (film to air, n decreases) causes NO flip.
Net: one phase shift -> need extra half wavelength for constructive interference.
Condition: 2*n*t = (m + 1/2)*lambda_air, m=0,1,2,...

(a) m=0: 2*1.35*t = 0.5*530 = 265 nm; t = 265/(2*1.35) = 98.1 nm

(b) m=1: 2*1.35*t = 1.5*530 = 795 nm; t = 795/2.7 = 294 nm

(c) t=300 nm: 2*1.35*300 = 810 nm = (m+1/2)*lambda
    lambda = 810/(m+0.5)
    m=0: lambda=1620 nm (IR -- not visible)
    m=1: lambda=540 nm (green -- yes, visible!)
    m=2: lambda=324 nm (UV -- not visible)
    Bubble appears green at t=300 nm
FRQ 7 -- Diffraction Grating
A diffraction grating with 600 lines/mm. (a) Grating spacing d. (b) First-order angle for red (700 nm) and violet (400 nm). (c) At what order do red and violet overlap? (d) Angular dispersion in 2nd order. 

(a) d = 1mm/600 = 1.667e-3 mm = 1667 nm

(b) First order: d*sinθ = 1*lambda
    Red: sinθ = 700/1667 = 0.4199; θ_red = 24.8°
    Violet: sinθ = 400/1667 = 0.2399; θ_violet = 13.9°

(c) Order m for red overlapping m+1 for violet:
    m*700 = (m+1)*400 -> 300m = 400 -> m = 4/3 (not integer -- they never exactly overlap)
    Approximate: 2nd order violet (800 nm equiv) overlaps 1st order red (800>700, slightly past)
    Check: 3rd order violet = 3*400=1200, 2nd order red = 2*700=1400 -> don't overlap
    More precisely: m_red * 700 = m_violet * 400 -> m_r/m_v = 4/7 -> at 4th red, 7th violet overlap!

(d) 2nd order: d*sinθ = 2*lambda; dθ/dlambda = 2/(d*cosθ)
    At theta_red_2nd: sinθ=2*700/1667=0.840, cosθ=0.543
    dθ/dlambda = 2/(1667*0.543) = 2.21e-3 rad/nm = 0.127 deg/nm
FRQ 8 -- Mirror and Lens Comparison
For each setup, find image distance, magnification, and describe the image: (a) Concave mirror f=25cm, object at 75cm. (b) Convex mirror f=-25cm, object at 75cm. (c) Converging lens f=25cm, object at 75cm. (d) Diverging lens f=-25cm, object at 75cm. 

(a) Concave mirror, do=75: 1/di=1/25-1/75=2/75, di=37.5cm (real, in front)
    m=-37.5/75=-0.5 (inverted, half size)

(b) Convex mirror, do=75: 1/di=1/(-25)-1/75=-3/75-1/75=-4/75, di=-18.75cm (virtual, behind)
    m=-(-18.75)/75=+0.25 (upright, quarter size)

(c) Converging lens, do=75: 1/di=1/25-1/75=2/75, di=37.5cm (real, opposite side)
    m=-37.5/75=-0.5 (inverted, half size) -- same result as concave mirror!

(d) Diverging lens, do=75: 1/di=1/(-25)-1/75=-3/75-1/75=-4/75, di=-18.75cm (virtual, same side)
    m=-(-18.75)/75=+0.25 (upright, quarter size) -- same as convex mirror!

Note: Concave mirror ↔ converging lens (f>0, same math)
      Convex mirror  ↔ diverging lens  (f<0, same math) -- elegant symmetry!
FRQ 9 -- Snell's Law Application: Prism
A triangular prism (n=1.6, apex angle 45°). Light enters face 1 perpendicular (θ₁=0). (a) Angle of refraction at face 1. (b) Angle of incidence at face 2. (c) Does TIR occur at face 2? (d) If not, find exit angle. 

(a) Entering perpendicular to face 1: θ₁=0, no refraction: ray travels straight through face 1.

(b) Inside prism, ray hits face 2. Geometry of apex angle 45°:
    The angle of incidence at face 2 = 45° (by geometry: the deviation at face 1 is 0, and the prism apex is 45°, so ray hits face 2 at 45°).

(c) Critical angle: sinθc = 1/1.6 = 0.625; θc = 38.7°
    Angle at face 2 = 45° > 38.7° -> TIR OCCURS! Light is totally internally reflected.

(d) N/A (TIR -- no transmitted ray at face 2)
    The ray then hits face 3 at angle 45° (from symmetry), and again...
    Actually at face 3, angle = 90-45 = 45°, again > critical angle, so TIR again?
    No: face 3 is the bottom. Angle at face 3 = 0° (ray goes straight out the bottom)
    because TIR at face 2 redirects it perpendicular to face 3.
FRQ 10 -- AP-Style: Complete Optics Problem
An object 6 cm tall is placed 40 cm left of a converging lens (f=12 cm). A plane mirror is 60 cm to the right of the lens. (a) Find lens image position. (b) Does the image from the lens fall before or after the mirror? (c) Find the final image after mirror reflection. (d) Final image height. 

(a) 1/di = 1/12 - 1/40 = 40/480 - 12/480 = 28/480; di = 480/28 = 17.14 cm right of lens
    m1 = -17.14/40 = -0.429; image height = -0.429*6 = -2.57 cm (inverted)

(b) Mirror is 60 cm right of lens. Lens image at 17.14 cm < 60 cm.
    Image falls BEFORE the mirror (between lens and mirror).

(c) Plane mirror reflects this image. The image is 60-17.14 = 42.86 cm left of mirror.
    Plane mirror: image appears 42.86 cm BEHIND the mirror = 42.86 cm to the right of mirror
    = 60 + 42.86 = 102.86 cm right of lens.
    But we should trace from the object: reflected image is 42.86 cm behind mirror.

(d) Plane mirror doesn't change image size, so final image height = -2.57 cm (still inverted)
    The plane mirror creates an upright copy of whatever it reflects, so if the intermediate image is inverted (-2.57 cm), the plane mirror creates a virtual image of it that is also -2.57 cm tall (same orientation as intermediate image -- plane mirrors don't flip in the sense that changes up/down).
    
    Final: Image 42.86 cm behind mirror, virtual, inverted relative to original object, height = 2.57 cm.

Unit 14  ·  AP1/2
Waves & Sound
Doppler · Interference · Diffraction
v = fλβ = 10log(I/I₀)f' = f(v±v_o)/(v∓v_s)d sinθ = mλ

Unit 14: Waves, Sound, and Physical Optics

AP Physics 1 & 2 | Georgia Standards of Excellence

PART A: CHAPTER BLUEPRINT & CONCEPTS

Sub-Chapter 14.1 — Wave Properties and Classifications

A wave is a disturbance that propagates through a medium (or through empty space, for electromagnetic waves), transferring energy without permanently displacing the medium. Consider a ripple on a pond: the water molecules oscillate up and down about their equilibrium positions, but the circular wave pattern travels outward. The molecules oscillate; the pattern propagates. This distinction — between the motion of the medium and the motion of the wave — is fundamental.

Waves are classified by the relationship between oscillation direction and propagation direction. Transverse waves oscillate perpendicular to the direction of travel (waves on a string, all electromagnetic waves). Longitudinal waves oscillate parallel to the direction of travel — compressions and rarefactions of the medium (sound waves, seismic P-waves). Some waves, like surface water waves, have both transverse and longitudinal components.

The key wave parameters: Amplitude A (maximum displacement from equilibrium — determines intensity and energy, with E ∝ A²); wavelength λ (distance between successive identical points in the pattern, in meters); period T (time for one complete oscillation at any point); frequency f = 1/T (oscillations per second, in Hz); wave speed v = λf (depends on the medium, not on frequency or amplitude).

WAVE EQUATION:
  v = λf = λ/T          [m/s]
  f = 1/T               [Hz = cycles/s]

WAVE TYPES:
  Transverse: oscillation ⊥ propagation (string, EM waves)
  Longitudinal: oscillation ∥ propagation (sound)

WAVE SPEED (depends on medium, NOT on f or A):
  String: v = √(F_T/μ)  (F_T = tension, μ = linear mass density kg/m)
  Sound in air (20°C): v ≈ 343 m/s
  Sound: v increases with temperature and density of medium

SINUSOIDAL WAVE:
  y(x,t) = A sin(kx − ωt + φ)
  k = 2π/λ (wave number [rad/m]),  ω = 2πf (angular frequency [rad/s])

Sub-Chapter 14.2 — Superposition, Interference, and Diffraction

The principle of superposition states that when two or more waves overlap in the same medium, the net displacement at any point and time is the algebraic sum of the individual displacements. Superposition leads to interference — the constructive or destructive combination of waves.

Constructive interference produces maximum amplitude when two waves arrive at a point in phase (crest meets crest, trough meets trough). This occurs when the path length difference from the two sources to the observation point is an integer multiple of the wavelength: Δd = nλ (n = 0, ±1, ±2, ...). Destructive interference produces minimum (zero) amplitude when two waves arrive exactly out of phase (crest meets trough). This requires Δd = (n + ½)λ.

Young's double-slit experiment produces a pattern of bright and dark fringes on a screen by exploiting these conditions. The fringe spacing Δy = λL/d (L = distance to screen, d = slit separation) allows precise measurement of the wavelength of light — this is how visible light wavelengths were first accurately measured.

Diffraction is the bending and spreading of waves around obstacles or through openings. It is most pronounced when the wavelength is comparable to the opening size. This is why sound (wavelengths 0.02–17 m) diffracts easily around corners and furniture, while visible light (wavelengths ~400–700 nm) does not diffract noticeably around macroscopic obstacles.

SUPERPOSITION: y_net = y₁ + y₂  (algebraic addition)

DOUBLE-SOURCE INTERFERENCE (path difference Δd):
  Constructive: Δd = nλ          (n = 0, ±1, ±2, ...)
  Destructive:  Δd = (n + ½)λ

YOUNG'S DOUBLE SLIT (slit sep. d, screen distance L, λ):
  Bright fringes: y_n = nλL/d   (from center)
  Fringe spacing: Δy = λL/d

SINGLE SLIT DIFFRACTION (slit width a):
  1st dark fringe: sin θ = λ/a   (central maximum ± λ/a)

DIFFRACTION GRATING (slit spacing d):
  Maxima at: d sinθ = nλ   (n = 0, ±1, ±2, ...)

Sub-Chapter 14.3 — Standing Waves and Resonance

When two identical waves (same frequency, wavelength, amplitude) travel in opposite directions through the same medium, they superpose to create a standing wave — a pattern that oscillates in place rather than traveling. The pattern has fixed points of permanently zero displacement called nodes (where the two waves always cancel) and fixed points of maximum displacement called antinodes (where the waves always add constructively). Adjacent nodes are separated by half a wavelength.

Standing waves form at resonant frequencies — specific frequencies for which the boundary conditions are satisfied (e.g., nodes at fixed ends, antinodes at open ends). For a string fixed at both ends (or an open pipe open at both ends), both ends are nodes/antinodes of the same type, giving the harmonic series fₙ = nv/(2L) for n = 1, 2, 3, .... For a pipe closed at one end (node) and open at the other (antinode), only odd harmonics are present: fₙ = nv/(4L) for n = 1, 3, 5, ....

STANDING WAVE NODES AND ANTINODES:
  Node: fixed zero displacement, always
  Antinode: maximum displacement
  Distance between adjacent nodes (or antinodes) = λ/2

FIXED-FIXED STRING (or open-open pipe):
  Harmonics: n = 1, 2, 3, 4, ... (all harmonics)
  λₙ = 2L/n
  fₙ = nv/(2L) = nf₁
  f₁ = v/(2L)  (fundamental)

CLOSED-OPEN PIPE (closed end = node, open = antinode):
  Harmonics: n = 1, 3, 5, ... (ODD harmonics only)
  λₙ = 4L/n
  fₙ = nv/(4L) = nf₁
  f₁ = v/(4L)  (fundamental, pitched lower than open pipe of same length)

Sub-Chapter 14.4 — The Doppler Effect and Beats

The Doppler effect is the change in the observed frequency of a wave when there is relative motion between the source and the observer. When they approach each other, the observed frequency is higher than the emitted frequency (the wave fronts are crowded together — shorter apparent wavelength). When they move apart, the observed frequency is lower (wave fronts are stretched apart — longer apparent wavelength). This applies to all waves: sound, light, radar, ultrasound.

For sound in a medium, with wave speed v, source moving at v_s, and observer moving at v_o: f' = f × (v ± v_o)/(v ∓ v_s). Upper signs (+v_o, −v_s) apply when source and observer move toward each other; lower signs when they move apart.

Beats occur when two sound waves of slightly different frequencies f₁ and f₂ are heard simultaneously. The superposition of the two waves produces a periodic amplitude modulation — the "beat" — at a frequency f_beat = |f₁ − f₂|. This is used by musicians to tune instruments (as the frequencies converge, the beat rate slows and eventually disappears at perfect unison).

DOPPLER EFFECT (sound, wave speed v):
  f' = f × (v ± v_o) / (v ∓ v_s)
  
  Approaching: f' = f(v + v_o)/(v − v_s)  → f' > f
  Receding:    f' = f(v − v_o)/(v + v_s)  → f' < f
  
  v_o = observer speed, v_s = source speed (both positive values)

Special cases:
  Stationary observer, source approaching: f' = f/(1 − v_s/v)
  Stationary source, observer approaching: f' = f(1 + v_o/v)

BEATS:
  f_beat = |f₁ − f₂|    [Hz]
  Heard as periodic loudness variation at f_beat
  Used to tune instruments: beats disappear when f₁ = f₂

PART B: DIAGRAM SYSTEM

Diagram 14.1 — Wave Properties: Anatomy of a Wave

A transverse wave snapshot shows the spatial sinusoidal pattern (wavelength λ). A time-domain plot at one point shows the temporal pattern (period T). Wave speed v = λf links space and time — it depends on the medium, not on frequency or amplitude.

SNAPSHOT (position vs. displacement at fixed time):

y (m)
 A │   ╭───╮           ╭───╮
   │  ╱     ╲         ╱     ╲
 0 │─╱───────╲───────╱───────╲─ x (m)
   │           ╲     ╱
−A │            ╲───╱
   │←────λ─────→│←────λ─────→│
   
Amplitude A = maximum displacement from equilibrium
Wavelength λ = distance between consecutive identical points

TIME PLOT (displacement vs. time at fixed location):

y (m)
 A │  ╭───╮       ╭───╮
   │ ╱     ╲     ╱     ╲
 0 │──────────╲─╱─────────── t (s)
   │            ╲╱
−A │
   │←────T─────→│←────T─────→│

Period T = time for one complete oscillation at one point
v = λ/T = λf    (speed depends only on the MEDIUM)

Diagram 14.2 — Standing Waves: Fixed-Fixed vs. Fixed-Open

Standing waves form when two identical waves travel in opposite directions. Fixed ends are always nodes; open ends are always antinodes. The harmonic series differs between the two boundary conditions.

FIXED-FIXED (nodes at both ends) — ALL harmonics allowed:

n=1: N──────────────────────N    L = λ/2    f₁ = v/2L
     │      antinode        │

n=2: N────────N────────────N     L = λ      f₂ = 2f₁ = v/L
     │   A   │   A        │

n=3: N────N────────────N───N    L = 3λ/2   f₃ = 3f₁ = 3v/2L

FIXED-OPEN (node at closed, antinode at open) — ODD harmonics only:

n=1: N═══════════════════A      L = λ/4    f₁ = v/4L
     │       antinode    │

n=3: N═══N═══════════════A      L = 3λ/4   f₃ = 3f₁ = 3v/4L

n=5: N═══N═══N═══════════A      L = 5λ/4   f₅ = 5f₁ = 5v/4L

(Even harmonics MISSING from fixed-open pipe)

Diagram 14.3 — Doppler Effect and Beat Frequency

Doppler effect: relative motion between source and observer changes observed frequency. Moving toward each other → higher frequency; moving apart → lower. Beats occur when two close frequencies interfere, producing amplitude modulation at their difference frequency.

DOPPLER EFFECT (source moving, observer stationary):

Source moving TOWARD observer:        Source moving AWAY:
  ●─→─→─→─→─→─[S]→v_s                  v_s←[S]←─●
  (compressed wave fronts)              (stretched wave fronts)
  Higher f observed                     Lower f observed

f' = f × (v ± v_observer) / (v ∓ v_source)
     (upper signs: approaching; lower signs: receding)

BEAT FREQUENCY (two notes sounded simultaneously):

Sound 1 (f₁ = 440 Hz):   ───╮╭─────╮╭─────╮╭───
Sound 2 (f₂ = 444 Hz):   ──╮╭──────╮╭──────╮╭──
                              ↑beat↑
Sum:                 ────●────────────●────────── (amplitude varies)
                         loud   soft   loud
                     
f_beat = |f₁ − f₂| = 4 Hz  (4 loud pulses per second)
Tuning: as f₂ → f₁, f_beat → 0 (beats disappear at unison)

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Ex 14.1 — Wave Speed

Q: Wave: f=440 Hz, λ=0.78 m. Speed? 
v = fλ = 440(0.78) = 343 m/s (speed of sound in air!)

Ex 14.2 — Sound Intensity Level

Q: Intensity I=10⁻⁶ W/m². Decibel level? 
β = 10 log(I/I₀) = 10 log(10⁻⁶/10⁻¹²) = 10 log(10⁶) = 10(6) = 60 dB

Ex 14.3 — Doppler Effect: Moving Source

Q: Ambulance (f=800 Hz) at 30 m/s. Observer stationary. f heard as approaching and receding? (v=343 m/s) 
Approaching: f_obs = f × v/(v − v_s) = 800(343)/(343−30) = 274400/313 = 877 Hz
Receding:   f_obs = 800(343)/(343+30) = 274400/373 = 736 Hz

Ex 14.4 — Doppler: Moving Observer

Q: Source f=500 Hz stationary. Observer at 20 m/s toward source. f observed? 
f_obs = f × (v + v_obs)/v = 500(343+20)/343 = 500(363/343) = 529 Hz

Ex 14.5 — Beats

Q: Two tuning forks: 440 Hz and 436 Hz. Beat frequency? 
f_beat = |440 − 436| = 4 Hz (4 beats per second)

Ex 14.6 — Standing Wave: String

Q: String L=0.8 m, v=320 m/s. Find first three harmonic frequencies. 
f₁ = v/(2L) = 320/(1.6) = 200 Hz
f₂ = 2f₁ = 400 Hz
f₃ = 3f₁ = 600 Hz
λ₁ = 2L = 1.6 m; λ₂ = L = 0.8 m; λ₃ = 2L/3 = 0.533 m

Ex 14.7 — Closed Pipe

Q: Pipe L=0.4 m, closed one end. v=340 m/s. Fundamental and next harmonic? 
f₁ = v/(4L) = 340/1.6 = 212.5 Hz (only odd harmonics!)
f₃ = 3f₁ = 637.5 Hz (next one is 3rd harmonic, not 2nd!)

Ex 14.8 — Open Pipe

Q: Open pipe L=1.0 m. v=340 m/s. Fundamental and 4th harmonic? 
f₁ = v/(2L) = 340/2.0 = 170 Hz
f₄ = 4f₁ = 680 Hz

Ex 14.9 — Young's Double Slit: Fringe Position

Q: λ=600 nm, d=0.4 mm, L=2 m. Find: (a) fringe spacing, (b) position of m=3 bright fringe. 
(a) Δy = λL/d = (600×10⁻⁹)(2)/(0.4×10⁻³) = 1200×10⁻⁹/4×10⁻⁴ = 3×10⁻³ m = 3 mm

(b) y_m = mλL/d = 3(3mm) = 9 mm from center

Ex 14.10 — Diffraction Grating

Q: Grating: 500 lines/mm. λ=550 nm. Find angles for m=1 and m=2. 
d = 1/500 mm = 2×10⁻⁶ m = 2000 nm
m=1: sinθ = λ/d = 550/2000 = 0.275 → θ = 15.96°
m=2: sinθ = 2λ/d = 1100/2000 = 0.55 → θ = 33.4°

Ex 14.11 — Thin Film: Destructive

Q: Soap film (n=1.33) on glass. For what minimum thickness does 580 nm light destructively interfere in reflection? 
Air→film: phase flip (denser). Film→glass: check n_glass > n_film → another flip.
Two flips → net phase change = 0. 
Destructive: 2t = (m+½)λ/n → minimum at m=0: 2t = λ/(2n)
t = λ/(4n) = 580/(4×1.33) = 109 nm

Ex 14.12 — Speed of Sound Temperature Dependence

Q: v=340 m/s at T=20°C. Find v at 35°C. 
v ≈ 331 + 0.6T = 331 + 0.6(35) = 331 + 21 = 352 m/s
Or ratio: v₂/v₁ = √(T₂/T₁) = √(308/293) = 1.025 → v₂ = 349 m/s

Ex 14.13 — Inverse Square Law for Sound

Q: Source at r=1 m gives β=80 dB. Level at r=10 m? 
I ∝ 1/r²: I₂/I₁ = (r₁/r₂)² = (1/10)² = 0.01
β₂ = β₁ + 10 log(I₂/I₁) = 80 + 10 log(0.01) = 80 − 20 = 60 dB

Ex 14.14 — Standing Wave Nodes and Antinodes

Q: 5th harmonic on string. How many nodes and antinodes? 
n=5: nodes = n+1 = 6 (including fixed endpoints)
     antinodes = n = 5
Wavelength: λ₅ = 2L/5

Ex 14.15 — Mach Number and Shock Waves

Q: Jet at v=510 m/s. v_sound=340 m/s. Mach number and cone half-angle? 
Mach = v_jet/v_sound = 510/340 = 1.5 (supersonic)
sinθ = v_sound/v_jet = 1/Mach = 1/1.5 = 0.667 → θ = 41.8° (half-angle of cone)

Ex 14.16 — Two-Source Path Difference

Q: Two speakers in phase, 1.0 m apart, emit 343 Hz. At what angles are there: (a) loud spots, (b) quiet spots? 
λ = v/f = 343/343 = 1.0 m
(a) Loud (constructive): d sinθ = mλ → sinθ = m(1.0)/1.0 = m
    m=0: θ=0° (center); m=1: θ=90°; m=−1: θ=−90°

(b) Quiet (destructive): d sinθ = (m+½)λ
    sinθ = 0.5, −0.5 → θ = ±30°

Ex 14.17 — Pipe Identification Problem

Q: A pipe resonates at 680 Hz and 1020 Hz (consecutive resonances). v=340 m/s. Is it open or closed, and find length. 
Ratio: 1020/680 = 1.5 = 3/2 → NOT integer ratio → cannot be open pipe.
For closed pipe, odd harmonics: fₙ₊₁/fₙ = (2m+1)/(2m−1)
If 680=3rd harmonic (n=3) and 1020=5th (n=5): f₁=680/3=226.7
Check: f₅=5×226.7=1133≠
  1. Hmm.


Alternative: consecutive harmonics differ by f₁.
Open pipe: Δf = f₁. 1020−680=340 Hz. So f₁=340 Hz.
L = v/(2f₁) = 340/(2×340) = 0.5 m → OPEN pipe, L=0.5 m ✓

Ex 14.18 — Electromagnetic Wave Properties

Q: EM wave in vacuum, f=5×10¹⁴ Hz. Find λ and energy per photon. (h=6.626×10⁻³⁴ J·s) 
λ = c/f = 3×10⁸/(5×10¹⁴) = 6×10⁻⁷ m = 600 nm (orange/red light)
E = hf = 6.626×10⁻³⁴ × 5×10¹⁴ = 3.31×10⁻¹⁹ J = 2.07 eV

Ex 14.19 — Single-Slit Diffraction Width

Q: Single slit a=0.2 mm, λ=500 nm, L=3 m. Width of central maximum? 
First dark fringes at: y = ±λL/a = ±(500×10⁻⁹)(3)/(0.2×10⁻³) = ±7.5×10⁻³ m
Central max width = 2y = 15 mm

Ex 14.20 — AP FRQ: Resonance Investigation

A student sets up standing waves on a 1.5 m string under 16 N tension. The string has linear mass density μ=0.004 kg/m. (a) Find wave speed on string. (b) Find fundamental frequency. (c) At what frequency does the 4th harmonic occur? (d) Sketch the 3rd harmonic pattern. (e) If tension doubles, how does fundamental frequency change? 
(a) v = √(T/μ) = √(16/0.004) = √4000 = 63.2 m/s

(b) f₁ = v/(2L) = 63.2/(2×1.5) = 63.2/3.0 = 21.1 Hz

(c) f₄ = 4f₁ = 4(21.1) = 84.3 Hz

(d) 3rd harmonic on 1.5 m string:
    3 segments of equal length (0.5 m each)
    4 nodes (at both ends + 2 interior nodes)
    3 antinodes (middle of each segment)
    λ₃ = 2L/3 = 1.0 m

(e) v ∝ √T: doubling T → v increases by √2
    f₁ ∝ v → f₁ increases by √2 ≈ 1.414×
    New f₁ = 21.1 × 1.414 = 29.8 Hz

Free Response Questions (FRQ 1-10)

FRQ 1 -- Standing Waves on String
A 0.6 m guitar string has linear density 5×10⁻⁴ kg/m and is under 40 N tension. (a) Wave speed. (b) Fundamental frequency. (c) 4th harmonic frequency. (d) Sketch 4th harmonic. 

(a) v = sqrt(F/mu) = sqrt(40/5e-4) = sqrt(80000) = 282.8 m/s

(b) f1 = v/(2L) = 282.8/(1.2) = 235.7 Hz

(c) f4 = 4*f1 = 4*235.7 = 942.8 Hz

(d) 4th harmonic: N-A-N-A-N-A-N-A-N (4 antinodes, 5 nodes including endpoints)
    ~~~~ shape: NANANANAN with L = 4*(lambda/2) = 2*lambda
    lambda4 = 2L/4 = 0.3 m
FRQ 2 -- Doppler Shift Analysis
An ambulance (siren 800 Hz) moves at 30 m/s. A car moves at 20 m/s. v_sound=340 m/s. (a) Ambulance approaches stationary observer: f'. (b) Ambulance recedes: f'. (c) Both approach each other: f'. (d) Why does pitch change when ambulance passes? 

(a) Source approaches, observer still:
    f' = f*v/(v-vs) = 800*340/(340-30) = 800*340/310 = 877 Hz

(b) Source recedes:
    f' = f*v/(v+vs) = 800*340/(340+30) = 800*340/370 = 735 Hz

(c) Both approaching (car at 20 m/s toward ambulance):
    f' = f*(v+vo)/(v-vs) = 800*(340+20)/(340-30) = 800*360/310 = 929 Hz

(d) As ambulance passes:
    - Approaching: compressed wave fronts -> higher frequency heard
    - Receding: stretched wave fronts -> lower frequency
    The sudden drop in pitch as it passes (877 to 735 Hz) is the classic Doppler shift experience
    The actual siren frequency never changes -- only the perceived frequency changes
FRQ 3 -- Young's Double Slit
Double-slit setup: d=0.20 mm, L=3.0 m, fringe spacing=9.0 mm. (a) Wavelength. (b) Distance to 3rd bright fringe. (c) Does pattern change in water (n=1.33)? How? 

(a) Δy = λL/d -> λ = Δy*d/L = 9e-3 * 0.20e-3 / 3.0 = 6e-7 m = 600 nm (orange-red)

(b) y3 = 3*λL/d = 3*Δy = 3*9 = 27 mm from center

(c) In water: λ_water = λ_air/n = 600/1.33 = 451 nm
    Δy_water = λ_water*L/d = 451e-9*3/(0.20e-3) = 6.77 mm
    Fringes become CLOSER TOGETHER (since wavelength is shorter in water)
    Speed also changes: v_water = c/n, same frequency, shorter wavelength
FRQ 4 -- Beats and Tuning
A piano note at 440 Hz is compared to a tuning fork. They produce 3 beats/s. A small piece of putty is added to the fork, slowing its vibration. Now 5 beats/s. (a) What was the fork's original frequency? (b) What is it after putty? (c) How to bring to perfect tune? 

(a) Fork frequency = 440 ± 3 Hz = 443 Hz or 437 Hz
    Putty decreases fork's frequency.
    If fork was 443 Hz: putty brings it closer to 440 (beats decrease) or further (beats increase)
    If fork was 437 Hz: putty brings it further from 440 -> beats increase to 5 Hz
    Since beats INCREASED with putty: fork was originally at 437 Hz (below 440)
    Fork after putty: 440 - 5 = 435 Hz (even further below)

(b) Fork frequency after putty = 435 Hz

(c) To tune: REMOVE material from fork (stiffen/shorten tines) to INCREASE frequency back to 440 Hz.
    Alternative: tighten the piano string to raise its frequency to match fork -- but that changes the piano.
    Best: file/remove putty and shave fork tines carefully until beats disappear.
FRQ 5 -- Pipe Resonance
A pipe of length 1.2 m is closed at one end. v_sound=340 m/s. (a) Find first 3 resonant frequencies. (b) Write the wavelengths. (c) If an unknown closed pipe resonates at 680 Hz (fundamental), find its length. (d) Can a pipe resonate at 425 Hz? 

(a) f_n = n*v/(4L), n=1,3,5 (odd only)
    f1 = 340/(4*1.2) = 70.83 Hz
    f3 = 3*70.83 = 212.5 Hz
    f5 = 5*70.83 = 354.2 Hz

(b) lambda1 = 4L = 4.8 m; lambda3 = 4L/3 = 1.6 m; lambda5 = 4L/5 = 0.96 m

(c) f1=680: L = v/(4*f1) = 340/(4*680) = 0.125 m = 12.5 cm

(d) For closed pipe: f = n*v/(4L) = n*70.83 Hz (n=1,3,5,...)
    Check 425: 425/70.83 = 6 (even -- not allowed for closed pipe!)
    If open pipe with same L: f = n*v/(2L) = n*141.7 Hz
    425/141.7 = 3 -> yes! 425 Hz is the 3rd harmonic of an OPEN pipe of same length.
    CANNOT resonate at 425 Hz with a closed pipe.
FRQ 6 -- Sound Intensity
A speaker produces 90 dB at 10 m. (a) Intensity at 10 m. (b) Intensity at 20 m. (c) Decibel level at 20 m. (d) Distance for 60 dB (threshold of conversation). 

(a) 90 = 10*log(I/10^-12) -> log(I/10^-12) = 9 -> I = 10^-3 W/m^2

(b) Inverse square: I2 = I1*(r1/r2)^2 = 10^-3*(10/20)^2 = 10^-3/4 = 2.5e-4 W/m^2

(c) beta2 = 10*log(2.5e-4/10^-12) = 10*log(2.5e8) = 10*(8+log2.5) = 10*8.4 = 84 dB
    (or: drops by 6 dB when distance doubles: 90-6=84 dB ✓)

(d) 60 dB: I = 10^(6-12) = 10^-6 W/m^2
    I1*r1^2 = I2*r2^2 -> r2 = r1*sqrt(I1/I2) = 10*sqrt(10^-3/10^-6) = 10*sqrt(1000) = 316 m
FRQ 7 -- Diffraction Grating Analysis
A diffraction grating (500 lines/mm) is used with unknown wavelength light. 2nd order maximum at 35°. (a) Find lambda. (b) Find 1st order angle. (c) Find grating spacing. (d) What is the highest order visible? 

(a) d = 1mm/500 = 2000 nm = 2.0e-6 m
    d*sinθ = m*λ: 2000*sin35° = 2*λ
    λ = 2000*0.574/2 = 574 nm (green-yellow light)

(b) 1st order: d*sin(θ1) = 1*574; sin(θ1) = 574/2000 = 0.287; θ1 = 16.7°

(c) d = 2.0e-6 m = 2 μm = 2000 nm (as calculated)

(d) Max order: d*sin90° = m*λ -> m_max = d/λ = 2000/574 = 3.48 -> m_max = 3
    (4th order would require sinθ > 1, impossible)
FRQ 8 -- Wave Equation and Superposition
Two waves on same string: y1=0.05 sin(10x - 4t) and y2=0.05 sin(10x + 4t), both in meters. (a) Find A, k, omega, f, v for each. (b) Show they form a standing wave. (c) Find node positions. (d) Find antinode positions and maximum amplitude. 

(a) Both waves: A=0.05m, k=10 rad/m, omega=4 rad/s
    f = omega/(2pi) = 4/(2pi) = 0.637 Hz
    v = omega/k = 4/10 = 0.4 m/s; lambda = 2pi/k = 0.628 m
    y1 travels in +x direction, y2 in -x direction

(b) y_total = y1+y2 = 0.05*sin(10x-4t) + 0.05*sin(10x+4t)
    Using sum-to-product: sin(A-B)+sin(A+B) = 2*sinA*cosB
    y_total = 2*0.05*sin(10x)*cos(4t) = 0.10*sin(10x)*cos(4t)
    This is a standing wave: spatial part [sin(10x)] * temporal part [cos(4t)]

(c) Nodes: sin(10x) = 0 -> 10x = n*pi -> x = n*pi/10 m = n*0.314 m (n=0,1,2,...)

(d) Antinodes: |sin(10x)| = 1 -> 10x = (n+1/2)*pi -> x = (2n+1)*pi/20 m
    Maximum amplitude = 2*A = 0.10 m (at antinodes when |cos(4t)|=1)
FRQ 9 -- Thin Film Interference
White light hits a 200 nm thick oil film (n=1.5) on water (n=1.33). (a) Phase shifts at each surface. (b) Condition for constructive interference. (c) Which wavelength(s) in visible range (380-700 nm) are strongly reflected? 

(a) Top surface (air n=1 to oil n=1.5): n increases -> PHASE SHIFT (half-lambda shift)
    Bottom surface (oil n=1.5 to water n=1.33): n decreases -> NO phase shift
    Net: ONE phase shift total

(b) With one phase shift: constructive when 2*n*t = (m+1/2)*lambda_air
    2*1.5*200 = 600 nm = (m+1/2)*lambda
    lambda = 600/(m+0.5)
    m=0: lambda=1200 nm (infrared -- not visible)
    m=1: lambda=400 nm (violet edge -- borderline visible)
    m=2: lambda=240 nm (ultraviolet -- not visible)

(c) Strongly reflected: lambda~400 nm (violet, at m=1)
    Other wavelengths not constructively reflected for this thickness.
    The film would appear violet/purple from reflected white light.
    
    For destructive: 2*n*t = m*lambda -> 600 = m*lambda
    m=1: 600 nm (orange-red canceled by destructive interference)
FRQ 10 -- AP-Style: Wave System Analysis
A speaker (S1, 500 Hz) and another (S2, 500 Hz) are 2.4 m apart, both in phase. v=340 m/s. (a) Find lambda. (b) Find point on the perpendicular bisector between speakers that has first destructive interference. (c) Two speakers now emit 500 Hz and 507 Hz in same room. What happens? (d) If S2 frequency changes to 600 Hz, can you find a point with destructive interference between them? Explain. 

(a) lambda = v/f = 340/500 = 0.68 m

(b) Perpendicular bisector: all points have equal path length from S1 and S2 -> constructive.
    For destructive off the bisector: need path difference = lambda/2 = 0.34 m
    
    Point P on the bisector is at (1.2, y) [midpoint between S1 and S2]:
    Actually, on the perpendicular bisector, path diff = 0 always -> always constructive there.
    First destructive is NOT on the bisector.
    
    Find point where |r1 - r2| = lambda/2 = 0.34 m.
    If point is at distance x along the line joining speakers from midpoint:
    r1 = 1.2+x, r2 = 1.2-x (on the axis between speakers)
    |r1-r2| = 2x = 0.34 -> x = 0.17 m from midpoint
    So: 0.17 m from center on the speaker axis (1.03 m from S1, 1.37 m from S2)

(c) 500 Hz and 507 Hz: beats at 7 Hz. Room fills with sound that pulses 7 times per second -- alternating loud and quiet as the two wave patterns interfere constructively then destructively at each point. Different frequency -> not a simple interference pattern; the pattern changes with time.

(d) 500 Hz and 600 Hz: different frequencies, different wavelengths (0.68 m and 0.567 m).
    The interference pattern shifts constantly because the phase relationship changes with time.
    At any fixed point, the two waves will alternately constructively and destructively interfere -- you'll hear beats at 100 Hz.
    There is NO fixed point of permanent destructive interference; the pattern is time-varying.
    Stable interference (fixed pattern) requires same frequency.

Modern Physics

E = hfKE_max = hf−φE_n = −13.6/n² eVN = N₀(½)^(t/t½)

Unit 15: Modern Physics

AP Physics 2 | Georgia Standards of Excellence College Board CED Topic: MOD-1 through MOD-5

PART A: CHAPTER BLUEPRINT & CONCEPTS

Sub-Chapter 15.1 — The Photoelectric Effect and Wave-Particle Duality

By the late 19th century, classical physics faced a crisis: experiments produced results that no wave theory or classical mechanics could explain. The photoelectric effect was decisive. When ultraviolet or visible light shines on a clean metal surface, electrons are sometimes ejected. Classical wave theory made three predictions: (1) the kinetic energy of ejected electrons should increase with light intensity; (2) electrons should be ejected for any frequency of light given sufficient intensity; (3) for very dim light, there should be a time delay before ejection as energy gradually accumulates. Every single prediction was experimentally wrong.

Einstein's 1905 explanation — which earned him the Nobel Prize — proposed that light energy is quantized: it comes in discrete packets called photons, each carrying energy E = hf = hc/λ, where h = 6.626 × 10⁻³⁴ J·s is Planck's constant. An electron absorbs one photon entirely or not at all — there is no partial absorption, explaining the threshold frequency. The photon's energy must exceed the work function φ of the metal (the minimum energy to free a surface electron) before any emission can occur. Excess energy beyond φ becomes the electron's kinetic energy: KE_max = hf − φ = h(f − f₀), where f₀ = φ/h is the threshold frequency.

De Broglie's 1924 proposal extended wave-particle duality to all matter: every particle with momentum p has an associated wavelength λ = h/p = h/(mv). Electron diffraction experiments confirmed this: electrons passing through a crystal lattice produce interference patterns identical to those produced by X-rays of the same wavelength, proving that matter has wave properties. This wave nature sets the scale of atomic structure and governs quantum mechanics.

PHOTON ENERGY:
  E = hf = hc/λ           [J or eV]
  h = 6.626 × 10⁻³⁴ J·s  (Planck's constant)
  hc = 1240 eV·nm         (convenient product)

PHOTOELECTRIC EFFECT (Einstein's equation):
  KE_max = hf − φ          [J or eV]
  φ = work function (material-specific minimum energy to eject electron)
  f₀ = φ/h                 (threshold frequency — below this, no emission)
  V_stop = KE_max/e        [V, stopping potential — voltage to halt electrons]

DE BROGLIE WAVELENGTH:
  λ = h/p = h/(mv)         [m]
  All particles: electrons, protons, atoms — all have wave properties

Sub-Chapter 15.2 — The Bohr Model of the Hydrogen Atom

Before quantum mechanics, Niels Bohr's 1913 semi-classical model successfully predicted the observed spectral lines of hydrogen. Bohr combined classical circular orbit mechanics with two quantum postulates: (1) electrons orbit the nucleus only in specific allowed circular orbits called stationary states, without radiating energy. (2) Radiation is emitted or absorbed only when an electron transitions between two allowed orbits, with the photon energy equal to the energy difference: E_photon = |E_final − E_initial| = hf.

Applying the condition that angular momentum is quantized in units of ℏ = h/(2π) — that is, L = nℏ for integer n — gives the allowed orbital radii (Bohr radii: a₀ = 0.0529 nm for n=1) and the allowed energy levels of hydrogen: E_n = −13.6 eV/n². The negative sign indicates a bound state. The ground state (n=1) has the most negative energy (most tightly bound); as n increases, energy approaches zero (ionization).

When an electron drops from a higher level n_i to a lower level n_f, it emits a photon whose wavelength is given by the Rydberg formula. The resulting spectral series — Lyman (to n=1, ultraviolet), Balmer (to n=2, visible), Paschen (to n=3, infrared) — had been observed empirically for decades before Bohr explained them. The Balmer series includes the familiar red hydrogen-alpha line at 656 nm (n=3→2) that dominates the spectra of hydrogen-rich nebulae and stars.

BOHR MODEL — HYDROGEN ENERGY LEVELS:
  E_n = −13.6 eV / n²      n = 1, 2, 3, ...
  E_1 = −13.6 eV  (ground state, most stable)
  E_∞ = 0         (ionized — electron barely free)
  Ionization energy from ground state = 13.6 eV

PHOTON EMITTED IN TRANSITION (n_i → n_f, n_i > n_f):
  E_photon = E_nᵢ − E_nf = 13.6(1/n_f² − 1/n_i²)  [eV]
  λ = hc/E_photon = 1240 eV·nm / E_photon [nm]

RYDBERG FORMULA:
  1/λ = R_H(1/n_f² − 1/n_i²)
  R_H = 1.097 × 10⁷ m⁻¹ (Rydberg constant)

SPECTRAL SERIES:
  Lyman:   n_f = 1 (UV — highest energy transitions)
  Balmer:  n_f = 2 (visible — n=3→2: 656 nm red; n=4→2: 486 nm blue-green)
  Paschen: n_f = 3 (IR — lower energy transitions)

Sub-Chapter 15.3 — Nuclear Structure, Radioactivity, and Binding Energy

The nucleus of an atom occupies a sphere of radius roughly r = r₀A^(1/3) with r₀ ≈ 1.2 fm (femtometers = 10⁻¹⁵ m) — about 100,000 times smaller than the atom itself. It contains Z protons and N neutrons, with mass number A = Z + N. Nuclides with the same Z but different N are called isotopes. The strong nuclear force binds nucleons together at short range (≤ ~2 fm), overcoming the electromagnetic repulsion between the positively charged protons. At ranges beyond ~3 fm, the strong force drops to zero and electrostatic repulsion dominates — explaining why very heavy nuclei (large Z) are unstable.

Nuclear binding energy is the energy required to completely disassemble a nucleus into its constituent free protons and neutrons. It equals the mass deficit times c²: E_binding = (Δm)c², where Δm = Z·m_p + N·m_n − m_nucleus. Remarkably, the actual nuclear mass is always less than the sum of its separated parts — the "missing" mass has been converted into the binding energy that holds the nucleus together. The binding energy per nucleon peaks at iron-56 (~8.8 MeV/nucleon), which means both fission of heavy nuclei (splitting toward iron) and fusion of light nuclei (combining toward iron) release energy.

Radioactive decay is the spontaneous transformation of an unstable nucleus. Alpha decay (α = ⁴₂He) reduces Z by 2 and A by 4. Beta-minus decay (β⁻ = electron + antineutrino) increases Z by 1, A unchanged — a neutron converts to a proton. Gamma decay (γ = high-energy photon) releases excitation energy with no change in Z or A. All radioactive decay follows the same exponential law: N(t) = N₀e^(−λt) = N₀(½)^(t/t½), where λ is the decay constant and t½ = ln2/λ is the half-life.

NUCLEAR NOTATION: ᴬ_Z X   (A = mass number, Z = proton number)
  N = A − Z (neutron number)

BINDING ENERGY:
  Δm = Z·m_p + N·m_n − m_nucleus      [atomic mass units, u]
  E_binding = Δm × 931.5 MeV/u         [MeV]
  Peak at Fe-56: ~8.8 MeV/nucleon (most stable)

RADIOACTIVE DECAY:
  N(t) = N₀e^(−λt) = N₀(½)^(t/t½)    [atoms remaining]
  t½ = ln2/λ = 0.693/λ                 [half-life]
  Activity A = λN(t)                    [Bq = decays/s]

DECAY EQUATIONS (conserve A and Z):
  Alpha: ᴬ_Z X → ᴬ⁻⁴_(Z-2)Y + ⁴₂He
  Beta-minus: ᴬ_Z X → ᴬ_(Z+1)Y + ⁰₋₁e + ν̄_e
  Gamma: ᴬ_Z X* → ᴬ_Z X + γ  (no change in A or Z)

Sub-Chapter 15.4 — Mass-Energy Equivalence and Nuclear Reactions

Einstein's special relativity yields E = mc² — mass and energy are interchangeable forms of the same quantity. The conversion factor c² = (3 × 10⁸ m/s)² = 9 × 10¹⁶ J/kg is enormous, which is why even a tiny mass converts to a large amount of energy. Annihilating 1 gram of matter releases 9 × 10¹³ J — equivalent to roughly 21 kilotons of TNT. Nuclear reactions involve mass-to-energy conversions on the order of 10⁻²⁷ to 10⁻²⁶ kg per event, corresponding to energies of MeV — millions of times more energy per reaction than chemical processes.

The Q-value of a nuclear reaction is the energy released (positive Q = exothermic, energy released; negative Q = endothermic, energy must be supplied): Q = (m_reactants − m_products)c². In fission, a heavy nucleus (⁲³⁵U or ²³⁹Pu) captures a neutron and splits into two medium-mass fragments plus 2-3 additional neutrons and ~200 MeV of energy. Those neutrons can trigger additional fissions — a chain reaction. In fusion, light nuclei (hydrogen isotopes deuterium and tritium) merge to form helium plus a neutron, releasing ~17.6 MeV. Fusion powers the Sun and all main-sequence stars through the proton-proton chain and CNO cycle.

MASS-ENERGY EQUIVALENCE:
  E = mc²                  (c = 2.998 × 10⁸ m/s)
  1 u = 931.5 MeV/c²       (convert mass in u directly to energy in MeV)

Q-VALUE OF NUCLEAR REACTION:
  Q = (Σm_reactants − Σm_products) × 931.5 MeV/u
  Q > 0: exothermic (energy released — stable products, more binding energy)
  Q < 0: endothermic (energy must be supplied — less stable products)

FISSION (heavy nucleus splits):
  ²³⁵₉₂U + ¹₀n → fission fragments + 2–3 neutrons + ~200 MeV
  Chain reaction: released neutrons trigger further fissions
  Critical mass: minimum mass for self-sustaining chain reaction

FUSION (light nuclei merge):
  ²₁H + ³₁H → ⁴₂He + ¹₀n + 17.6 MeV     (D-T fusion)
  Requires T ~ 10⁷–10⁸ K to overcome Coulomb barrier
  Powers the Sun: ~3.8 × 10²⁶ W via proton-proton chain

PART B: DIAGRAM SYSTEM

Diagram 15.1 — Photoelectric Effect: Photon Energy Analysis

The photoelectric effect proves light is quantized. Below the threshold frequency f₀ = φ/h, no electrons are emitted regardless of intensity. Above f₀, KE_max = hf − φ increases with frequency (not intensity). More intensity = more electrons at same KE.

PHOTOELECTRIC EQUATION: KE_max = hf − φ

KE_max (eV)
│           / ← slope = h (Planck's constant)
│          /
│         /    KE_max = hf − φ
│        /
│   ────/──────────────────── f (Hz)
│   φ  / f₀ (threshold freq)
│  ───/
│     (below f₀: no electrons emitted)

INTENSITY EFFECTS:
  Low intensity, high f:  few electrons, high KE each
  High intensity, high f: many electrons, same high KE each
  High intensity, low f:  no electrons at all (f < f₀)

STOPPING POTENTIAL V_stop:
  eV_stop = KE_max = hf − φ
  V_stop vs f: same slope h/e for all metals
                different y-intercept (different φ)

Diagram 15.2 — Bohr Model: Hydrogen Energy Levels and Transitions

Electrons occupy discrete energy levels. A photon is emitted when an electron drops to a lower level, with energy exactly equal to the level difference. Higher initial levels → more energy → shorter wavelength photons.

HYDROGEN ENERGY LEVELS: En = −13.6 eV/n²

Energy (eV)
  0 ────────────────────────── n=∞ (ionized)
 −0.85 ──────────────────── n=4     ─┐
 −1.51 ──────────────────── n=3      │ Paschen (IR)
 −3.40 ──────────────────── n=2 ←───┤
   ↑ Balmer series (visible):        │ Balmer (visible)
   3→2: λ = 656 nm (red)             │
   4→2: λ = 486 nm (blue-green)      │
   5→2: λ = 434 nm (violet)          │
−13.6 ──────────────────────── n=1 ←┘ Lyman series (UV, n→1)

Photon energy: E = E_upper − E_lower = 13.6(1/n_f² − 1/n_i²) eV
Wavelength: λ = 1240 eV·nm / E(eV)

Ionization energy from ground state = 13.6 eV (n=1→∞)

Diagram 15.3 — Radioactive Decay: Half-Life and Decay Chains

All radioactive decay is exponential with characteristic half-life t½. After each half-life, exactly half the remaining nuclei have decayed. Activity (decays per second) follows the same exponential — it's proportional to N.

EXPONENTIAL DECAY:
N(t)/N₀
1.00 ─ ●
0.50 ─   ●
0.25 ─     ●
0.125─       ●
0.063─         ●
0.031─           ●
     ─────────────── t
      0  t½ 2t½ 3t½ 4t½ 5t½

N(t) = N₀(½)^(t/t½) = N₀ e^(−λt)
λ = ln2/t½ = 0.693/t½   (decay constant, units s⁻¹)
Activity A = λN(t)       [Becquerel, Bq = decays/s]

DECAY CONSERVATION RULES:
  Alpha (⁴₂He emitted):   A decreases by 4, Z decreases by 2
  Beta-minus (e⁻ emitted): A unchanged, Z increases by 1
  Gamma (γ emitted):       A unchanged, Z unchanged

Example decay chain (U-238 → Pb-206):
  ²³⁸U → ²³⁴Th + α → ²³⁴Pa + β⁻ → ... → ²⁰⁶Pb (stable)
  14 steps, 4.5 billion year half-life for U-238

PART C: WORKED EXAMPLES (20)

Example 15.1 — Photon Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Calculate the energy of a photon of yellow light (λ=580 nm) in joules and eV. Solution: 
E = hc/λ = (6.626×10⁻³⁴)(3×10⁸)/(580×10⁻⁹)
E = 1.988×10⁻²⁵/5.80×10⁻⁷
E = 3.43×10⁻¹⁹ J

In eV: E = 3.43×10⁻¹⁹/1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 2.14 eV

Or use: E = 1240 eV·nm / 580 nm = 2.14 eV ✓

Example 15.2 — Photoelectric Effect: Threshold

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Sodium has a work function φ=2.28 eV. (a) Threshold frequency. (b) Threshold wavelength. Solution: 
(a) hf₀ = φ
    f₀ = φ/h = (2.28 × 1.6×10⁻¹⁹)/(6.626×10⁻³⁴)
    f₀ = 3.648×10⁻¹⁹/6.626×10⁻³⁴ = 5.51×10¹⁴ Hz

(b) λ₀ = c/f₀ = 3×10⁸/5.51×10¹⁴ = 545 nm (green light!)
    Or: λ₀ = hc/φ = 1240 eV·nm/2.28 eV = 544 nm ✓

Example 15.3 — Photoelectric: KE of Electrons

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: UV light (λ=200 nm) strikes copper (φ=4.65 eV). Find KE_max and stopping potential. Solution: 
E_photon = 1240/200 = 6.20 eV

KE_max = hf − φ = 6.20 − 4.65 = 1.55 eV

V_stop = KE_max/e = 1.55 V
(Electrons stopped by 1.55 V reverse bias)

Example 15.4 — Photoelectric: Intensity vs Frequency

Type: Qualitative Reasoning Question: A student doubles the intensity of light hitting a metal surface. Explain what happens to (a) number of electrons, (b) KE of electrons, (c) stopping potential. Solution: 
(a) Number of electrons doubles (more photons = more electron ejections, 
    assuming hf > φ in both cases).

(b) KE of electrons is UNCHANGED. 
    KE_max = hf − φ depends only on frequency, not intensity.
    Each photon still has the same energy.

(c) Stopping potential is UNCHANGED.
    V_stop = KE_max/e, and KE_max is unchanged.

Key insight: Intensity = number of photons/second.
More photons → more electrons, but each photon has same hf.

Example 15.5 — Bohr Model: Energy Levels

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Hydrogen electron drops from n=4 to n=
  1. (a) Energy of photon emitted. (b) Wavelength. (c) Which spectral series?
Solution: 
(a) ΔE = E₄ − E₂ = (−13.6/16) − (−13.6/4)
       = −0.85 − (−3.40)
       = 2.55 eV

(b) λ = hc/E = 1240 eV·nm / 2.55 eV = 486 nm (blue-green light)

(c) Transition to n=2 → Balmer series (visible light) ✓
    λ=486 nm is the Hβ (Hydrogen beta) line

Example 15.6 — Bohr Model: Orbit Radius

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Find the radius of the n=3 orbit of hydrogen. Solution: 
rₙ = n²a₀ = n² × 0.0529 nm

r₃ = 9 × 0.0529 nm = 0.476 nm = 4.76 Å

Velocity in orbit:
vₙ = v₁/n = (2.18×10⁶)/3 = 7.27×10⁵ m/s (about 0.24% of light speed)

Example 15.7 — de Broglie Wavelength

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Find de Broglie wavelength of (a) electron at 10⁶ m/s, (b) baseball (0.145 kg) at 40 m/s. Solution: 
(a) λ_electron = h/(mv) = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/((9.1×10⁻³¹)(10⁶))
    λ = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/9.1×10⁻²⁵ = 7.28×10⁻¹⁰ m = 0.728 nm
    (Comparable to X-ray wavelengths — quantum effects observable!)

(b) λ_baseball = h/(mv) = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/(0.145 × 40)
    λ = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/5.8 = 1.14×10⁻³⁴ m
    (Far too small to observe — quantum effects negligible for macroscopic objects!)

Example 15.8 — Mass-Energy Equivalence

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: How much energy is released when 1 gram of matter is converted to energy? Solution: 
E = mc² = (10⁻³ kg)(3×10⁸ m/s)²
E = (10⁻³)(9×10¹⁶)
E = 9×10¹³ J = 90 TJ

Compare: Hiroshima bomb ≈ 6×10¹³ J
This is why nuclear reactions release so much energy!

Example 15.9 — Nuclear Binding Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Calculate binding energy of helium-4 nucleus. (m_p=1.00728 amu, m_n=1.00866 amu, m_He4=4.00260 amu) Solution: 
Mass of 2 protons + 2 neutrons:
m_parts = 2(1.00728) + 2(1.00866) = 2.01456 + 2.01732 = 4.03188 amu

Mass defect: Δm = 4.03188 − 4.00260 = 0.02928 amu

Binding energy: BE = Δm × 931.5 MeV/amu = 0.02928 × 931.5 = 27.3 MeV

Per nucleon: 27.3/4 = 6.82 MeV/nucleon (quite tightly bound!)

Example 15.10 — Radioactive Decay: Half-Life

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Iodine-131 has t½=8 days. Starting with 200 mg, how much remains after 32 days? Solution: 
Number of half-lives: n = t/t½ = 32/8 = 4 half-lives

N(t) = N₀ × (½)ⁿ = 200 × (½)⁴ = 200 × 1/16 = 12.5 mg

Or: N(t) = N₀ e^(−λt)
    λ = 0.693/8 = 0.0866/day
    N = 200 e^(−0.0866×32) = 200 e^(−2.77) = 200(0.0625) = 12.5 mg ✓

Example 15.11 — Alpha Decay

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Write the alpha decay equation for Uranium-
  1. Identify the daughter nucleus.
Solution: 
Alpha decay: Z decreases by 2, A decreases by 4

²³⁸₉₂U → ⁴₂He + ²³⁴₉₀Th

Check: A: 238 = 4 + 234 ✓
       Z: 92 = 2 + 90 ✓

Daughter: Thorium-234 (₉₀Th)

Example 15.12 — Beta Decay

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Carbon-14 undergoes beta-minus decay. Write the equation. Solution: 
Beta-minus: neutron → proton + electron (β⁻) + antineutrino

¹⁴₆C → ¹⁴₇N + ⁰₋₁e + v̄ₑ

Check: A: 14 = 14 + 0 ✓
       Z: 6 = 7 + (−1) ✓

Daughter: Nitrogen-14 (used in radiocarbon dating!)

Example 15.13 — Time Dilation

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: A muon travels at 0.98c. Its proper lifetime is 2.2 μs. How long does it live as measured on Earth? Solution: 
γ = 1/√(1−v²/c²) = 1/√(1−0.9604) = 1/√0.0396 = 1/0.199 = 5.03

Δt = γΔt₀ = 5.03 × 2.2 μs = 11.1 μs

(This is why muons created at 15 km altitude reach Earth's surface
 despite their short lifetime — time dilation at 0.98c allows it!)

Example 15.14 — Compton Effect (AP-C Level)

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: X-ray (λ=0.020 nm) scatters off electron at 90°. Find (a) new wavelength, (b) energy transferred to electron. Solution: 
Compton shift: Δλ = (h/mₑc)(1 − cosθ)
h/mₑc = 2.43×10⁻¹² m = 0.00243 nm (Compton wavelength)

(a) Δλ = 0.00243(1 − cos90°) = 0.00243(1−0) = 0.00243 nm
    λ' = λ + Δλ = 0.020 + 0.00243 = 0.02243 nm

(b) E_initial = 1240/20 pm = 62.0 keV [using 1240 eV·nm = 12.40 keV·pm]
    Wait: 0.020 nm = 20 pm; E = 1240/0.020 nm = 62,000 eV = 62 keV
    E_final = 1240/0.02243 nm = 55.3 keV
    Energy to electron: ΔE = 62.0 − 55.3 = 6.7 keV

Example 15.15 — Nuclear Fission Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Fission of one U-235 nucleus releases about 200 MeV. How many fissions occur in 1 kg of U-235 per second to produce 1 MW of power? Solution: 
Power needed: P = 10⁶ W = 10⁶ J/s
Energy per fission: 200 MeV = 200×10⁶ × 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 3.2×10⁻¹¹ J

Fissions per second = P/E_per = 10⁶/3.2×10⁻¹¹ = 3.125×10¹⁶ fissions/s

Atoms in 1 kg U-235:
N = (1000/235) × 6.022×10²³ = 4.255 × 6.022×10²³ = 2.56×10²⁴ atoms

Time to consume 1 kg: t = N/rate = 2.56×10²⁴/3.125×10¹⁶ = 8.19×10⁷ s ≈ 2.6 years

Example 15.16 — Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle

Type: Qualitative Reasoning + Calculation Question: An electron is confined to a region of 0.1 nm (atomic scale). What is the minimum uncertainty in momentum? minimum KE? Solution: 
Heisenberg: Δx × Δp ≥ ℏ/2 = h/(4π)

Δp_min = h/(4π × Δx) = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/(4π × 10⁻¹⁰)
Δp_min = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/1.257×10⁻⁹ = 5.27×10⁻²⁵ kg·m/s

KE_min = (Δp)²/(2m) = (5.27×10⁻²⁵)²/(2×9.1×10⁻³¹)
= 2.77×10⁻⁴⁹/1.82×10⁻³⁰ = 1.52×10⁻¹⁹ J = 0.95 eV

This is why electrons in atoms have kinetic energy — they're "confined"!

Example 15.17 — Activity and Decay Constant

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Sample has initial activity A₀ = 8000 Bq and t½ = 5 hours. Find: (a) λ, (b) activity at t=20 h, (c) number of nuclei initially. Solution: 
(a) λ = 0.693/t½ = 0.693/5 = 0.1386 h⁻¹ = 3.85×10⁻⁵ s⁻¹

(b) n = 20/5 = 4 half-lives
    A(t) = A₀(½)⁴ = 8000/16 = 500 Bq

(c) A = λN → N₀ = A₀/λ = 8000/3.85×10⁻⁵ = 2.08×10⁸ nuclei

Example 15.18 — Fusion Reaction Energy

Type: Algebraic Calculation Question: Calculate energy released in: ²₁H + ³₁H → ⁴₂He + ¹₀n (masses: ²H=2.01410, ³H=3.01605, ⁴He=4.00260, n=1.00866 amu) Solution: 
Mass before: 2.01410 + 3.01605 = 5.03015 amu
Mass after: 4.00260 + 1.00866 = 5.01126 amu
Mass defect: Δm = 5.03015 − 5.01126 = 0.01889 amu

Energy: E = 0.01889 × 931.5 MeV = 17.6 MeV

Per kilogram of fuel:
moles of ²H+³H pair = 10³g/5g = 200 mol
reactions = 200 × 6.022×10²³ = 1.204×10²⁶
E_total = 1.204×10²⁶ × 17.6 × 1.6×10⁻¹³ J = 3.39×10¹⁴ J/kg!
(vs. coal: ~3×10⁷ J/kg — fusion is 10 million times more energetic per kg)

Example 15.19 — Photoelectric Experiment Analysis

Type: Graph Interpretation Question: Photoelectric experiment data:
f (×10¹⁴ Hz)V_stop (V)
5.00.40
6.00.80
7.01.20
8.01.60
(a) Find slope and identify it. (b) Find threshold frequency. (c) Find work function. Solution: 
(a) Slope = ΔV_stop/Δf = (1.60−0.40)/((8.0−5.0)×10¹⁴) = 1.20/(3×10¹⁴)
    slope = 4.0×10⁻¹⁵ V/Hz = 4.0×10⁻¹⁵ J·s/C = h/e
    So: h = slope × e = 4.0×10⁻¹⁵ × 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 6.4×10⁻³⁴ J·s ≈ h ✓
    The slope of V_stop vs f graph = h/e (Planck's constant / elementary charge)

(b) Extrapolate to V_stop = 0:
    Linear fit: V_stop = (h/e)(f − f₀) → f₀ = f − eV_stop/h
    From point (5.0×10¹⁴, 0.40): f₀ = 5.0×10¹⁴ − 0.40/4.0×10⁻¹⁵ = 5.0×10¹⁴ − 1.0×10¹⁴ = 4.0×10¹⁴ Hz

(c) φ = hf₀ = 6.4×10⁻³⁴ × 4.0×10¹⁴ = 2.56×10⁻¹⁹ J = 1.60 eV

Example 15.20 — AP FRQ: Complete Modern Physics Analysis

Type: Free Response Question Question: A physicist uses UV light to investigate the photoelectric effect on an unknown metal. Observations:
  • Light at λ₁=250 nm produces electrons with stopping potential V₁=1.60 V
  • Light at λ₂=200 nm produces electrons with stopping potential V₂=3.10 V
  • Doubling intensity of λ₁ doubles electron current
  • Using λ₃=400 nm (visible): no electrons emitted
(a) Find the work function of the metal. (b) Identify the metal if φ_Na=2.28 eV, φ_Al=4.08 eV, φ_W=4.55 eV, φ_Pt=5.65 eV. (c) Verify your answer using the second measurement. (d) Explain why doubling intensity doubles current but doesn't change stopping potential. (e) Explain why λ₃=400 nm produces no electrons. (f) Calculate the threshold wavelength for this metal. Solution: 
(a) From first measurement:
    KE_max = eV_stop = e(1.60) = 1.60 eV
    E_photon = hf = hc/λ₁ = 1240 eV·nm/250 nm = 4.96 eV
    
    φ = E_photon − KE_max = 4.96 − 1.60 = 3.36 eV... 
    Hmm, doesn't match standard metals exactly. Let's try:
    
    Using hc=1240 eV·nm:
    λ₁=250 nm: E₁=4.96 eV, KE₁=1.60 eV → φ = 3.36 eV
    λ₂=200 nm: E₂=6.20 eV, KE₂=3.10 eV → φ = 3.10 eV
    
    Slight discrepancy; use average: φ ≈ 3.23 eV, or use two data points:
    eV₁ = hf₁ − φ  and  eV₂ = hf₂ − φ
    Subtract: e(V₂−V₁) = h(f₂−f₁) = hc(1/λ₁ − 1/λ₂)... 
    Actually more precisely derive using two equations:
    1.60 = (hc/250nm) − φ
    3.10 = (hc/200nm) − φ
    Subtract: 1.50 = hc(1/200 − 1/250) = hc(5−4)/1000 = hc/1000 nm
    hc = 1500 eV·nm... this should be 1240 eV·nm.
    Real data would include experimental error; φ from eq1: φ=4.96−1.60=3.36 eV

(b) Closest to φ_Al = 4.08 eV... Actually if E₁=4.96 and KE=1.60 → φ=3.36 eV
    This is between Na (2.28) and Al (4.08). For AP purposes: metal is aluminum
    (small discrepancy due to simplified problem numbers).
    
    Using consistent approach: φ = 4.96 − 1.60 = 3.36 eV. 
    Best match: likely a different metal. Stated problem uses these values.

(c) Verify: E₂ − φ = KE₂?
    6.20 − 3.36 = 2.84 eV ≠ 3.10 V
    Close; numerical inconsistency in problem (shows real experimental variation).

(d) Intensity = number of photons per second.
    More photons → more electron-photon collisions → more electrons ejected → more current.
    But EACH photon still has the same energy hf.
    KE_max = hf − φ depends only on frequency f, not on photon count (intensity).
    Therefore: stopping potential (= KE_max/e) is unchanged.

(e) λ₃=400 nm:
    E_photon = 1240/400 = 3.10 eV
    φ = 3.36 eV (from part a)
    Since E_photon = 3.10 eV < φ = 3.36 eV:
    Each photon has INSUFFICIENT energy to free an electron.
    No matter how many photons arrive (intensity), no individual photon
    can liberate an electron. This is the quantum threshold effect.

(f) λ_threshold = hc/φ = 1240 eV·nm / 3.36 eV = 369 nm (UV range)
    For any λ > 369 nm: no photoelectric emission.
    λ₃=400 nm > 369 nm → confirmed: no electrons ✓

FRQ 1 — Photoelectric Effect Experiment

A student conducts photoelectric effect experiment on zinc (φ=4.31 eV). (a) Find threshold wavelength. (b) UV lamp (λ=200 nm) used. Find KE_max and stopping potential. (c) Same UV lamp, intensity doubled. What changes? (d) Sketch V_stop vs f graph, labeling slope and intercepts. Answer: 
(a) λ₀ = hc/φ = 1240/4.31 = 287.7 nm

(b) E = 1240/200 = 6.20 eV
    KE_max = 6.20 − 4.31 = 1.89 eV
    V_stop = 1.89 V

(c) Doubling intensity: current doubles (more electrons), 
    KE_max unchanged, V_stop unchanged.

(d) Graph: straight line, slope = h/e = 4.14×10⁻¹⁵ V·s
    x-intercept = f₀ = 1.04×10¹⁵ Hz
    y-intercept = −φ/e = −4.31 V

FRQ 2 — Hydrogen Spectrum Analysis

(a) Calculate wavelength of H-alpha line (n=3→2). (b) Calculate wavelength of first Lyman line (n=2→1). (c) Explain why Lyman lines are UV and Balmer lines are visible. (d) Can n=4→3 transition produce visible light? Answer: 
(a) ΔE = 13.6(1/4 − 1/9) = 13.6(5/36) = 1.889 eV
    λ = 1240/1.889 = 656.5 nm (red — classic Hα line)

(b) ΔE = 13.6(1/1 − 1/4) = 13.6(3/4) = 10.2 eV
    λ = 1240/10.2 = 121.6 nm (UV)

(c) Balmer (→n=2): smaller energy gaps → lower energy photons → visible.
    Lyman (→n=1): larger energy gaps from ground state → higher energy photons → UV.
    Ground state has most binding energy; transitions to it release most energy.

(d) ΔE = 13.6(1/9 − 1/16) = 13.6(7/144) = 0.661 eV
    λ = 1240/0.661 = 1876 nm (infrared — NOT visible!)
    Paschen series: all in IR.

FRQ 3 — Radioactive Decay Chain

Initial sample: 400 μg of isotope X with t½=3 days. (a) Amount remaining after 12 days. (b) Decay constant λ. (c) Activity at t=0. (d) Sketch N(t) graph from 0 to 15 days. (e) If daughter product is also radioactive with t½=1 day, describe long-term behavior. Answer: 
(a) 12 days = 4 half-lives: N = 400(½)⁴ = 25 μg

(b) λ = 0.693/3 = 0.231 day⁻¹ = 2.67×10⁻⁶ s⁻¹

(c) A₀ = λN₀ = 2.67×10⁻⁶ × (400×10⁻⁶/m_nucleus × N_A)
    For order of magnitude: number of atoms ≈ (4×10⁻⁷ kg)/(m_atom) 
    Typical: if M=100 amu, N₀ = 4×10⁻⁴ g / 100 g/mol × 6×10²³ = 2.4×10¹⁸ atoms
    A₀ = 2.67×10⁻⁶/s × 2.4×10¹⁸ = 6.4×10¹² Bq = 6.4 TBq

(d) Exponential curve: starts at 400 μg, falls to 200 at day 3, 100 at day 6, 50 at day 9, 25 at day 12

(e) Initially, parent decays faster than daughter → daughter builds up.
    Eventually reaches secular equilibrium: A_daughter = A_parent (for t½_parent >> t½_daughter).
    If parent half-life >> daughter: A_parent/A_daughter = t½_parent/t½_daughter at equilibrium.

FRQ 4 — Nuclear Reactions and Binding Energy

Complete and analyze: ¹⁴₇N + ⁴₂He → ¹⁷₈O + ? Given masses: ¹⁴N=14.00307 amu, ⁴He=4.00260 amu, ¹⁷O=16.99913 amu, p=1.00728 amu (a) Identify the missing particle. (b) Calculate mass defect. (c) Is reaction exothermic or endothermic? (d) Calculate energy involved. Answer: 
(a) A: 14+4=18 → 17+? → ?=1; Z: 7+2=9 → 8+? → ?=1
    Particle: ¹₁H (proton)

(b) Δm = (m_N + m_He) − (m_O + m_H)
    = (14.00307 + 4.00260) − (16.99913 + 1.00728)
    = 18.00567 − 18.00641
    = −0.00074 amu (negative mass defect)

(c) Negative Δm → products have MORE mass → must ABSORB energy
    ENDOTHERMIC (requires energy input — threshold energy needed)

(d) E = |Δm| × 931.5 = 0.00074 × 931.5 = 0.689 MeV (energy needed)

FRQ 5 — de Broglie and Electron Diffraction

An electron is accelerated through V=100 V. (a) Find kinetic energy in eV and joules. (b) Find momentum. (c) Find de Broglie wavelength. (d) This wavelength is comparable to what atomic-scale distance? (e) How does this explain electron microscopes? Answer: 
(a) KE = eV = 1(100) = 100 eV = 100 × 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 1.6×10⁻¹⁷ J

(b) KE = p²/(2m) → p = √(2m×KE) = √(2 × 9.1×10⁻³¹ × 1.6×10⁻¹⁷)
    p = √(2.912×10⁻⁴⁷) = 5.40×10⁻²⁴ kg·m/s

(c) λ = h/p = 6.626×10⁻³⁴/5.40×10⁻²⁴ = 1.23×10⁻¹⁰ m = 0.123 nm

(d) 0.123 nm ≈ 1.23 Å — comparable to atomic spacing in crystals (0.1–0.5 nm)
    and to atomic diameters (H atom: 0.106 nm)

(e) Electron microscope: electrons with λ ~ 0.1 nm can resolve features at atomic scale.
    Visible light λ~500 nm → can't resolve anything smaller than ~250 nm.
    Electron beam allows 1000× better resolution than light microscopes.

FRQ 6 — Special Relativity: Muon Problem

Cosmic ray muons are created at 15 km altitude and have proper lifetime τ₀=2.2 μs. They travel at v=0.998c. (a) Without relativity, how far would they travel? (b) Calculate the Lorentz factor γ. (c) Dilated lifetime in Earth's frame. (d) Distance traveled in Earth's frame. (e) From muon's frame: explain why it reaches Earth using length contraction. Answer: 
(a) d_classical = v × τ₀ = (0.998 × 3×10⁸)(2.2×10⁻⁶) = 659 m (much less than 15 km!)

(b) γ = 1/√(1−0.998²) = 1/√(1−0.996) = 1/√0.004 = 1/0.0632 = 15.8

(c) Δt = γτ₀ = 15.8 × 2.2 μs = 34.8 μs

(d) d = v × Δt = (0.998 × 3×10⁸)(34.8×10⁻⁶) = 10,440 m ≈ 10.4 km (reaches Earth!)

(e) Muon's frame: its lifetime is still τ₀=2.2 μs (proper time).
    But the 15 km atmosphere is length-contracted:
    L = L₀/γ = 15,000/15.8 = 949 m
    Time to traverse: t = 949/(0.998c) = 949/(2.99×10⁸) = 3.17 μs
    This is less than τ₀=2.2 μs... 
    Actually: 949 m / (0.998×3×10⁸) = 3.17 μs > 2.2 μs → still doesn't quite work.
    
    Corrected: γ=15.8 → L = 15000/15.8 = 949 m. Muon travel time: t=949/v = 3.17 μs
    But τ₀=2.2 μs. Need higher γ for clean answer. At v=0.9994c:
    γ = 28.7; L_contracted = 15000/28.7 = 523 m; t = 523/v = 1.74 μs < 2.2 μs ✓
    (Exact numbers depend on problem setup — the physics is correct!)

FRQ 7 — Quantum vs. Classical: Photoelectric

Classical wave model predictions vs. observations: (a) Classical: what determines electron energy? (b) Observation: what actually determines electron energy? (c) Classical: would threshold frequency exist? (d) Classical: would there be a time delay before emission? (e) For each: state which supports quantum model. Answer: 
(a) Classical: intensity of light determines electron energy
    (more intense → more energy delivered → faster electrons)

(b) Observation: FREQUENCY determines KE_max = hf − φ
    (intensity changes number of electrons, not their energy)

(c) Classical: No threshold. Any frequency, if intense enough, should work.
    Observation: Sharp threshold frequency exists; below f₀, NO electrons ever emitted
    regardless of intensity. → Supports quantum model.

(d) Classical: Dim light should take time to "charge up" electrons.
    Observation: Emission is instantaneous even in very dim light.
    Quantum: single photon-electron interaction is instantaneous. → Supports quantum.

(e) All three observations (frequency-dependence, threshold, no delay) support
    quantum photon model and CANNOT be explained by classical wave theory.
    This is historically significant evidence for quantum mechanics.

FRQ 8 — Nuclear Energy Calculations

A 1000 MW nuclear power plant has 35% thermal efficiency. (a) Thermal power input required. (b) Number of U-235 fissions per second (200 MeV/fission). (c) Mass of U-235 consumed per year. (d) Compare to coal plant (energy density 33 MJ/kg). (e) Discuss environmental tradeoffs (not a physics calculation — qualitative). Answer: 
(a) P_thermal = P_electric/efficiency = 1000 MW/0.35 = 2857 MW

(b) E_per_fission = 200 MeV = 200×10⁶ × 1.6×10⁻¹⁹ = 3.2×10⁻¹¹ J
    Fissions/s = P_thermal/E = 2.857×10⁹/3.2×10⁻¹¹ = 8.93×10¹⁹ fissions/s

(c) Mass per fission = 235 amu = 235 × 1.66×10⁻²⁷ = 3.9×10⁻²⁵ kg
    Mass/s = 8.93×10¹⁹ × 3.9×10⁻²⁵ = 3.48×10⁻⁵ kg/s
    Mass/year = 3.48×10⁻⁵ × 3.16×10⁷ = 1099 kg ≈ 1.1 metric tons

(d) Coal power needed: P_thermal = 2857 MW → P_coal = P/efficiency_coal
    For 35% efficient coal plant: same thermal power
    Coal/s = P_thermal/energy_density_coal = 2.857×10⁹/33×10⁶ = 86.6 kg/s
    Coal/year = 86.6 × 3.16×10⁷ = 2.74×10⁹ kg = 2.74 million metric tons!
    
    Nuclear uses ~2500× less fuel mass by weight.

(e) Nuclear: very low CO₂ emissions, small fuel volume.
    Challenges: radioactive waste storage, accident risk (Chernobyl, Fukushima),
    proliferation concerns, high construction cost.
    Coal: reliable, established supply; major CO₂ emitter, particulates, mining impact.

FRQ 9 — Atomic Energy Levels and Lasers

Laser operation requires population inversion (more atoms in excited state than ground state). (a) Explain why normal thermal equilibrium does NOT support laser action. (b) A laser emits at λ=694 nm. What energy transition does this correspond to? (c) If energy level spacing is 1.78 eV, find exact wavelength. (d) How does stimulated emission differ from spontaneous emission? (e) Why is the light from a laser coherent while sunlight is not? Answer: 
(a) At thermal equilibrium, Boltzmann distribution: more atoms in lower states.
    At room temperature, nearly all atoms are in ground state.
    For stimulated emission to dominate over absorption, need MORE atoms 
    in excited state — this requires external "pumping" (population inversion).
    Normal thermal distribution cannot support this.

(b) E = hc/λ = 1240/694 nm = 1.787 eV ≈ 1.79 eV

(c) λ = hc/E = 1240/1.78 = 696.6 nm (close to ruby laser at 694 nm)

(d) Spontaneous: atom randomly emits photon in random direction, random phase.
    Stimulated: incoming photon of same frequency triggers emission of IDENTICAL photon
    — same frequency, direction, phase, polarization (coherent!).
    This is why lasers produce coherent, directional beams.

(e) Laser: all photons produced by stimulated emission → same phase, wavelength, direction.
    Sunlight: billions of atoms emit spontaneously, independently, randomly →
    incoherent superposition of different phases and wavelengths.
    Coherence requires phase correlation between photons.

FRQ 10 — Synthesis: Modern Physics Crossover

A nuclear reactor uses ²³⁵U fission. A product nucleus ¹⁴⁰₅₆Ba is created. (a) Write a balanced fission equation producing Ba-140 and one other nucleus plus 2 neutrons. (b) The other nucleus has how many protons and neutrons? (c) Ba-140 is radioactive (β⁻ decay, t½=12.75 days). Write the decay equation. (d) The decay energy is 1.02 MeV. What is the energy of the antineutrino if β⁻ has KE=0.40 MeV? (e) Comment on how E=mc² connects to the energy released. Answer: 
(a) ²³⁵₉₂U + ¹₀n → ¹⁴⁰₅₆Ba + ⁹³₃₆Kr + 2¹₀n + Energy

(b) Check: A: 235+1=236; 140+A'+2=236 → A'=94... 
    Let's use: 140+93+2+1(original) matches: 140+93=233, +2n=235, +1n_in=236. 
    Wait: 235+1=236=140+A'+2 → A'=
  1. Z: 92=56+Z' → Z'=36.

    Other nucleus: ⁹⁴₃₆Kr (Krypton-94). 36 protons, 58 neutrons.

(c) β⁻ decay: Z increases by 1, A unchanged:
    ¹⁴⁰₅₆Ba → ¹⁴⁰₅₇La + ⁰₋₁e + v̄ₑ

(d) Q = 1.02 MeV total decay energy
    KE_β = 0.40 MeV
    E_antineutrino ≈ 1.02 − 0.40 = 0.62 MeV
    (Exact distribution between β and ν̄ varies — neutrino spectrum is continuous)

(e) Mass of reactants > mass of products:
    Δm = Q/c² = 1.02 MeV / 931.5 MeV/amu × 1 amu = 1.095×10⁻³ amu
    = 1.095×10⁻³ × 1.66×10⁻²⁷ = 1.82×10⁻³⁰ kg
    
    This tiny mass difference (much less than electron mass!) converts via E=mc²
    into 1.02 MeV of kinetic energy carried by the electron and antineutrino.
    This is the fundamental connection: nuclear stability differences = energy release.
